vi/250 [SOURCE: Page_8]

Abstract [SOURCE: Page_9]

Organic matter (OM) is an important component of soils. In particular, in the rhizosphere (the soil volume influenced by root activity) specific OM compounds in the form of polymers such as ExoPolySaccharides (EPS) are present. These compounds, exuded by roots and micro-organisms, are amphiphile. They can influence the water transfer in the root zone and act as a buffer in root water uptake. Understanding this regulatory role of EPS and, more generally, how organic matter alters water transfer in the soil, are open questions in which modelling can give some insights. To better account for the variable hydrophobic/hydrophilic nature of organic matter, we propose a paradigm shift in the representation of soil wettability. Instead of associating wettability with just a contact angle, we propose a conceptual energetic framework, in which an energy functional represents the effective interactions between the soil matrix and the free water surface. This energy of interaction contains both the attractive (hydrophilic) and repulsive (hydrophobic) interactions, in order to reproduce the amphiphilic nature of the molecules. By including all the energies at work in the soil, i.e. energy associated with wettability , gravity and surface tension of the water, we can define the free energy of the system. We derived the governing equation of the water saturation leading to a Darcy-Richards type equation coupled with a free energy equation. This system of equations ensures consistency with thermodynamics. The PDE system is solved numerically using finite element methods implemented in C++ with the Oomph-lib library based on adaptive spatial and temporal scheme. The code is applied to 1D, 2D and 3D axis-symmetrics including different boundary conditions. The model is first tested for its behaviour regarding hydrophobicity effects in soil. In this respect, it is able to reproduce the finite horizontal spreading of water over a hydrophobic porous medium. Applied to a vertically stratified porous medium with alternating hydrophilic and hydrophobic layers, the numerical simulation is able to reproduce, depending on the mean water content, the phenomena of water trapping in hydrophilic layers, the ’common’ diffusive transfer (as the medium approaches saturation), intermittent flow and fingering instability. In addition, the numerical bifurcation analysis using the PDE2PATH library display hysteresis phenomena. This is the first time that a PDE system captures all of these phenomena in a unique soil model. In the second part, we adapt and test from experiments our model to the specific characteristics of rhizospheric soil. We built a model medium consisting of sand with added EPS derived from chia mucilage (analogous of maize mucilage). Experiments were carried out to characterize the model parameters. In particular, we studied experimentally and numerically the infiltration of a drop placed on the sand/EPS artificial porous medium. Regarding the model, we show that the laws governing the water flow infiltrating the matrix are modified in the presence of organic matter. Once calibrated, [SOURCE: Page_9]

vii [SOURCE: Page_9]

the model can reproduce various phenomena specific to the presence of amphiphilic matter observed during our experiments or in the literature. In particular, the “buffering” effect during wetting and the different stages of drop imbibition. Finally, the model is applied to root water uptake, at the scale of a root segment with its rhizosphere, exhibiting a concentration gradient of EPS with distance from root. This application enables us to study the impact of the amphiphilic characteristics of the EPS in the rhizosphere during the water extraction phase by the root or during the night rewetting phase of the rhizosphere, with or without external water supply. Integrated into a broader framework, at the scale of a root system, this modelling will contribute to the analysis of rhizospheric traits likely to improve water use by plants in a context of climate change and practices. [SOURCE: Page_10]

Keywords of the thesis: Porous media - Wettability - Exopolysaccharides - Free energy - Numerical modelling [SOURCE: Page_10]

viii/250 [SOURCE: Page_10]

Résumé [SOURCE: Page_11]

La matière organique (MO) est un composant important des sols. En particulier, dans la rhizosphère (le volume de sol influencé par l’activité des racines), des composés spécifiques de la MO sont présents sous la forme de polymères tels que les ExoPolySaccharides (EPS). Ces composés, exsudés par les racines et les micro-organismes, sont amphiphiles. Ils peuvent influencer le transfert d’eau dans la zone racinaire et agir comme un tampon dans l’absorption de l’eau par les racines. Comprendre ce rôle régulateur des EPS et, plus généralement, comment la matière organique modifie le transfert d’eau dans le sol, sont des questions ouvertes pour lesquelles la modélisation peut apporter des éléments de réponse. Pour mieux prendre en compte la nature variable hydrophobe/hydrophile de la matière organique, nous proposons un changement de paradigme dans la représentation de la mouillabilité du sol. Au lieu d’associer la mouillabilité à un simple angle de contact, nous proposons un cadre conceptuel énergétique, dans lequel une fonctionnelle énergétique représente les interactions effectives entre la matrice du sol et la surface de l’eau libre. Cette énergie d’interaction contient à la fois les interactions attractives (hydrophiles) et répulsives (hydrophobes), afin de reproduire la nature amphiphile des molécules. En incluant toutes les énergies à l’œuvre dans le sol, c’est-à-dire l’énergie associée à la mouillabilité, à la gravité et à la tension superficielle de l’eau, nous pouvons définir l’énergie libre du système. Nous avons dérivé l’équation régissant la saturation en eau, ce qui conduit à une équation de type Darcy- Richards couplée à une équation d’énergie libre. Ce système d’équations assure la cohérence avec la thermodynamique. Le système d’EDP est résolu numériquement à l’aide de méthodes d’éléments finis implémentées en C++ avec la bibliothèque Oomph-lib basée sur un schéma spatial et temporel adaptatif. Le code est appliqué à des systèmes 1D, 2D et 3D axisymétriques incluant différentes conditions aux limites. Le modèle est d’abord testé pour son comportement concernant les effets d’hydrophobie dans le sol. À cet égard, il est capable de reproduire l’étalement horizontal fini de l’eau sur un milieu poreux hydrophobe. Appliquée à un milieu poreux stratifié verticalement avec une alternance de couches hydrophiles et hydrophobes, la simulation numérique est capable de reproduire, en fonction de la teneur moyenne en eau, les phénomènes de piégeage de l’eau dans les couches hydrophiles, le transfert diffusif "commun" (à mesure que le milieu approche de la saturation), l’écoulement intermittent et l’instabilité de doigté. En outre, l’analyse numérique de bifurcation utilisant la bibliothèque PDE2PATH met en évidence des phénomènes d’hystérésis. C’est la première fois qu’un système EDP capture tous ces phénomènes dans un modèle de sol unique. Dans la deuxième partie, nous adaptons et testons expérimentalement notre modèle aux carac- téristiques spécifiques du sol rhizosphérique. Nous avons construit un milieu modèle composé de sable [SOURCE: Page_11]

ix [SOURCE: Page_11]

additionné d’EPS dérivé du mucilage de chia (analogue au mucilage de maïs). Des expériences ont été réalisées pour caractériser les paramètres du modèle. En particulier, nous avons étudié expérimentale- ment et numériquement l’infiltration d’une goutte placée sur le milieu modèle sable/EPS. Concernant le modèle, nous montrons que les lois régissant le flux d’eau s’infiltrant dans la matrice sont modifiées en présence de matière organique. Une fois calibré, le modèle peut reproduire différents phénomènes spécifiques à la présence de matière amphiphile observés lors de nos expériences ou dans la littérature. En particulier, l’effet "tampon" lors du mouillage et les différentes étapes de l’imbibition des gouttes. Enfin, le modèle est appliqué à l’absorption d’eau par les racines, à l’échelle d’un segment de racine avec sa rhizosphère, présentant un gradient de concentration en EPS en fonction de la distance à la racine. Cette application nous permet d’étudier l’impact des caractéristiques amphiphiles des EPS dans la rhizosphère pendant la phase d’extraction d’eau par la racine ou pendant la phase de réhumidification nocturne de la rhizosphère, avec ou sans apport d’eau externe. Intégrée dans un cadre plus large, à l’échelle d’un système racinaire, cette modélisation contribuera à l’analyse des traits rhizosphériques susceptibles d’améliorer l’utilisation de l’eau par les plantes dans un contexte de changement climatique et de pratiques. [SOURCE: Page_12]

Mots-clés de la thèse : Milieu poreux - Mouillabilité - Exopolysaccharides - Energie libre - Mod- élisation numérique [SOURCE: Page_12]

xviii/250 [SOURCE: Page_20]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_21]

General context The effects of greenhouse gases in the atmosphere are rapidly warming the earth’s surface [309]. This rise in average temperatures is having a major impact on oceans, glaciers, hydrological cycles and biodiversity [118]. Among the impacts of climate change, average changes in annual hydrology have been observed. In terms of precipitation, some zones are impacted by an increase in average precipitation, such as the equator and high latitudes, while others are suffering from heatwaves [309]. In Central Europe, we are seeing increasingly hot summers with severe heatwaves [10], particularly in the Mediterranean zone. Rising temperatures and climatic deregulation over extended periods impact soil and plant cover (hydric stress influences the reproduction and performance of plants [181, 308]). This deregulation influences water resources in the soil, such as groundwater recharge. In dry periods, it is difficult for water to infiltrate into the soil, and the small amount of water that infiltrates is used to irrigate crops, which has a major impact on water resources [192]. Before trying to find ways of improving plants’ resistance to water stress, we need to understand the mechanisms used by plants and soils to influence water transfer, such as the release of organic matter (mucilage for plants and Extracellullar Polymeric Substance for microorganisms [185]). There is a lot to learn about the underground part of plants, which is a much less well known zone than the aerial part. The release of these amphiphilic substances^1 must have a role to play for soil organisms, since they require an energy cost from them that could have been allocated to other functions. Therefore, it is interesting to study the influence of these substances in the soil, and in particular on hydraulic transfer such as water uptake by roots in the rhizosphere. Almost all the water used by the plant passes through zones of the soil influenced by these substances. This water is uptaken by the plant’s root system to satisfy the environmental demands of the aerial part (evapotranspiration process). This zone of soil close to the roots is distinguished from bulk soil by its special relationship with the plant: it is called the rhizosphere [306]. However, this zone is complex to study because of its location in the soil. Mathematical models are therefore needed to study the processes and simulate the hydraulic behavior of the rhizosphere under the influence of amphiphilic matter. [SOURCE: Page_21]

1 chemical substances containing a part with an affinity for water (hydrophilic) and an another one which repels it (hydrophobic). [SOURCE: Page_21]

1 [SOURCE: Page_21]

## INTRODUCTION

The objectives The aim of this thesis is to go further than current approaches to model water transfer in soils by taking into account the wettability of organic matter in the soil. In this work, we will pay particular attention to a thermodynamic concept affecting soil dynamics: the free energy of water in the soil. Thus, knowing the hydraulic properties of the soil makes it possible to couple processes in it, then better understand the processes associated with amphiphilic matter and water in the soil. We developed a model for simulating water transfer in the rhizosphere. Due to the high non- linearity of this new model, we will propose tools for temporal analysis and simulation. The objectives of this thesis are: [SOURCE: Page_22]

• improve water transfer models in soils, particularly for macroscopic models taking into account the presence of amphiphilic matter; [SOURCE: Page_22]

• provide numerical simulation tools; [SOURCE: Page_22]

• understand the influence of organic matter in the soil (e.g. EPS in the rhizosphere). [SOURCE: Page_22]

Outline This manuscript is divided into three main parts. The first part presents a state of the art on the issue of organic matter influence in soil and the modeling of their function in water transfer. The objectives of this chapter 1 are (i) to understand the composition of a soil and the effects of organic matter on its hydraulic properties, and (ii) to understand the modeling of water transfer in a soil in the presence of organic matter such as in the rhizosphere. Moreover, this chapter suggests ways of improving the modeling of water transfer taking into account the concept of wettability. The second part is devoted to the modeling of water transfer in a porous medium in the presence of amphiphilic matter. The chapter 2 presents the analysis and theoretical results of water transfer modeling based on the concept of free energy of water in soil. We will see in this chapter (i) how to represent the free energy of water in a soil and (ii) how wettability in the soil affects water transfer dynamics. The chapter 3 concerns numerical tools for simulating water transfer. We will present the numerical difficulties in simulating and analyzing transfer dynamics with this new model. The third and final part of the manuscript is devoted to the application of this modeling in the rhizosphere. The chapter 4 presents the experimental approaches for characterizing and parameterizing an analogue rhizosphere medium. This chapter shows (i) how to design an analogue rhizosphere medium and (ii) how to characterize and parameterize its hydraulic properties. Finally, the chapter 5 presents applications of water transfer dynamics in the rhizosphere, such as the infiltration of a drop into an analogue rhizosphere medium, or water uptake by a root. Finally, this manuscript ends with a general conclusion and possible perspectives for extending this work. [SOURCE: Page_22]

2/250 [SOURCE: Page_22]

Part I [SOURCE: Page_23]

State of art: The basics of soil water transfer models [SOURCE: Page_23]

3 [SOURCE: Page_23]

Chapter 1 [SOURCE: Page_25]

The basics of soil water transfer models [SOURCE: Page_25]

The aim of this chapter is to define the theoretical concepts associated to water transfer in soils, and to present existing work in the literature on the amphiphilic matter that influences water transfer dynamics in soil. We review the description of a soil and the effects of organic matter in the soil (section 1.1), to describe water transfer models in the soil and more particularly in the rhizosphere (section 1.2). To complete the global view of this thesis, we present the notion of wettability on a substrate as a perspective for improving models of water transfer in section 1.3. [SOURCE: Page_25]

## CHAPTER 1. THE BASICS OF SOIL WATER TRANSFER MODELS

1.1      Description of a soil Soil is a complex porous medium containing minerals and organisms (living and dead). It is represented by three main phases: [SOURCE: Page_26]

• the solid phase composed of organic matter and minerals (sand, silt and clay); [SOURCE: Page_26]

• the liquid phase composed of inorganic ions and ionized and non-ionized organic molecules; [SOURCE: Page_26]

• the gaseous phase close to air at the soil surface, but which may be enriched at depth with gaseous compounds such as nitrogen oxides(NO,N$_2$ O). [SOURCE: Page_26]

In this section, we present the complexity of a soil, describing the characteristics of a porous medium, such as the structure of a soil in the presence of organic matter, in order to define the hydraulic properties of a soil. [SOURCE: Page_26]

1.1.1   Characteristic parameters and notations of a porous medium A porous medium has physical characteristics associated with dynamics such as the flow of water through it. Characteristic parameters are therefore needed to describe and model the structure and porosity of the medium, or its moisture content. These characteristic parameters are essential for representing the properties of the medium in models and simulations. [SOURCE: Page_26]

Structure and porosity [SOURCE: Page_26]

A porous medium is formed by solid elements and void space (Figure 1.1). Here we refer to the solid phase as the solid matrix, and the void phase as the pore space or fluid space. The total volume of the porous medium Vt [m^3 ] is the sum of fluid volume Vf [m^3 ] and solid volume Vs [m^3 ], [SOURCE: Page_26]

Vt = Vf + Vs .                                        (1.1) [SOURCE: Page_26]

The pore space can contain one or more fluids (e.g. water and air). In the case of water transfer in [SOURCE: Page_26]

ﬂuid space soil matrix [SOURCE: Page_26]

Figure 1.1: Sketch of a porous medium, inspired of [91]. [SOURCE: Page_26]

the medium, the pore space of volume Vf comprises a volume of air Va [m^3 ] and a volume of water Vw [m^3 ] such that Vf = Va + Vw .                                       (1.2) [SOURCE: Page_26]

## 1.1. DESCRIPTION OF A SOIL

The pore space depends on its available fluid volume. Indices of void volume relative to the structure of the medium are used to characterize the fluid space. Among these indices, we have the following: [SOURCE: Page_27]

• porosity ϕ [−] is a soil physics notation representing the index of relative pore void volume Vf in soil Vt , Vf ϕ=      ;                                       (1.3) Vt [SOURCE: Page_27]

• void index Iv [−] is a soil mechanics and engineering notation representing the index of relative void volume of pores Vf compared to solid volume Vs , [SOURCE: Page_27]

Vf Iv =      .                                       (1.4) Vs [SOURCE: Page_27]

In the rest of this manuscript, we will use the porosity notation to represent the fluid space. Note that this ϕ parameter is between 0 and 1. Porosity size varies in porous media. A classification of porosity in the medium is based on the void size of the pore space. This criterion’s limitations in classifying porosity are much debated in the literature [14, 31]. Luxmoore [222] suggests a classification that associates a type of porosity with a void size: [SOURCE: Page_27]

• macroporosity: size greater than 100 µm; [SOURCE: Page_27]

• mesoporosity: size between 10 and 100 µm; [SOURCE: Page_27]

• microporosity: size smaller than 10 µm. [SOURCE: Page_27]

However, porosity also depends on the solid structure of the medium. The distribution of solid elements influences the pore space. The more compact the medium, the smaller the void volume is. We then express a specific volume Vb [m^3 .kg−1 ] indicating the compactness index representing the total volume of the medium in relation to its dry mass Md [kg], [SOURCE: Page_27]

Vs Vb =      ,                                          (1.5) Md [SOURCE: Page_27]

where Md is the mass of dry solid particles [kg]. Note that this specific volume is the inverse of the apparent density of a porous medium in a dry state ρb [kg.m−3 ], [SOURCE: Page_27]

Md ρb =      .                                          (1.6) Vt [SOURCE: Page_27]

Apparent density and specific volume are two indicators of the compaction of a porous medium. The bulk density is the most widely used compaction indicator in the literature. [SOURCE: Page_27]

Moisture of the porous medium [SOURCE: Page_27]

Water is a fluid commonly found in porous media. This liquid flows into the pore space. A level of moisture is then present in porous medium. There are several indicators that express this moisture: [SOURCE: Page_27]

• weight moisture or gravimetric water content w [kg.kg−1 ] is the mass of water Mw [kg] relative to the mass of dry solid particles Md , Mw w=       ;                                      (1.7) Md [SOURCE: Page_28]

• volumetric water content θ [m^3 .m−3 ] is the volume of water Vw in the volume of the medium Vt , [SOURCE: Page_28]

Vw θ=       ;                                         (1.8) Vt [SOURCE: Page_28]

• degree of saturation or saturation s [m^3 .m−3 ] is the volume of water Vw in the volume of pores Vf , Vw s=        .                                    (1.9) Vf [SOURCE: Page_28]

Note that these moisture indicators are equivalent. Each indicator is appropriate to the moisture study to be carried out. Gravimetric water content is very useful for measuring moisture in porous media. However, it is not representative of moisture in terms of the volume of water available and present in the medium. In this situation, volumic water content and saturation indicators are more appropriate. These indicators are used to study the moisture in the volumes of the medium. There exist equivalence relationships between each moisture. Among these relationships, we have these main equivalences [128, 170]: [SOURCE: Page_28]

• the gravimetric water content w and the volumic water content θ are related by the dry density γd [−] of the medium and is written as θ = wγd ,                                     (1.10) [SOURCE: Page_28]

where γd is the ratio of the density of the dry medium ρd [kg.m−3 ] to the density of the water ρw [kg.m−3 ], ρd γd =    ;                                     (1.11) ρw [SOURCE: Page_28]

• saturation s is expressed by the ratio between the volume weight of the gravimetric water content ρd gw [kg.m−3 .kg−1 .m^3 ] and the volume weight of the gravimetric water content at saturation ρd gwsat [kg.m−3 .kg−1 .m^3 ], w s=       ;                                    (1.12) wsat [SOURCE: Page_28]

• saturation s is expressed as the ratio of the volumetric water content θ and the porosity ϕ, [SOURCE: Page_28]

θ s=     .                                       (1.13) ϕ [SOURCE: Page_28]

Note that the moisture index associated with saturation is not relevant for media whose porosity changes in the presence of water (e.g. clay media that swell in contact with water). In our study, we will assume that the medium porosity does not change in the presence of water, or that it is balanced on average in the medium. [SOURCE: Page_28]

## 1.1. DESCRIPTION OF A SOIL

These moisture indicators describe the distribution of water in a porous medium. There are three main classes of water present in a porous medium (Figure 1.2): [SOURCE: Page_29]

• gravitaty flow driven water is water flowing rapidly under gravitational forces, and is mainly found in macroporosity; [SOURCE: Page_29]

• the available water content is the water retained by the attractive force of the solids present mainly in the microporosity; [SOURCE: Page_29]

• capillary water is water strongly bound to solid particles. [SOURCE: Page_29]

gravity ﬂow driven water available water content capillary water [SOURCE: Page_29]

Figure 1.2: Sketch of water distribution in a porous medium. [SOURCE: Page_29]

In the case where the porous medium is a soil, this delimitation of water descriptions is associated with water accessibility by a plant. We call "field capacity" a soil without gravity water. Thus, we define the available water content as the water between the field capacity and the point of permanent wilting (plant death). The water in the available water content is available to the plant through root suction forces. Note that temporary wilting points correspond to transpiration by the plant exceeding water absorption in the soil (reversible wilting). In the case where water is not accessible by the roots, the water flows is rapidly into the macroporosity (gravity flow driven water) or it is retained by the solid phase (capillary water). This water is therefore unavailable to the plant. A porous medium is never totally dry or totally saturated (all pores filled with water). A residual saturation remains adsorbed to the solid elements of the medium, and the medium is rarely saturated at 100%, as a residual amount of air is almost always trapped even in very wet pores [170]. We assume that the saturated water content θsat [m^3 .m−3 ] is approximately equal to the porosity, i.e. θsat ≈ ϕ. An effective moisture is then used to represent the water in the medium, excluding capillary water and the fraction of potentially trapped air flowing through the medium. This new indicator is generally expressed as a volume ratio. The effective volumetric water content θe [m^3 .m−3 ] is then expressed as the difference between the water content in the medium θ and the residual water content θr , [SOURCE: Page_29]

θ e = θ − θr .                                       (1.14) [SOURCE: Page_29]

An effective saturation se [m^3 .m−3 ] is generally expressed as the ratio between the effective volumet- ric water content and the difference between the water content at saturation θsat and the residual volumetric water content, θe se =            .                                     (1.15) θsat − θr [SOURCE: Page_29]

However, this indicator does not take into account the correct void volume, since it considers capillary water to be associated with the solid phase. For the remainder of this work, it is important to note that in multi-phase media such as porous media: the saturation of fluids in the pore space must be decomposed such that the sum of these saturation is 1. In our case, we consider water in the two forms described above water (bound or free) and air. Thus, the saturation of water s and the saturation of air sa [-] are the only saturation in the pore space (Figure 1.3), i.e. [SOURCE: Page_30]

s + sa = 1.                                         (1.16) [SOURCE: Page_30]

The water saturation s in the medium is expressed as the sum of the residual water saturation [SOURCE: Page_30]

air water solid [SOURCE: Page_30]

Figure 1.3: Sketch of a porous medium with different phase of fluid. [SOURCE: Page_30]

sr [m^3 .m−3 ] and the free water saturation sw [m^3 .m−3 ], [SOURCE: Page_30]

s = sr + sw .                                        (1.17) [SOURCE: Page_30]

In the presence of air in the pore space, water saturation has a limit which takes into account the air trapped in the medium. This limit is given by a compression threshold scomp such that scomp = 1 − sa . Note that if all the pores are filled with water, then the water content by volume at saturation is equivalent to the porosity, i.e. θsat = ϕ. Thus, water saturation admits a limit under air compression (scomp = 1). In the rest of this manuscript, we will mainly use the moisture indicator associated to the volume to facilitate modeling and define definition domains for our moisture in the porous medium. [SOURCE: Page_30]

1.1.2   Organic matter: a substance influencing soil structure Soil is a porous medium composed of organic matter produced by living soil organisms. Figure 1.4 is a simplified sketch of a soil including the structure of a porous medium in the presence of organic matter as describe in Figures 1.5-1.6. The presence of organic matter and living organisms add complexity to the study of soil structure. In order to understand how organic matter interacts with the porous medium, it is essential to understand the living organisms and solid structures in the soil. [SOURCE: Page_30]

The living soil organisms [SOURCE: Page_30]

Soil is a porous medium colonized by a large variety of living organisms. They represent the entire pedoflora and pedofauna. [SOURCE: Page_30]

## 1.1. DESCRIPTION OF A SOIL

ﬂuid space soil matrix organic matter [SOURCE: Page_31]

Figure 1.4: Sketch of a soil structure [SOURCE: Page_31]

Soil fauna represents all the animals living in the soil. Depending on their size, soil fauna includes microfauna (organisms smaller than 0.2 mm, e.g. roundworms and protozoa), mesofauna (organisms between 0.2 and 4 mm, e.g. mites, springtails, enchytreids, nematodes), macrofauna (organisms be- tween 4 and 80 mm, e.g. worms, insects, molluscs) and megafauna (organisms larger than 80 mm, e.g. moles, groundhogs, rabbits) [217, 55]. Pedoflora is the group of microorganisms and macroscopic underground plant organs living in the soil. Microorganisms (bacteria, fungi) make up the majority of living soil organisms [150]. They account for 1 to 4% of soil organic carbon [312]. Among them, fungi account for up to 80% of microbial biomass [132]. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Aggregates in the soil: the habitat of living organisms [SOURCE: Page_31]

Soil is a habitat produced by living soil organisms. It is made up of several heterogeneous assemblages of substances or elements that adhere strongly to each other, called aggregates. These structures are more or less large in the medium. They have complex and irregular organizational structure. They are classified by aggregation size [116, 330, 151]: large aggregates and lumps (greater than 2000 mm), macroaggregates (between 250 µm and 2000 mm), microaggregates (between 2 and 250 µm) and primary particles (less than 2 µm). These aggregates offer liveable structures for soil organisms. They live in different soil areas in the void spaces adapted to their size. Therefore, the size of the living organisms present in a structure is correlated with the size of the aggregates. For example, microorganisms live in all structures larger than 0.2 µm, while megafauna and macrofauna live in large aggregates and lumps. Note that structures smaller than 0.2 µm are not habitable [217]. Pedofauna and pedoflora are important factors in soil aggregation. They constitute an important fraction of the aggregates and structure of their habitat. Mesofauna, macrofauna, megafauna and roots generate porosity to live in soil structures. For example, different species of earthworms burrow tunnels [184, 60] and roots move aggregates [278, 174, 153]. [SOURCE: Page_31]

Organic matter: a soil-aggregating substance [SOURCE: Page_31]

Living soil organisms make up and contribute to the living soil biomass. They produce organic matter, which accounts for 1 to 3% of soil solids [251]. Organic matter feeds a biotransformation cycle of living and dead soil biomass. It is in perpetual evolution, passing through three successive stages: [SOURCE: Page_31]

11/250 [SOURCE: Page_31]


---
[CHUNK_METADATA]
- Parent_Topic: 4623150

## CHAPTER 1. THE BASICS OF SOIL WATER TRANSFER MODELS

fresh organic matter (dead plants and animals), transitional or young humic products (mucilage, an Extracellular Polymeric Substances) and stable humus (cellulose and lignin). The biomass of living soil organisms represents around 15% of soil carbon [55]. Despite the low representation of living biomass, it plays an important role in the description of soil at different scales. Organic matter produced by living soil organisms has a direct or indirect influence on soil structure. The release of viscous organic matter promotes the direct adhesion of substances to solid minerals and living or dead organisms forming aggregates. Microorganisms are the main agents aggregating the soil [78]. Among them, bacteria and organic substances such as mucilage form microaggregates [74] while fungi produce hyphae that hold aggregates together forming macroaggregates [94]. Roots also play an aggregating role. They liberate exudates, border cells [25, 133] and root mu- cilage [185] that affect aggregation formation and stabilize it around the root [278, 174, 153]. Root mucilage aggregates and lubricates the medium to promote root penetration into the structure. Root tips displace soil particles [220, 268], reducing mechanical impedance [185]. Other plant organic substances (exudates and root debris) are integrated with the microbial biomass to form biofilms. These biofilms contain Extracellular Polymeric Substances produced by microbial which act as binding agents in the microaggregates [76]. A similar effect is seen with pedofauna (earth- worms), which produce faecal matter and mucus that stimulate aggregation by the microbial biomass. This aggregation changes the microbial habitat into a more porous, ordered and aggregated struc- ture [129]. The microaggregate stabilized by microbial matter, particularly polysaccharides, remains stable after the death of its hosts [333]. These processes are amplified by fine roots, which cause intense, periodic drying of their environment and promote aggregation processes [329, 278]. The drying phase is essential for aggregation. In the long term, this affects soil structure heterogeneity development in the soil profile [364]. In addition, interactions can accumulate during aggregate formation. For example, roots and associated mycorrhizal fungi promote soil macroaggregation [330, 94, 286, 353]. This overview of soil structure and the organic substances that affect it, proves that organic matter is a key substance in soil aggregation. In order to understand water transfer in the pore space, it is essential to look at the physical properties of this complex medium. A particular focus is set on the rhizosphere. [SOURCE: Page_32]

1.1.3    The hydraulic properties of a soil: application to the rhizosphere The rhizosphere is the soil zone under the influence of plant roots [171, 174]. This zone is one of the most complex and crucial interfaces on Earth [174]. A multitude of biological, physical and chemical processes support all the other trophic levels of the biosphere [298]. [SOURCE: Page_32]

Processes in the rhizosphere [SOURCE: Page_32]

Processes in the rhizosphere occur on multiple spatial and temporal scales. They result from biological, physical and chemical interactions between organisms, solids and fluids in the rhizosphere. These processes include, in particular: the development of the root hydraulic architecture [368], the impact of salinity stress [300, 187], the uptake of nutrients [114, 210], the interactions between soil structure and root growth [205, 220, 268], the impact of mucilage in root water uptake [301], the impact of organic matter the soil structure [24], the behavior of organic matter in presence of microorganism [254], the [SOURCE: Page_32]

Understanding processes in the rhizosphere should lead to better comprehend those at the woodland edge, forest edge and in the soil in general. For example, it is important to better understand the effects of organic matter on the structure and hydraulic properties of the medium. [SOURCE: Page_33]

Organic matter in the rhizosphere [SOURCE: Page_33]

The rhizosphere is mainly composed of rhizodeposits, including root mucilage, which stimulates bacte- rial activity (degradation of organic matter and production of Extracellular Polymeric Substances) [265]. This mucilage is secreted at the root tip [185] and varies along root axis [179, 344]. The chemical com- position of root mucilage can influence the hydraulic properties of the medium. This composition varies according to plant species [376, 245, 244, 37], microbial community [370] but also during their growth [72, 248, 58]. Carminati [62] has shown experimentally that mucilage-type substances have an impact on water content in the rhizosphere of 2 to 3 week-old roots. However, according to Nguyen et al. [249] and Mary et al. [229], the decomposition half-life of mucilage can vary between 3 and 11 days. This could be explained by the fact that bacteria degrade mucilage and produce substances similar to those of root mucilage [254]. [SOURCE: Page_33]

Hydraulic properties of mucilage in soil [SOURCE: Page_33]

Root mucilage is a gelatinous substance comprising mostly polysaccharides (between 78.4% [246] and 94% [38]). These polysaccharides are sugar units with amphiphilic properties, i.e. they have hydrophilic head (capillary effect) and hydrophobic tail (repellent water) which affect the hydraulic properties of the mucilage. This root mucilage impacts soil properties similarly as the Extracellular Polymeric Substances of bacteria [79, 367]. Here, we assume that we are not differentiating between root mucilage and the Extracellular Polymeric Substances of bacteria. These properties vary according to mucilage concentration and water saturation [277], i.e. more or less diluted mucilage changes its physico-chemical properties. These properties go from liquid to viscoelastic hydrogel [40], which influences the behavior of water in the soil and its structure [63, 361]. At high concentrations, when the medium is drying, [SOURCE: Page_33]

and Figure 1.6 are schemes from Bernard et al [23] and Van Veelen et al [344] which represents liquid bridges due to mucilage in the porous medium, at pore scale. It illustrates the principle of aggregation to form a structure around the root, called rhizosheath. This aggregation is generated by the low surface tension and high viscosity of the mucilage [63]. This process is referred to adsorption of the mucilage to soil solids. The mucilage is too sticky to diffuse into the pore space [277] due to the increased viscosity of the fluid [282, 130, 131, 314, 245] as saturation decreases. The glue effect of the mucilage forms a three-dimensional gel network in the pore space between the water and the solid [287, 131, 199, 37]. The forces in the soil increase [245, 252], improving connectivity between soil particles and water [66, 3, 63]. Because mucilage increases viscosity in soil, hydraulic conductivity decreases in the presence of mucilage for a constant load [238, 68, 199]. By increasing surface tension and decreasing viscosity, i.e the mucilage concentration decrease [281, 274], liquid bridges are broken [65, 68, 5, 298]. In other words, at low concentrations and in the presence of water, mucilage forms a Newtonian fluid with low viscosity [63, 124, 125, 298], which reduces the attraction with the solid of the liquid bridge. In this state, the mucilage promotes diffusion [277] that lubricates the soil and reduces friction between soil particles and the root, i.e. it facilitates root penetration into the soil. Mucilage affects soil water flux during drying cycles that modify the water adsorption, viscosity, wettability [348]. It has a high water retention capacity [288, 280, 130, 302], swelling on contact with water and can hold water up to 1000 times its dry weigh [231]. It improves water storage compared with bulk soil during drying of the medium [201, 125]. However, it becomes hydrophobic during dry phase [61, 238, 24], i.e. repellent water occurs during dry cycle. Thus, the mucilage acts as a buffer, regulating hydraulic flow to the root. For example, rehumectation of the dry rhizosphere can take between a few hours and a few days [282, 65, 38, 244], illustrating the slow movement of water in this [SOURCE: Page_34]

1.2.1.1    Saturated medium: Darcy law [SOURCE: Page_36]

Darcy law [90] is a fundamental empirical relationship used to study a laminar, viscosity-dominated flow (low Reynolds number Re ≪ 1) in a porous medium. It represents the water flow whose volume flow rate Q [m^3 .s−1 ] passes through a water-saturated porous medium of cross-section S [m^2 ] and length L [m]. It establishes a proportional relationship between the volumetric flow rate Q and the applied pressure gradient P , k P Q= S ,                                           (1.18) µ L where µ is the dynamic viscosity of the flowing fluid [Pa.s] and k is the intrinsic permeability of the medium [m^2 ]. Figure 1.8 displays the Darcy experiment associated to the Darcy law for a constant potential H (difference in water level between two reservoirs). Permeability is a variable parameter [SOURCE: Page_36]

The system is assumed to include an open area for releasing a volume flow of water Q. This experiment measures the volume flow rate of the medium at a given pressure. [SOURCE: Page_36]

1.2.1.2   Unsaturated porous medium [SOURCE: Page_37]

Buckingham [48] proposed a generalization of Darcy law for unsaturated media in 1907. He proposed that the hydraulic conductivity K becomes a function of the water content of the porous medium. Thus, he established Buckingham law, also known as Buckingham/Darcy law, expressed: [SOURCE: Page_37]

v = −K(s)∇H.                                           (1.24) [SOURCE: Page_37]

Hydraulic potential of the soil [SOURCE: Page_37]

Hydraulic potential is the energy state of water in the soil, which govern the movement of the liquid phase in the soil. This potential is affected by any potential difference that causes water to move in the soil. The total hydraulic potential of a soil, denoted Φt [J.kg−1 ], corresponds to the sum of the components influencing the forces acting on the water and affecting its energetic state [260, 170, 256]. These components are generally: [SOURCE: Page_37]

• gravitational potential, denoted Φg , corresponds to the gravitational forces. [SOURCE: Page_37]

• matric potential or capillary potential, denoted Φm , corresponds to adsorption and capillary effects of the solid phase which interact with water phase. It is composed of capillary forces and attractive forces at the surface of solid particles. These forces are responsible for the retention of water in soil pores. [SOURCE: Page_37]

• air pressure potential, denoted Φa , resulting from external gas pressure applied to the water. This potential is generally neglected. [SOURCE: Page_37]

• osmotic potential, denoted ΦΠ , due to solutes in water. Molecules of water will move from the area with the highest concentration of solutes to the area with the lowest concentration, reducing the hydraulic potential. In soil, osmotic potential is often neglected except near root cell walls. [SOURCE: Page_37]

Therefore the total hydraulic potential is given by the sum of these components: [SOURCE: Page_38]

Φt = Φg + Φm + ΦΠ + Φa + . . .                                 (1.25) [SOURCE: Page_38]

Note that hydraulic potential is an energy that can be expressed in different ways [55]: [SOURCE: Page_38]

• hydraulic potential per unit mass Φ [J.kg−1 ]; [SOURCE: Page_38]

• hydraulic potential per unit volume of water Ψ = ρw Φ = ρw gφ [J.m−3 ]. This is homogeneous to a pressure [Pa]; [SOURCE: Page_38]

• hydraulic potential per unit weight of water φ = ρΨ wg = Φg [J.N−1 ]. This is homogeneous to a pressure head [m]. [SOURCE: Page_38]

This concept of hydraulic potential in a soil is generally well defined in theoretical articles mention- ing free energy [260, 170, 256]. However, there remains an ambiguity with measured pressure, which has the same physical dimension as hydraulic potential. A confusion arises where pressure is associated with hydraulic potential. We will discuss this in more detail in chapter 2 in order to clarify it and its components. [SOURCE: Page_38]

Hydrodynamic properties of a porous medium [SOURCE: Page_38]

Water transfer in a porous medium is characterized by hydrodynamic properties. These properties are defined by the water retention characteristic curve h(θ) and the hydraulic conductivity K(θ) or K(h). [SOURCE: Page_38]

The water retention characteristic curve is the representation of the soil’s capacity to retain water. This retention is due to the water potential, also called matric potential, noted h or ψm . This term is called potential here, but it corresponds to the pressure associated with the volumetric energy required to retain the water in the medium. We will discuss about this ambiguity further in the chapter 2. In this chapter, we assume that matric potential is a pressure. In unsaturated medium, this matric potential is negative. Note that, the negative of this potential is called suction. As the soil becomes drier, the medium looses saturation, and the remaining water is more strongly bound to the soil. It becomes harder to extract it from the soil. More energy is required to loosen this water. The retention curve is a measure of the bonding intensity between the water and solid phase. It varies for each soil with the characteristics of pore (size, shape and distribution) and decreases with the rate of water saturation. Then, the matric potential is related to water content by empirical function defined by the retention curve. In this study, we assume that it is expressed per unit weight of water, as is generally the case in the soil physics literature. In other words, it is expressed in terms of water column height. Several empirical relationships have been established over the last few decades, as shown in Leong’s, Bullied’s or Too’s reviews [211, 49, 332]. These functions have to be fitted with retention measurements, i.e some parameters need to be fix. The number of parameters varies according to the model chosen (from 2 parameters for the Williams model to 5 parameters for the Omuto model). The table 1.1 summarizes the various relationships associated with the water retention characteristic curve and the [SOURCE: Page_38]

The hydraulic conductivity is the capacity of a porous medium to infiltrate water, and relates the water content to the hydraulic conductivity K. This expresses a soil’s ability to let a fluid through under the effect of a pressure gradient. The hydraulic conductivity is the ratio between the volume flow of water (expressed in m.s−1 ) through a given soil section and the gradient head (expressed without unit) governing this flow: Darcy law if the medium is saturated, or Buckingham’s law if the medium is unsaturated. Therefore, it is homogeneous to a water flow or velocity, and is expressed in m.s−1 . In a saturated medium, all pores are filled and hydraulic conductivity is at its maximum (K = Ksat ). By analogy, the pore network can be assimilated to a set of capillaries, which allow water to flow through them. However, the forces of the substrate become more and more difficult to transfer water when the soil is drying. So, during drying processes, the pores with the largest diameters are emptied first: the water flows into pores with smaller and smaller diameters. As a result, friction forces increase, slowing down the flow through smaller pores and reducing hydraulic conductivity. Water flow is much lower in dry soil than in moist soil. Hydraulic conductivity is a function proportional to hydraulic conductivity, and relative conductivity kr (increases with soil water saturation), [SOURCE: Page_40]

K(s) = Ksat kr (s).                       (1.27) [SOURCE: Page_40]

This model most commonly used to describe the hydraulic conductivity curve is that proposed by Van Genuchten [343] using Mualem model [240],    h      1 im  2 K(s) = Ksat sl 1 − 1 − s m        ,               (1.28) [SOURCE: Page_40]

where Ksat is the hydraulic conductivity at saturation [m.s−1 ] and l, m are parameters. Note that generally l = 0.5 and m = 1 − n^1 [240]. Figure 1.10 illustrates the hydraulic conductivity curve for a [SOURCE: Page_40]

Richards equation [SOURCE: Page_41]

Water transfer in an unsaturated porous medium is described by the Richards equation. In 1931, Richards [284] proposed coupling the Buckingham-Darcy equation with the mass conservation equation to describe dynamic phenomena in a porous medium. The mass conservation equation for a liquid is given by ⃗ = 0, ∂t ρ + ∇ · ρU                                      (1.29) [SOURCE: Page_41]

with ρ the density of the fluid and U the velocity of a fluid particle. The density of the fluid in a porous medium ρp depends on the density of the water ρw , the porosity ϕ and the saturation s: [SOURCE: Page_41]

ρp = ρw sϕ.                                  (1.30) [SOURCE: Page_41]

Thus, the mass conservation of the water phase in the porous medium reads [SOURCE: Page_41]

∂t (ϕρw s) + ∇ · (ρw v) = 0.                          (1.31) [SOURCE: Page_41]

The medium is assumed to not be deformed, or to be slowly deformed compared to the fluid motions, i.e. the porosity is assumed to be constant. Inserting Darcy law Eq. (1.18) into the mass conservation of the water phase Eq. (1.31), we obtain Richards equation ( ϕ∂t s + ∇ · [K(s)∇H] = 0, (1.32) H = h(s) + z. [SOURCE: Page_41]

Note that this formulation is equivalent to the following: [SOURCE: Page_41]

∂t θ + ∇ · [K(θ)∇ (hc (θ) + z)] = 0.                        (1.33) [SOURCE: Page_41]

In soil physics, the hydraulic head associated with capillary pressure is defined by the water retention curve. This curve is monotonic and decreasing. A bijective function exists between water content and hydraulic head hc . Using this relationship between matric potential and water content, Richards equation can be written as follows [SOURCE: Page_42]

C(θ)∂t hc + ∇ · [K(hc )∇ (hc + z)] = 0,                         (1.34) [SOURCE: Page_42]

with C(θ) specific capillary capacity [m−1 ] [SOURCE: Page_42]

∂θ C(θ) =       .                                    (1.35) ∂h Note that Richards equation can also be expressed as [SOURCE: Page_42]

∂t θ + ∇ · [D(θ)∇θ] + ∇ · [K(θ)⃗ez ] = 0,                       (1.36) [SOURCE: Page_42]

with D(θ) the specific diffusion coefficient [m^2 .s−1 ] [SOURCE: Page_42]

∂h D(θ) = K(θ)       .                                  (1.37) ∂θ This equation is a complex relationship in the natural sciences [127]. Despite its similarity to the diffusion equation, its non-linearity and variations in diffusion according to water content make it complex to study. There are methods for solving this equation on one-dimensional domains and obtaining analytic solutions [345]: Kirchhoff transform, Laplace transform, Boltzmann transform or Traveling wave solutions. These methods have been also used to solve higher-dimensional problems using one-dimensional formulations. This model is one of the most widely used to represent transfer in porous media, despite its poor representation in highly unsaturated media. Note that there are other water transfer models based on other laws of fluid mechanics in porous media, such as the Navier-Stokes, Stokes or Brinkman equations (high porosity ϕ > 0.95 [115]). We have seen many models have been developed to represent water transfer in porous media. Therefore, we can investigate the development of water transfer models that take into account the presence of organic matter (mucilage), which influences the transfer (1.1.3). [SOURCE: Page_42]

1.2.2    Model in effective medium in presence of mucilage [SOURCE: Page_42]

One of the first approaches to modeling water transfer in the presence of mucilage is to consider a homogeneous medium and measure its water properties. For example, models have been developed for soil sampling that neglect the influence of mucilage [290, 183, 111]. Thus, they consider that transfer in this soil zone is modeled by the Richards model (section 1.2.1.2). Mucilage introduces heterogeneity into the medium, particularly at the interfaces between soil with and without mucilage. These interfaces are generally considered to be at non-equilibrium due to swelling and shrinkage of the mucilage during wetting and drying cycles. Carminati [61] proposes adding the first order time constant equilibration model with Richards model to represent the wetting rate of mucilage in this non-equilibrium zone. He considers that the water content θ in the rhizosphere [SOURCE: Page_42]

The first-order time constant equilibration model was developed in particular for mobile and immobile solute transport in a soil zone [261] and between fracture macroporosity and matrix pores [147]. Carminati model takes into account a fraction of soil with mucilage (non-equilibrium dynamics) and one without, following the classical transfer equations, with a clear boundary between soil without and with mucilage. This model has been made more flexible and complete by Kroener et al. [201]. They consider a progressive transition between bulk soil and rhizosphere. They define a nonmucilage- filled pore ratio R in the rhizosphere. This ratio is the ratio between the volume of pores that are not filled with mucilage and the total pore volume. Assuming that the rhizosphere is located between two extremes: the soil completely saturated with mucilage (R = 0) and without mucilage (R = 1). Therefore, there are models that take into account the effects of mucilage in the rhizosphere for constant concentrations [61, 64, 301] and for variable concentrations between rhizosphere and bulk soil [200]. ## 1.3. MODELING OF WETTABILITY AND APPLICATION TO THE SOIL

1.2.3    Pore scale model [SOURCE: Page_45]

During the last decade, several models have been developed, seeking to integrate biological, chemical and physical processes in the rhizosphere [298]. Recent work has focused on better modeling the effects of mucilage, particularly at the pore scale. Jahromi et al [125] have developed a model based on a lattice spring method (LSM) to simulate the distribution of mucilage in the dry state at the pore scale. However, for wetter states, it is necessary to take into account additional physical phenomena such as surface tension, contact angle and viscoelasticity. They aim to develop a Lattice-Boltzmann simulation framework (methods commonly used to simulate pore scale dynamics of liquids [272, 285, 315, 340]) to simulate two-phase flow involving mucilage. This model should improve the representation of the retention curve in the presence of mucilage [125]. However, this type of model requires the geometry of the soil structure in order to be used. Imaging methods (X-ray computed tomography [334, 275] or magnetic resonance imaging [313, 157]) are gener- ally used to characterize the spatial distribution of soil structure (macropore networks and plant roots in 3D) and to visualize and quantify soil water dynamics [297, 218]. Note that this question of imaging coupled with predictive modeling is a recent topic for understanding rhizosphere processes [273, 291], the challenges of modeling soil processes [346], or plant-soil modeling [294]. In general, current models do not take into account the modification of geometry over time due to the actions of mucilage and their degradation by microbial action. Rötzer et al [296] have proposed a model representing soil aggregation dynamics in the presence of mucilage and a root. This model is an interesting perceptive for studying the evolution of soil structure, and could be coupled with one of the existing pore scale models. However, to reproduce the transfer dynamics on a larger scale, the challenge is to select a correct pore volume representative of the medium. A mathematical homogenization of the domain is required [279], but this is not always apparent. Thus defining this representative volume is easier in controlled media (microfluidic model [289, 311, 310]) but remains very complex to define in a natural medium such as the rhizosphere. Pore scale models are very useful for better understanding certain local pore scale processes, but they are difficult to extend to larger scales. Moreover, models in the rhizosphere do not take into account the hydrophobic interactions present in the concept of soil wettability in the presence of amphiphilic or hydrophobic matter. Therefore, wettability offers an potential improvement to these water transfer models in the presence of amphiphilic matter. [SOURCE: Page_45]

1.3      Modeling of wettability and application to the soil 1.3.1    Wettability on plane substrate [SOURCE: Page_45]

Wetting is the study of the spreading of a liquid placed on a solid (or liquid) substrate [93]. Wettability is the ability of a fluid to spread on a surface. This phenomenon affects a wide range of fields, including industry, the automotive industry, pharmaceuticals and soil. Understanding surface wetting means understanding why water spreads on hydrophilic substrates rather than hydrophobic surfaces. Moreover, it allows us to understand the spreading behavior of a [SOURCE: Page_45]

Capillary phenomena [SOURCE: Page_46]

Capillary phenomena take many forms: the rise of water with nutrients by the roots in the stems of a plant, the formation of a meniscus in a glass of water or the rise of water in a sponge or wall [93]. They occur at interfaces between two non-miscible liquids, between a liquid and air, or between a liquid and a solid surface. They are due to surface tension forces corresponding to surface energy. Surface tension is a physico-chemical phenomenon related to molecular interactions in a fluid. Molecules interact with each other in a fluid. However, in the neighbourhood of another phase (solid, liquid or gas), the molecules in this interface are influenced by the molecular forces of the other phase (Figure 1.11). The forces that interact are generally attractive (Van der Waals) and long-range. They can also be repulsive, but act at short range [227]. These interactions at this interface explain why a [SOURCE: Page_46]

Gas Interface Liquid [SOURCE: Page_46]

Figure 1.11: Sketch of surface tension. The black arrows correspond to attractive molecular interac- tions. [SOURCE: Page_46]

Gerris can stand on water and move along its surface without going underwater, or why a needle can float. From an energetic point of view, surface tension influences the shape of the liquid. When a liquid is influenced by elementary work W [J] at an interface A [m^2 ], it modifies its shape to adjust its surface tension and minimize its free surface (available surface not subject to viscous stress from the other phase). This elementary work is proportional to surface tension γ [N.m−1 ], [SOURCE: Page_46]

dW = γdA. [SOURCE: Page_46]

Thus, when the liquid supplies (resp. receives) elementary work, the molecules on the liquid surface have lost (resp. gained) their attractive interactions, affecting the surface tension of the liquid. This change represents an energy cost in order to maintain the liquid in its current state. Indeed, the surface tension at the γ interface corresponds to the derivative of the free energy of a closed system (liquid [SOURCE: Page_46]

Therefore, a liquid with a free surface minimizes its surface area until its surface tension minimizes the energy of the system. This explains why a drop with a free surface has a spherical shape. Thus, to increase the spherical surface area of the drop, in our example, we need to supply it with energy. However, the curvature of the free surface implies the formation of over pressure and under-pressure on the liquid surface (pressure discontinuity), called Laplace pressure. This pressure tends to increase (in the case of over-pressure) or decrease (in the case of under-pressure) the water level at the convex or concave part respectively of the water surface (Figure 1.12) [19]. Laplace pressure at the interface between a liquid and another non-miscible phase expresses the pressure difference between the inside of any fluid pint and the outside pressure pout at the interface as proportional to the mean surface curvature κ and surface tension [93]: [SOURCE: Page_47]

∆p = pint − pout = κγ. [SOURCE: Page_47]

In the case of a liquid on a solid, the free surface of the liquid is curved on contact with a solid wall along a typical length, noted ℓc : this length is called the capillary length. The curvature of the free liquid surface is governed by capillary forces and gravity. Thus, capillary length results from the equilibrium between Laplace pressure and hydrostatic pressure (∆p = ρgℓc ): r γ ℓc =          . Note that the capillary length corresponds to the inverse of the curvature (κ−1 ). This length is the critical equilibrium length between gravity and capillary forces. When the fluid length is greater than the capillary length, gravity dominates capillary forces. Conversely, if its radius R ≪ ℓc , the 2D drop (reps. 3D) forms a parabolic cap (reps. spherical) which forms a contact angle Θ with the substrate. The notions of wetting presented in the next section, such as contact angle and triple line 1 , appear naturally here. These notions of wetting and capillarity are important for understanding the shape taken by the free surface of a liquid in contact with a solid. This is particularly true for the formation of a meniscus on the liquid surface between two plates of a vertical solid, or the rise of water in a tube with a small radius and a hydrophilic wall (Jurin’s law). Therefore, surface tension and capillary forces between a fluid and another non-miscible phase influence the shape of the liquid interface. In the case of solid substrates, this results in wetting phenomena such as spreading of the liquid on the solid substrate, or the formation of a contact angle. [SOURCE: Page_48]

Spreading parameter [SOURCE: Page_48]

The spreading parameter S characterizes the spreading of a liquid on a surface. It expresses the difference between the surface energies of the dry substrate Edry substrate and the wet substrate E substrate , wet [SOURCE: Page_48]

substrate S = Edry          substrate − Ewet       .                                          (1.48) [SOURCE: Page_48]

If a drop is placed on a flat, smooth, homogeneous surface, it adapts its shape and spread to minimize its energy. This minimization results from competition between the interactions on each free surface: the surface tensions between the solid and the liquid γSL , the surface tensions between the solid and the gas γSG and the surface tensions between the liquid and the gas γLG or γ. Thus, the spreading parameter described in Eq.(1.48), can be rewritten, [SOURCE: Page_48]

S = γSG − (γSL + γ) .                                               (1.49) [SOURCE: Page_48]

This parameter expresses the spreading behavior of the liquid on the substrate and defines whether the substrate is wetting or non-wetting. When a drop is placed on a flat and hydrophilic surface, the drop spreads on the surface. Two types of spreading behaviour are distinguished: [SOURCE: Page_48]

• the drop spreads infinitely until it completely covers the surface, i.e. the surface energies of the dry substrate are dominant over the surface energies of the wet substrate, so the spreading parameter is positive (S > 0); [SOURCE: Page_48]

• the drop has a finite spread that does not cover the entire surface, i.e. the surface energies of the wetted substrate are dominant over the surface energies of the dry substrate, so the spread parameter is negative (S < 0). [SOURCE: Page_48]

Note that a liquid will spread completely if it is less polarizable than a solid. These two wetting regimes, represented by figure 1.13 are called complete wetting (S > 0) and partial wetting (S < 0). However, the spreading parameter is not sufficient to highlight the behavior of the liquid on non- wetting surfaces. On hydrophobic surfaces, the liquid withdrawal until the surface area of the wetted substrate is almost vanished. In this case, the substrate is considered as non-wetting. 1 The triple line is an imaginary line resulting from the interactions of the three phases at the wettability front. [SOURCE: Page_48]

Note that the theoretical criteria associated with the spreading parameter are related to the empir- ical criteria of Zisman [207, 135] who considers that any solid substrate has a critical surface tension γc : partial wetting (γ > γc ) or complete (γ < γc ). Therefore, considering that γc ∼ γSG − γSL , the definition of wetting given by De Gennes [92] is found: complete wetting (γ + γSL < γSG ) and partial wetting (γSL < γSG < γSL + γ). The medium is wettable if γSG > γSL else it is not wettable (γSL ≥ γSG ). However, this notion alone does not characterize all types of partial wetting, such as finite with- drawal (see Figure 1.14b). To represent these partial wettings, we need to define additional concepts. [SOURCE: Page_49]

Triple line and contact angle [SOURCE: Page_49]

The triple line is an imaginary line resulting from the interactions of the three phases at the wettability front. At the junction of these surface interactions, a Θ angle is generally defined as a contact angle. This angle represents the degree of wettability of the liquid on the substrate. Note that this triple line and contact angle evolve during the the drop spreads on the flat substrate. At equilibrium, Young [365, 318] defined a relation between the wettability front and the static contact angle Θe , γ cos Θe = γSG − γSL .                                    (1.50) [SOURCE: Page_49]

Young relation can be rewritten as follows in order to relate the static contact angle and the spreading parameter, S = γ (cos Θe − 1) .                                      (1.51) [SOURCE: Page_49]

A partial wetting is considered to have a hydrophilic tendency if the static contact angle satisfies the condition: 0 < Θe < π2 . Conversely, it is considered hydrophobic if π2 ≤ Θe < π. The notion of contact angle and spreading parameter are generally used for experimental charac- terization of substrate wettability, according to the following classification: [SOURCE: Page_50]

• hydrophilic substrate with complete wetting if S > 0 and Θe = 0; [SOURCE: Page_50]

• hydrophilic substrate with partial wetting if S < 0 and 0 < Θe < π2 ; [SOURCE: Page_50]

• hydrophobic substrate with partial wetting if S < 0 and π2 ≤ Θe < π; [SOURCE: Page_50]

• non-wetting substrate if S ≤ 0 and Θe = π. [SOURCE: Page_50]

1.3.2    Wettability in the soil [SOURCE: Page_50]

A porous medium has no continuous surface, unlike a flat substrate. Its surface is at least rough, and its geometry is highly complex. Wetting may therefore vary from the basic description given in section 1.3.1. De Gennes et al. [93] consider the "wetting of porous media" as being similar to the wetting of highly irregular surfaces, but with the component of imbibition. Thus a drop placed on a porous surface spreads and penetrates its support, whose modifies its wetting properties. A film deposited on a porous substrate is removed by two mechanisms: suction and dewetting. This domain is very large, covering the wetting of powders, rocks and soils, as well as the entire paper industry. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Capillary phenomena in soil [SOURCE: Page_50]

Porous media are formed by randomly distributed pores with complex geometries. They are often sim- plified by vertical capillaries of simple cross-section. In this case, the capillarity phenomena presented in section 1.3.1 occur. The phenomenon of capillarity is also present at the pore scale: wetting on a grain of sand, for example. On macroscopic scales, these phenomena are less evident without this ideal framework. In petroleum engineering literature, the Leverett J-function was introduced by Leverett [212] to describe the capillary pressure Pc [Pa] in a porous medium: s Pc (s)       k J(s) =                 .                                  (1.52) γ cos Θ       ϕ [SOURCE: Page_50]

De Gennes et al. [93] proved, by macroscopic reasoning, that the capillary height Hcap is defined in a porous medium such that γ cos(Θe )   ℓ2 Hcap ≈              = c cos(Θe ),                              (1.53) ρgr^0      r^0 q where ℓc is the capillary length, r^0 is the typical pore radius such that r^0 ≈ ϕk and Θe is the equilibrium contact angle of the liquid on the non-porous substrate. Thus, capillary rise in a porous medium is similar to Jurin law [189], but expressed with macroscopic parameters of the porous medium [212, 93]. [SOURCE: Page_50]

Characterization of soil wettability [SOURCE: Page_51]

The wettability measurement of a porous medium is generally based on the direct measure of the contact angle at the surface of the porous medium or at the pore scale on an aggregate. This measure is used to characterize the capillary pressure. At the pore and aggregate scale, measurement requires a tomography device and a representative pore volume. This type of measurement is useful in controlled microfluidic media [289, 311, 310] but is more difficult to define a representative pore volume in a natural environment. On a macroscopic scale, the contact angle is measured at the surface of the porous medium. The wettability of the porous medium is defined at its surface. Note also that the effective contact angle at the surface [92] is not always the same as that at the pore scale [303]. In addition, the contact angle value is affected by hysteresis phenomenon, surface irregularities and the imbibition of the droplet in the medium. Particular wettability models are required to understand these phenomena: wetting models on physically textured surfaces (Wenzel model [358]) or chemically textured surfaces (Cassis-Baxter model [69]). These models are used to describe drop infiltration at the pore scale [35]. In the literature on powdery media, an alternative method is used to remove the imbibition com- ponent. This involves applying a thin layer of substrate to the tape and measuring the contact angle. However, this method remains contestable: the wettability of the tape can influence the contact angle measured. Indirect measurement methods were developed to measure the contact angle at the surface of the medium: Molarity of Ethanol Droplet (MED) test [357, 194] and the Water Drop Penetration Time (WDPT) test [357]. These tests are based on measuring the infiltration time of a drop placed on the medium. More details on these tests are given in chapter 4. Results of these tests are related to contact angles and are more reproducible than direct measurements [188]. Regardless of the method used, we obtain wettability tendencies of the porous medium. Moreover, it is important to note that the wettability of a porous medium varies according to the amount of water in the medium [219]. As the water content decreases, the contact angle increases with the infiltration time in the porous medium. Therefore, a drier medium is more hydrophobic than a wet one. Muehl et al. [241] define the notion of subcritical hydrophobicity, which occurs on perfectly dry surfaces, for Θ < π2 . However, in a dry medium, water is confined to a void volume and does not diffuse as a thin film around grains or aggregates, so the hydrophobic character is strongly accentuated. Beltrame [19] considers that hydrophobic interactions manisfest at low saturation and when the medium saturates, it becomes hydrophilic again. This phenomenon can be observed using tomography equipment such as a confocal microscope [105] at pore scale, or drop infiltration measurements [162, 82, 73]. These angle variations are also impacted by the presence of amphiphilic matter. Chenu et al. [77] [SOURCE: Page_51]

have shown that the contact angle increases with the concentration of amphiphilic matter for a water droplet placed on soil fractions lower than 2 µm. Moreover, Kroener et al. [201, 198] have revealed the existence of a critical mucilage concentration defining a percolation threshold. Thus, wettability varies in the presence of organic matter. Whang and Wallach [354, 355] propose a model based on the time modification of the contact angle for a given concentration of amphiphilic matter. However, the models and measurements do not take into account the hydrophobic effect in the medium except by the contact angle. A porous medium must have hydrophobic interactions to generate partial wetting even if the medium is hydrophilic. The question is how wettability is represented in the porous medium? Hydrophobicity and, more generally, the physico-chemical properties of the soil influence flow in macropores, and generally favor preferential flow [105, 19] (and pollutant transport). Then how can parameters linked to wettability be measured and integrated into soils? In order to provide some answers to this question in the chapter 2, we propose to review the wettability energy approach on a flat substrate and the models existing in the literature to model it. [SOURCE: Page_52]

1.3.3    Wettability energies [SOURCE: Page_52]

Disjoining pressure in plan surface [SOURCE: Page_52]

Disjoining pressure is a pressure representing the interactions between two interfaces that are separated by a distance ζ and interact. As presented in section 1.3.1, surface tension results from the forces acting at the molecular scale of an interface. For a volume of liquid flowing over a wall, the solid/liquid and liquid/gas interfaces form a surface tension associated with the liquid. [SOURCE: Page_52]

(a)                                                       (b) [SOURCE: Page_52]

Figure 1.15: Sketch of of the evolution of the free energy density of an infinitesimal infinitesimal film slice as a function of thickness (a) γf ilm (ζ) = γSL + γ and (b) γf ilm (ζ) = γSG . [SOURCE: Page_52]

However, in the neighbourhood of the contact line, it becomes more difficult to separate the two interfaces zones. The principle of disjoining pressure does not require an accurate description of the interface. It is therefore not necessary to model the effects of the triple line on the flow. Consider two critical situations with a flat film of thickness ζ on a wall: [SOURCE: Page_52]

i. the film is thick, i.e. ζ is very large; [SOURCE: Page_52]

ii. the film is ultra-thin, i.e. ζ is practically non-existent. [SOURCE: Page_52]

The energies in these two situations are different: in case (i), the film surface energy is γSL + γ because the two interfaces zones are distinct from each other, whereas in case (ii), it is equivalent to the water [SOURCE: Page_52]

## 1.3. MODELING OF WETTABILITY AND APPLICATION TO THE SOIL

To model this change in interface behavior, Derjaguin and Churaev [97] consider the two critical situations as without thickness interfaces and introduce an energy V between the interfaces, which depends on the liquid thickness separating them. The surface energy associated with the flat water film can then be defined as a function of the film thickness: [SOURCE: Page_53]

γf ilm (ζ) = γSL + γ + V (ζ),                               (1.55) [SOURCE: Page_53]

where V is a surface energy representing the continuity between the two situations. Thus, V satisfies the following conditions: [SOURCE: Page_53]

• V (0) = γSG − γSL − γ = S; [SOURCE: Page_53]

• interfaces do not interact and limζ→+∞ V (ζ) = 0. [SOURCE: Page_53]

Note that when the interfaces interact, i.e. the two zones partially overlap, a non-zero V energy must be considered. Derjaguin and Churaev [97] and De Gennes [92] then introduce the notion of "disjoining pres- sure" Π(ζ) associated with this energy. They considered disjoining pressure to be the negative of the derivative of the surface energy function V , [SOURCE: Page_53]

dV Π=−         .                                       (1.56) dζ [SOURCE: Page_53]

The surface energy V is expressed as a function of the disjoining pressure for a thickness ζ, Z ∞ V (ζ) =         Π(e)de.                                  (1.57) ζ [SOURCE: Page_53]

The form of V and P i are used to express the wettability behavior between the two critical cases (i) and (ii). Different forms of function exist to represent the wetting of the drop on the solid [182]. [SOURCE: Page_53]

At the mesoscopic scale between the solid surface and the free water surface, partial wetting occurs when repulsive interactions exist at least at short range. A competition between attractive (e.g. Van der Walls) and repulsive interactions can then be considered, as indicated by the green (resp. red) arrows representing the attractive (resp. repulsive) interactions in Figure 1.16). Note that if V (ζ) decreases, the film will be thicker, and Π(ζ) is the pressure that must be applied to maintain the film at a thickness ζ. Otherwise, Π(ζ) is the depression that must be imposed on the film surface to prevent it from vanishing the film thickness. When the film thickness is zero, a singularity is observed at the triple line for partial wetting, as there is a discontinuity between an area of the dry solid substrate in contact with the drop. To avoid this problem, it is important to define a minimum film thickness that coexists 2 with thicker films. Lallement [203] proposes to set a thickness ζ ∗ which avoids the singularity. However, this condition is too restrictive on the residual thickness. Other authors [93, 190] propose to define a precursor film ζp allowing to have a family of stable film 3 (ζ ≤ ζp ) coexist with thicker films. In order to avoid spontaneous dewetting of this equilibrium film, the disjoining pressure and its energy must be adapted such that:                              ( V (ζp ) = γSG − γSL − γ, (1.58) Π(ζp ) = 0. [SOURCE: Page_54]

This results in the increased Young-Dupré relation, relating the notion of contact angle and the disjoining energy: V (ζp ) = S = γ (cos Θe − 1) .                            (1.59) [SOURCE: Page_54]

However, if there is not enough liquid to cover the entire surface for a film of thickness ζp , a drop of liquid will spread out completely. In this case, two flat films of different thickness may coexist. In this case, we assume that the wetting is pseudo-partial rather than partial (Figure 1.18). 2 Two films of thickness ζ1 and ζ2 coexist if and only if: 1. Π(ζ1 ) = Π(ζ2 ); 2. V (ζ2 ) = Π(ζ1 ) (ζ1 − ζ2 ) + V (ζ1 ). [SOURCE: Page_54]

3 A film of thickness ζ is stable if P (ζ) has a positive curvature (P ′′ (ζ) > 0) [SOURCE: Page_54]
