## 1.4. CONCLUSION

Figure 1.18: Sketch of pseudo-partial wetting. [SOURCE: Page_55]

Therefore, the notion of disjoining pressure makes it possible to define different wetting models: [SOURCE: Page_55]

• complete wetting if S > 0 and V decreasing at long-range; [SOURCE: Page_55]

• partial wetting if S < 0 and V increasing at long-range; [SOURCE: Page_55]

• pseudo-partial wetting if S > 0 and V increasing at long-range (Figure 1.17c). [SOURCE: Page_55]

Pseudo-partial wetting model [SOURCE: Page_55]

Pseudo-partial wetting is wetting where the coexistence of a droplet and an ultra-thin precursor film occurs [328]. Experimentally, these precursor films have been observed in specific cases [163]. The precursor film is used as the cut-off parameter required to avoid the triple-line singularity of a wetting front [33]. Thus, the advancing and receding angles (angle derived from surface defects) do not defined with this model. In recent years, this type of wetting model has been used to develop lubrication-type models based on the free energy of the system [271, 236, 257, 321]. These gradient-type models generally represent the dynamics of a drop on a substrate: bacterial biofilm [336, 337], liquid in the presence of surfactant [322] or on polymer brushes [323, 155, 165]. The question is how this type of modeling can be integrated into the soil? Cueto and Juanes [87, 86] by introducing a free energy per unit volume in the soil E as the sum of local potential energy (gravitational Egr and capillary pressure Ecap ) and non-local potential energy Enl [84]: [SOURCE: Page_55]

E = Egr + Ecap + Enl .                                    (1.60) [SOURCE: Page_55]

This energy purposed by Cueto and Juanes does not take into account wettability notion and physico- chemical interaction. An interesting idea could be to relate this energy to the disjoining pressure with the saturation distribution in the soil. Thus, this notion of energy in the soil deserves to be explored in more detail to generalize water transfer dynamics, particularly in the presence of amphiphilic matter. [SOURCE: Page_55]

1.4      Conclusion The main theoretical concepts relating to the effect of amphiphilic matter on the soil, capillarity and wetting are now defined. We have seen that the soil in the presence of amphiphilic matter includes the wetting mechanism using basic concepts such as surface tension and static contact angle. However, these concepts are insufficient to describe water transfer mechanisms, which become more complex in the presence of mucilage in the rhizosphere, for example. [SOURCE: Page_55]

## CHAPTER 1. THE BASICS OF SOIL WATER TRANSFER MODELS

The literature offers many possibilities for improving the representation of water transfers in soils in the presence of amphiphilic matter, but these remain open. The next two parts of this thesis are devoted to the modeling and numerical simulation of water transfers based on an energetic representation by analogy with the lubrication equation. An application will be realized to the rhizosphere in part III. [SOURCE: Page_56]

36/250 [SOURCE: Page_56]

Part II [SOURCE: Page_57]

Model and numerical scheme [SOURCE: Page_57]

37 [SOURCE: Page_57]

Chapter 2 [SOURCE: Page_59]

Wettability and free energy in soil [SOURCE: Page_59]

The aim of this chapter is to model water transfer in a soil in the presence of hydrophobic matter. We introduce a free energy associated to the transfer dynamics and we neglect inertial effects in the flow processes. This approach allows to build a model consistent with thermodynamics. One of the advances of this model is that the asymptotic dynamics are no longer unique, unlike current models. It is then possible to simulate hysteresis effects in soil flows. Finally, by modifying only the boundary conditions and the initial water content, we highlight water trapping phenomenon, fingering flows and intermittent-like dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_59]

This chapter is organised as follows. First, we describe the energy components in the soil in the presence of hydrophobic matter and define a free energy associated with these components in sec- tion 2.1. Water transfer modeling is presented in section 2.2 and its mathematical properties are described in sections 2.3-2.4. Finally, we present the water transfer simulation results from a published article (section 2.5). [SOURCE: Page_59]

## CHAPTER 2. WETTABILITY AND FREE ENERGY IN SOIL

2.1       Free energy in soil 2.1.1     Thermodynamic approach The concept of hydraulic potential in soil was first defined by Buckingham in 1907 [48]. He considered this potential to be the attractive energy present between soil and water: capillary potential. He suggests that this notion be used in conjunction with more general concepts such as thermodynamic potential or free energy. Later, Gardner [144] demonstrated how capillary potential depends on the volumetric water content θ. This concept of capillary potential is used to represent the "soil/hydraulic potential". Water in the soil interacts with certain force fields that influence the potential of free water and bound water (water adsorbed to solid matrix). Thus, the hydraulic potential replaces the arbitrary classifications of water in the soil (gravity water, available water content, capillary water) introduced at the beginning of soil physics [170]. Therefore, an energetic classification was set up based on the potential energy of water in the soil, for instance gravity potential and capillary potential. However, the term potential, as used in the literature on soils, has led to confusion because potential has the same physical dimension as pressure, which is not necessarily the thermodynamic potential. This confusion appears early with the experimental measurement which refers to the pressure and not the thermodynamic potential. A measuring system called the tensiometer was developed by Richards in 1931 [284] to measure this potential and define the retention curve used in Richards equation. However the retention curve refers to the pressure and not the thermodynamics potential. This confusion is source of ad hoc term (particularly in coupled system) because construction of the model is based on the pressure which is an intensive^1 variable whereas it should be based on extensive^2 variable as free energy. This leads to confusion between the Pc capillary pressure and the derivative of the free energy functional, which have the same physical dimension. Leverett [212] has shown that the negative of the capillary pressure is analogous to Lewis and Randall’s "partial free energy" [213] and Gibbs "chemical potential" [223]. Thus, the capillary pressure is related to the free energy of the water per unit of pore space F . The free energy of the water per unit of pore space f changes with the variation of water saturation s, [SOURCE: Page_60]

df = −Pc ds.                                                      (2.1) 1 An intensive property is a physical quantity whose value does not depend on the quantity of substance measured. 2 An extensive property is a physical quantity whose value is proportional to the size of the system it describes, or to the quantity of matter in the system. [SOURCE: Page_60]

## 2.1. FREE ENERGY IN SOIL

Therefore the difference in free energy of the water per unit of pore space between two saturation states s^1 and s^2 in the same volume is given by, Z s^2 ∆f = f^2 − f^1 = −          Pc ds.                             (2.2) s^1 [SOURCE: Page_61]

ﬂuid air                                                   air water water                                                 water        solid solid                                                 solid [SOURCE: Page_61]

(a)                                                      (b) [SOURCE: Page_61]

Figure 2.1: Panel (a) displays the volumes Vt , Vf and Vw . Vt is the elementary volume of soil. In this volume, the hashed region corresponds to the fluid: water or gas. Vw is the water volume included in Vw . Panel (b) displays the variation dVw of Vw . [SOURCE: Page_61]

Eq. (2.1) can be proven using a standard thermodynamic reasoning. Let us consider an elementary volume Vt of soil. The pore space is noted Vf which contains water volume Vw (Figure 2.1a). We aim to express the change in free energy when the volume of water has varied by dVw (Figure 2.1b). We assume all transformations are isothermal. Since the number of water molecules N varies, we consider the Landau free energy, also called grand potential J. We note, U the internal energy of water in the soil, T the temperature T , S the entropy and µ the chemical potential. We neglect the gas phase. By definition, the Landau free energy is J = U − T S − µN.                                     (2.3) [SOURCE: Page_61]

The variation of the Landau free energy during water exchange is [SOURCE: Page_61]

dJ = dU − SdT − T dS − µdN − N dµ.                                  (2.4) [SOURCE: Page_61]

Since exchange of water dN takes place at the same pressure and temperature, there is no variation in chemical potential: dµ = 0. Under the isotherm assumption, the Landau free energy reads: [SOURCE: Page_61]

dJ = dU − T dS − µdN.                                       (2.5) [SOURCE: Page_61]

From the first thermodynamics law, [SOURCE: Page_61]

dU = dW + dQ = −Pc dVw + T dS + µdN.                                  (2.6) [SOURCE: Page_61]

With the isotherm assumption, dJ = −Pc dVw .                                         (2.7) [SOURCE: Page_61]

As Vw = sVf then dVw = Vf ds, then the free energy per unit of pore space is [SOURCE: Page_61]

d(J/Vf ) = −Pc ds.                                       (2.8) [SOURCE: Page_61]

## CHAPTER 2. WETTABILITY AND FREE ENERGY IN SOIL

Considering the free energy of the whole soil assumed to be as a closed system (no water exchange), then the Landau free energy J is the Helmholz free energy F . Then, by identifying J/V f to the Helmholtz free energy per unit of pore space f in Eq. (2.8), we retrieve the relation Eq. (2.1). [SOURCE: Page_62]

In recent reviews, soil water potential is based on Gibbs free energy [369, 221, 372]. It contains different energy components depending on the processes studied (capillarity, gravity, . . . ). The termi- nology "total potential" is used to define this potential with the capillary pressure Pc . This definition is a source of confusion because the term potential refers to a thermodynamic state of a system, such as free energy. For instance, in Encyclopedia of Soils in the Environment, Tuller et al. [340] defines the total potential and its components as the thermodynamic potential. However, during the experimental application, this potential is identified as a measure of the capillary pressure! In 1960, Philip [270] already indicates that the total potential Φ in literature corresponds to the Gibbs df differential function. In our case, it will corresponds to − ds = Pc . The author points out that it may be important to use the "absolute" rather than the differential form of thermodynamic function to study the evolution between two states. This absolute form is defined for a small element of soil with a given volume water content as equal to "minus the work required, isothermally and reversibly, per unit quantity of water, to extract all the water from the soil and transform it into pure, free water at a particular reference level" [269, 170]. Namely, the absolute form corresponds to the free energy variation f^2 − f^1 defined in Eq. 2.2. [SOURCE: Page_62]

with the density free energy f [J.m−3 ] depending on (s, ∇s). In the rest of the manuscript, we will refer to the density free energy as the energy density. The variation of the free energy are given by the Euler operator Lf which is sometimes called the Lagrangian derivative of f . In physics, the expression Lf (s) is called the variational derivative of the functional F at s. Thus the variation of the free energy are Z δf δF(s, φ) =            δsd^3 x,    ∀δs := φ ∈ Cc∞ (Ω),                  (2.10) Ω δs [SOURCE: Page_62]

where Cc∞ (Ω) is the compact support of the domain Ω. This variational derivative of the functional F corresponds to distribution derivative, [SOURCE: Page_62]

δf δF(s, φ) =<           ,φ > .                             (2.11) δs [SOURCE: Page_62]

In the next sections, we describe the different contribution of free energy density f□ . We distinguish bulk energy from wetting and surface energy. [SOURCE: Page_64]

2.1.3    Bulk energy [SOURCE: Page_64]

Gravity [SOURCE: Page_64]

Gravity is a fundamental force in physics, corresponding to the mutual attraction between two bodies of non-zero mass. In soil, this energy attracts liquids to the depths of the medium. It contributes to the downward flow. This term depends on the z vertical coordinates. For an elementary ds saturation variation, ther elementary energy variation reads [SOURCE: Page_64]

dfgr = ρgzds.                                       (2.19) [SOURCE: Page_64]

Note that for the study of horizontal transfer, this term varies only slightly and is therefore neglected in relation to variations in capillary energy. [SOURCE: Page_64]

Compressed air [SOURCE: Page_64]

In a porous medium close to the saturation, it is common to observe that air is trapped in the soil structure. In particular, the air is surrounded by water in micro-pores (Figure 2.2). The contact line may be pinned due to contact angle hysteresis. In this case the air is trapped in cavities of the soil structure. If this volume remains trapped, then variations in saturation result in a variation in air pressure. The negative of this compression work can be classically interpreted as free energy noted fcomp . Since this trapping phenomenon essentially only occurs for saturation close to one, this term depends on the saturation and is non-zero in a range of values close to saturation. This term is also useful for numerical simulation, as it prevents the calculated saturation from exceeding unity. This allows us to successfully carry out numerical simulation of the phenomena of oversaturation at imbibition front and "infiltration refusals" in a porous layer. [SOURCE: Page_64]

2.1.4    Wettability Energy Although the notion of wettability appears through various phenomena reported in the literature, such as the penetration time of a drop placed on a soil or the emergence of preferential flow, this concept is not explicitly defined in literature. According to Beltrame [19], we propose the following definition: [SOURCE: Page_64]

## 2.1. FREE ENERGY IN SOIL

air                                    Pinning water solid [SOURCE: Page_65]

Figure 2.2: Sketch of air trapping in the soil at pore scale. Red dots represent pinned contact lines. [SOURCE: Page_65]

Definition 1. Soil wettability. The wettability energy is the free energy due to the interaction between the porous matrix and water free surface. Soil wettability refers to any property deriving from the wettability energy. [SOURCE: Page_65]

In soil literature, this interaction usually refers to capillarity. Since Einstein’s first paper in 1901 [117], it has been known that capillarity is due to interactions between a substrate and water. These interactions lead to a suction pressure and the capillary rise. Nevertheless, this only concerns attractive interactions and does not apply to hydrophobic matter. Before we propose an analytical form of this energy, we review the physico-chemical origins of these interactions. [SOURCE: Page_65]

Physico-chemical interactions [SOURCE: Page_65]

In general, polar groups are hydrophilic. This is particularly the case with oxygen, nitrogen and sulfur groups. Hydrophilic properties are also influenced by hydrogen bonds. Thus, in the presence of OH groups, generally present in EPS, there are attractive interactions via these groups. In contrast, unpolar groups are not attracted to water. In particular, long carbon chains CH$_3$ and rings (e.g. aromatic). [SOURCE: Page_65]

## CHAPTER 2. WETTABILITY AND FREE ENERGY IN SOIL

EPS are long carbon chains with OH groups, so they are amphiphilic. Depending on their geometry in space, the molecule will be more or less hydrophilic. Therefore attractive interaction depends on the composition and surface area of the porous matrix. In the case of water, its polar property is related to capillary action. This allows water to form hydrogen bonds using its partial charges. Water is a polar molecule due to the unequal sharing of electrons in the covalent bonds. Oxygen is more electro-negative and therefore brings the electrons closer to its nucleus. This creates a partial positive charge on the hydrogen atoms H and a partial negative charge on the oxygen atoms O. These partial charges allow water to form hydrogen bonds with other polar molecules. For example, water molecules will bind by their hydrogen atom to a particle containing chloride ions Cl− (anion negatively charged), while oxygen atoms will bind bond to a particle containing sodium ions Na+ (cation positively charged) (Figure 2.3). Therefore, water molecules adhere to the soil, they may be retained in the micro-pores of the soil. These interactions lead to attractive interaction in porous media. In contrast, the presence of organic matter can lead to repulsive interaction. Note that there are strongly hydrophobic interactions that form a coating on the surface of a solid, resulting in a non-wetting medium (e.g. TriMethylChloroSilane with sand or "magic sand" shown in Figure 2.4). [SOURCE: Page_66]

Figure 2.4: Photo of a drop on a sand with hydrophobic coating. [SOURCE: Page_66]

Modeling [SOURCE: Page_66]

In Brochard-Wyart et al. [41], substrate wettability is related to the different surfaces energies (see section 1.3). In a similar way, we can define a spreading parameter as the energy difference per aera unit S = Edry m − E m . Introducing the matrix surface tension γ and the γ wet                                             P           P L surface tension of a wetted porous matrix, then S = (γP − γP L ). The soil is wettable if S > 0, it means that the energy decrease when it is wetted. In this case, capillary rise is observed. This phenomenon is common in porous media such as sugar in coffee or water rising in a sponge (Figure 2.5). The wettability energy compensates the gravity potential energy allowing a rise.It is customary in the literature to compare the porous medium to Jurin tubes. The height of capillary rise in soil is comparable to tubes of mean radius rm such as [SOURCE: Page_66]

2(γP − γP L ) h =                   .                                   (2.20) ρgrm [SOURCE: Page_66]

## 2.1. FREE ENERGY IN SOIL

Figure 2.5: Sketch of a capillary rise in a porous medium. The green arrow represents the rise of the liquid in the medium. [SOURCE: Page_67]

Applying the Young equality: (γP − γP L ) = γ cos Θ, where γ is the surface tension of the liquid and Θ is static contact angle, the capillary rise then related to the contact static angle Θ. The drawback of this definition is that Θ is difficult to measure. Even if it could be measured for a substrate with the same physico-chemical properties, a Jurin tube does not behave in the same way as a porous medium. Some work has been carried out with corrugated tubes, but this is still far from reality in soils with very irregular, even fractal surfaces [267]. Finally, Jurin law does not take into account interactions between the free surface and the substrate. If the liquid film thickness ζ around the porous matrix is very thin, about a hundred nanometres then we have to take into account a ζ-dependent surface energy. In low saturated porous media, these thin films certainly make a non-negligible contribution to the wettability energy. Then, for an element volume of wetted porous medium, we assume, in a first approximation, that the energy depends on the water content. The pressure in the porous medium derived from the energy density noted fw (s) is [SOURCE: Page_67]

Definition 2. the wetting pressure defined as: [SOURCE: Page_67]

dfw Π(s) = − ds Assuming fw (1) = 0, we get the integrated expression of fw Z 1 fw (s) =         Π(s)ds s [SOURCE: Page_67]

This function is analogous to the disjoining pressure for substrate wettability; we have used the Π notation derived from Derjaguin. If we identify this pressure with the capillary pressure then it is a positive function and always decreasing. This implies that the energy fw (s) is decreasing ( df ds < 0) w [SOURCE: Page_67]

2 and convex ( ddsf^2w > 0). In the context of liquid on a substrate, it is known that such a wetting free energy corresponds to total wetting, i.e. total spreading of the water. Mathematically, the convexity of the free energy implies the uniqueness of the solution, so the only equilibrium in a homogeneous medium is a homogeneous saturation distribution. This corresponds to Richards equation case, where the soil will moisten as much as possible to minimize its wettability energy. Note that this precludes [SOURCE: Page_67]

the existence of preferential flows as a steady state. The presence of hydrophobic matter suggests that Π(s) cannot always be decreasing, since water is repelled. We therefore propose to write Π(s) as the sum of two functions: [SOURCE: Page_68]

Π(s) = Pc (s) + Pr (s)                                      (2.21) [SOURCE: Page_68]

the first term has the properties of capillary pressure: positive and decreasing. The second is negative and effectively represents repellent interactions. As for Pc (s), the repellent pressure Pr (s) vanishes near saturation. The wettability energy density writes [SOURCE: Page_68]

fw (s) = fc (s) + fr (s) [SOURCE: Page_68]

where                                             Z 1 fc (s) =         Pc (s)ds ≥ 0, s Z 1 fr (s) =         Pr (s)ds ≤ 0. s [SOURCE: Page_68]

The wetting energy density is then the sum of the attractive energy part by the positive and decreasing fc (s) function and the repellent energy part by the negative fr function. In very dry soils, residual water is observed experimentally. By analogy with pseudo-partial wetting with the existence of an ultra-thin film beyond the wetting line, we assume that for saturation close to zero, Π is a decreasing function, i.e. fc ≫ fr . In this case, we show in the next section that a residual saturation may remain, which we identify with the residual saturation discussed in the literature. For this reason, we have given the definition of saturation as the ratio of available volume to water volume and not the relationship s = θθ−θ       r s −θr , where θr and θs are the residual and saturated water contents. There is therefore a possible shift in our curves when comparing retention curves. The advantage of our modeling is that sr and then θr are derived from the free energy and are not additional ad hoc parameters. Note that we have introduced the repulsive term motivated by the presence of hydrophobic molecules. Nevertheless, the fact that the matrix is partially wetting, i.e. has an equilibrium contact angle Θ > 0 implies that the disjoining pressure of a liquid on a flat substrate is necessarily increasing over a range of film thicknesses. Thus, in the case where very thin films predominate around the porous matrix, i.e. for low saturation, the disjoining pressure increases with film thickness. This indicates the existence of repulsive interaction whenever wetting is partial. [SOURCE: Page_68]

2.1.5    Water free surface energy [SOURCE: Page_68]

In the previous section, interaction energy between the free surface and the matrix is taken into ac- count. However, the creation of a free surface also requires energy cost related to surface tension γ, regardless of interaction with the matrix. The energy cost to create the surface Af is γAf . Note, the air surface tension is negligible compared to the water surface tension. The energy density associated to this free surface is f∇ = γA Ω , where Af is the water free surface in the elementary volume Ω. In f [SOURCE: Page_68]

a continuous medium approach, it is difficult to estimate f∇ and, above all, to relate this surface to [SOURCE: Page_68]

In a first approxi- mation, they showed that surface energy depends not only on concentration but also on the square of concentration gradient called "gradient energy" [54]. Instead to define a sharp interface between two fluids, a "phase field" ϱ varying continuously represents the presence of each fluid. For instance, when ϱ = 0 the fluid is air while ϱ = 1 the fluid is water. Any value in between represents the diffuse interface. The gradient of ϱ is large only in the diffuse interface of width ϵ (Figure 2.6). It was proven that by minimizing the functional energy Z 1 loc Fϵ =       f (ϱ) + ϵ|∇ϱ|2 dΩ,                                (2.22) Ω ϵ [SOURCE: Page_69]

when ϵ → 0 (Γ−convergence), the phase field ϱ(x) tends towards to the equilibrium distribution [237]. The minimal energy (inf(F)) is then the surface free energy. Recently, this functional is used as com- putation tool to track the interface of fluid mixing [8]. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Figure 2.6: Surface tension in effective porous media. [SOURCE: Page_69]

Cueto applied the Cahn-Hilliard approach to porous media where saturation is the equivalent of con- centration in two-phase medium consisting of water and air. In other words, they make the assumption that the up-scaling at the elementary volume of porous medium has the same energy functional as the water/air interface. Then, the energy associated to the water/air interface expresses [SOURCE: Page_69]

1 f∇ = Γ|∇s|2 ,                                     (2.23) 2 with Γ is the macroscopic surface tension coefficient. This physical quantity Γ does not have the dimension of a surface tension (Energy per unit area) but its dimension is energy by unit length, i.e. a force in Newton. Note that in Cahn and Hilliard [54], the interface free energy depends also on the concentration c what suggests that we should add a local term f∇    loc (s) in porous media. In [SOURCE: Page_69]

the following, we associated the free surface energy only with the gradient term, called in Cueto and Juanes [86] non-local term since the local term can be included in the wetting free energy. Adding the f∇ energy to wetting free energy allows to raise the problem of ill-posed problem due to no convexity of fw as mentioned in Seppecher et al. [304]. The physical interpretation of mathematical regularizing role of f∇ is discussed later. Until Cueto-Juanes model, surface tension is not evoked in soil literature expected for wettability. expression is improved later in Beljadid et al. [17] to take into account the specificity of porous medium where the capillary energy interacts with the interface energy [17]: [SOURCE: Page_70]

f∇ ∝ fc (s) |∇s|2 .                                      (2.24) [SOURCE: Page_70]

While the |∇s|2 term proves crucial when instabilities are present (see Section 2.4), the improved ex- pression Eq. (2.24) does not seem to bring new insights into the dynamics. Furthermore, the expression Eq. (2.24) for the derived pressure requires square roots of fc , namely the wetting energy in our case. The latter can be negative in the case of highly hydrophobic media raising problem of its definition. Thus, in the present work, free surface terms will only be associated with the expression 2.23. It is remarkable that none of the gradient-type expressions have been taken up in the literature outside the authors since its publication in 2008. A first explanation is that, apart from front instabilities (see Section 2.4), it does not provide fundamentally new behavior in the context of the Darcy-Richards equation. If the action of this gradient term is primarily to homogenize, as with capillarity, it has prop- erties that cannot be described by simple capillarity. It prevents the formation of arbitrarily small or irregular structures. This is crucial during instabilities involving due to hydrophobic terms. Another, less obvious point is that it can generate flow front instability. It was the analogy with the viscosity instability of a falling film that motivated the introduction of this term by Cueto and Juanes [84]. [SOURCE: Page_70]

2.1.6    Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_70]

To conclude, the density energy f is the sum of four terms: wetting energy, compression energy, gravity energy and free surface energy. For convenience, in the rest of the manuscript, we include the compression energy term fcomp in a "total wettability" term still noted fw and called wetting energy. With these notations, the density free energy reads [SOURCE: Page_70]

f = fw + f∇ + fgr .                                      (2.25) [SOURCE: Page_70]

Regarding fw , we distinguish the attractive from repellent interaction: [SOURCE: Page_70]

fw = fc + frep + fcomp ,                                   (2.26) [SOURCE: Page_70]

where fc is called capillary energy and it is associated to attractive interaction. The repellent part is noted frep . Note that if frep ≡ 0, then dfdsw = −Pc where Pc has the same properties of capillary pressure presented in literature. The expression of total wetting energy fw depends on the model and is specified in section 2.2.4. [SOURCE: Page_70]

2.2      Water transfer model In this section, we present the development of a water transfer model based on the energy concepts mentioned in the previous section (Section 2.1). The mathematical model for a homogeneous medium is presented first, and then extended to heterogeneous media, in particular stratified media. [SOURCE: Page_70]

2.2.1   Homogeneous porous medium [SOURCE: Page_71]

In a homogeneous porous medium in the presence of hydrophobic matter, the water transfer in the medium Ω is modeled by describing its saturation profile s(x, t) [−] in a space x ∈ Ω during a finite time t [s]. The dynamics associated to water movement is governed by the continuity equation (con- servation law) of water density based on a hydraulic flow J (s) [m.s−1 ] (the s-conserving flux) such that, ∂s ϕ    = −∇ · J (s) = G(s),                                 (2.27) ∂t where the porosity ϕ [−] is constant in the space during all the study time. We propose a gradient dynamics model which only requires the knowledge of the free energy, the flux of an order parameter (saturation in our case) and a quadratic dissipation functional. In this section, we define the dimensionless disjoining pressure Πw given by Eq. (2.41), using the pressure associated to the components of the wetting density energies such that [SOURCE: Page_73]

2.2.2   Stratified porous medium A soil is a complex porous medium containing multiple heterogeneities. The thermodynamic model defined for a homogeneous medium, Eq. (2.40), is generalized to heterogeneous media. The dimensional formulation is a system of functions depending on space and time. In the case where the porosity is a constant in time then the system is given by:                                                         δF ϕ(x)∂t s = ∇ · K(s, x)∇        , δs (2.43)   δF = ∂s fw (s, x) − Γ(x)∆s − ρgz,  δs [SOURCE: Page_73]

where x = (x, y, z) are the spatial coordinates of the domain. In the rest of this section, porosity is assumed to uniform porosity in each heterogeneous zone over time in each zone of the medium, i.e. ϕ(x) = ϕ. A stratified medium is a medium made up of porous strata with different properties. In the most simple case, we consider two different strata in one dimension, where the transfer is vertical with S$_1$ = [z^1 , z^2 ] the domain of the first stratum and S$_2$ =]z^2 , z^3 ] the domain of the second stratum. The motion of the fluid in each stratum, according to Eq. (2.40), is governed by Stratum n°1: for all z ∈ S$_1$ , [SOURCE: Page_73]

ϕ(x)∂t s = ∇ · J$_1$ = ∇ · [K$_1$ (s)∇ (∂s fw^1 (s) − Γ1 ∆s − z)] ,               (2.44) [SOURCE: Page_73]

2.2.3   Boundaries conditions [SOURCE: Page_75]

The conditions on the boundary, called boundary conditions, are used to formulate the problem by imposing a physical situation corresponding to a phenomenon at the extremities of the domain. Thus system governed by Eq. (2.40) or Eq. (2.43) with an initial condition and boundary conditions forms a Cauchy problem. Let Ω denote our domain and ∂Ω denote the boundaries of the domain. The boundaries of the domain are taken to be a continuous boundary and on this boundary there is a condition on each unknown, i.e. the saturation s and the conservative energy functional δF δs . There are four types of boundary conditions that we present for a given variable u: the Dirichlet condition, the Neumann condition, the periodic condition and the flux condition (Robin-Fourier condition). Before to describe these conditions, the boundaries of the domain are considered to be expressed by the following partition: ∂Ω = ΓD ∪ ΓN ∪ ΓF ∪ ΓP .                                (2.52) [SOURCE: Page_75]

Each partition corresponds to a boundary condition for u: ΓD the Dirichlet condition, ΓN the Neumann condition, ΓF the flux condition and ΓP the periodic condition. Let g a real function depending on space. For all positive t and for all x ∈ ∂Ω, [SOURCE: Page_75]

2.2.4    Dimensionless water transfer in homogeneous medium [SOURCE: Page_76]

2.3     Theoretical results of homogeneous soil In this section, theoretical results are presented about the modeling in homogeneous medium with hydrophobic matter. Particular attention is devoted to the definition of wetting in the soil, which varies according to the water content in the medium, and its influence on the transfer behavior and equilibria resulting from this modeling. [SOURCE: Page_77]

2.3.1   Wetting pressure [SOURCE: Page_77]

The notion of wetting pressure is generally not defined in the soil. We propose here to compare the structure of the wetting energy density and pressure associate to the equivalent wetting pressure of two main model of the literature: Darcy-Richards [284] and Cueto-Juanes [85] model. In the Darcy-Richards model, the wetting pressure or the disjoining pressure Πw is represented by the capillary pressure Pc : Πw (s) = −Pc (s).                                      (2.63) [SOURCE: Page_77]

In the Cueto-Juanes [85] model, the pressure is constructed from free energy associated with cap- illarity fc and air compression fcomp . These components of the free energy are related to capillary pressure and compressed air pressure Pcomp , respectively. Then, we have the following equivalence with our model Πw (s) = Pc (s) + Pcomp (s).                            (2.64) [SOURCE: Page_77]

Therefore, the Darcy-Richards and the Cueto-Juanes model generate wetting pressures associated with decreasing wetting energies (Figure 2.8a). The monotonic behavior of pressure implies the exis- tence of a one-to-one relationship between pressure and saturation. These two wetting models only differ for saturation close to the saturation s = 1. The pressure defined by the Cueto-Juanes model decreases suddenly at negative pressures, whereas for the Darcy-Richards model the pressure remains positive. This difference defines an energy minimum close to saturation for the Cueto-Juanes model, [SOURCE: Page_77]

Linear instability means that an infinitesimal perturbation of the uniform profile leads to an exponential development of the amplitude of the perturbation. This phenomenon is similar to the dewetting phenomenon of thin film on a substrate. In our context, we expect coexistence of dry/wett soil regions. Assuming that in each dry and wet region the saturation is almost uniform then the saturation in dry, resp. wet, region correspond to smin , resp. smax , saturation discussed above. In the next section, we explain how to determine linear instability for equilibrium. [SOURCE: Page_81]

2.3.3    Linear stability of the uniform steady-state [SOURCE: Page_81]

In a homogeneous medium, due to the repulsive term hr , the uniform distribution of water s ≡ s^0 can be linearly unstable with respect to infinitesimal perturbation. The stability analysis was already carried out in the lubrication framework of a thin film, for instance Thiele [190], or in a similar framework in Beltrame chapter [19]. Let s^0 a fixed saturation of Eq. (2.70) such that s^0 satisfy Ġ(s^0 ) = 0 where G is defined in Eq. (2.27). The linear stability of this dynamic is given using the linearize expression of Eq. (2.58) with ϵ ≪ 1 yields. Let a small ϵ and u an integrable function in Ω such that s(x) = s^0 + ϵu. Inserting this expression in Eq. (2.58), for a fixed saturation s^0 , [SOURCE: Page_81]

ϵ∂t u = −∇ · (K(s^0 + ϵu)∇(Πw (s^0 + ϵu) + T ∆(s^0 + ϵu)).                     (2.73) [SOURCE: Page_81]

We linearize K and Πw thanks to Taylor expansion applied to s^0 with an error order of O(ϵ2 u^2 ). [SOURCE: Page_81]

K(s^0 + ϵu) = K(s^0 ) + ϵuDs K(s^0 ) + h.o.t.                          (2.74) [SOURCE: Page_81]

and Πw (s^0 + ϵu) = Πw (s^0 ) + ϵuDs Πw (s^0 ) + h.o.t.,                     (2.75) [SOURCE: Page_81]

where D is the differential operator. Thanks to Eqs. (2.74)-(2.75) and the fact that we have Eqs. (2.70)- (2.73) becomes, ∂t u = −∇ · [(K(s^0 ) + ϵuDs K(s^0 ))∇(uDs Πw (s^0 )) + T ∆u)]. [SOURCE: Page_81]

Thus, when ϵ goes to zero we have: [SOURCE: Page_81]

∂t u = −∇ · [K(s^0 )∇(uDs Πw (s^0 )) + T ∆u)].                         (2.76) [SOURCE: Page_81]

The eigenvalues of the linear operator on the right hand determines the linear stability. If all the eigenvalues are negative the steady-state is stable otherwise it is unstable. The determination of these eigenvalues requires numerical tools presented in the next chapter 3 except if s^0 is a uniform distribution. We focus, in the following, to this particular case to analyse the stability. [SOURCE: Page_81]

All perturbations with a wavelength above the critical value λ⋆c will develop exponentially. The resulting water distribution is a succession of dry and wet regions, the typical λ⋆m size of which is related to the mode of fastest growth rate. In this case, an inhomogeneous saturation distribution is expected. This equilibrium is similar to the pseudo-partial wetting where an ultra-thin precursor film coexists with drops or thicker films. Here, the low saturation in dry region plays the role of the precursor film. Note the importance of boundary conditions, since for domains smaller than λ⋆ the uniform distribution is stable. [SOURCE: Page_84]

2.4      Instabilities in stratified media Let us consider a vertical stratification of hydrophilic and hydrophobic layers as presented in sec- tion 2.2.2. According to experimental observation, water first infiltrates in the hydrophilic layer and then either stay trapped or for enough water content, or the water infiltrates into the hydrophobic layer via fingered [105, 307]. Since work in the seventies of [262] devoted to the study of the imbibi- tion front instability, this problem has been the subject of many studies in order to understand and model this phenomenon in homogeneous or heterogeneous media. However, this problem still remains an open question as shown by Fürst et al. [140]. It is known that the Richards equation is unable to reproduce this instability even by varying the capillary height in each stratum [100]. Indeed the asymp- totic dynamics is always a stationary flow where the water saturation is modulated by the heterogeneity. Many variants of the Richards equation, such as dynamic capillary pressure [168] and hypo-diffusion [119] have been developed to obtain out-of-equilibrium dynamics. However, the quantitative results are not in agreement with experiment, especially concerning the oversaturation observed at the infiltration front. Cueto-Felgueroso and Juanes [86] introduced the free surface energy discussed in section 2.1.5. The front instability then appears as a Saffman-Taylor type instability in homogeneous medium. Over- shoot of the saturation or oversaturation at the front appears as a consequence of the energy term |∇s|2 . The oversaturation induces a greater mobility, leading to fingering in a homogeneous porous medium. However, this model does not capture the instabilities of an initially blocked front, for instance, at the interface of a hydrophilic layer on a hydrophobic layer. One of the hypotheses of the link between hydrophobicity and fingering is the existence of the hys- teresis of the contact angle which is revealed through the hysteresis of the infiltration curves [168, 16]. Rätz and Schweizer [276] showed the existence of an unstable front due to a contact angle hysteresis [SOURCE: Page_84]

## 2.5. PUBLICATION OF THE MODEL

introduced directly into a Richards-type equation. Concretely, in order to advance the "charge" must pass a threshold such as a depinning phenomenon. In our model, at the imbibition front the repellent term are dominant and then a similar charge must pass a threshold arriving in the hydrophobic medium. Indeed, the governing equation of saturation is similar to the lubrication equation of the liquid bridge height driven by gravity on heterogeneous substrate stripped by periodic hydrophilic and hydrophobic coating. This configuration was deeply studied by Thiele and Knobloch [325, 324] then by Beltrame et al. [22] in 2D and 3D geometry. The case of liquid ridge blocked by an hydrophobic defect displayed very complex scenarios [21]. Under appropriate conditions the ridges may either depin and slide downslope as the slope is increased, or first break up into drops via a transverse instability, prior to depinning. The first instability is asso- ciated with the saddle-node bifurcation and is referred to in this context as the depinning instability. The resulting dynamics is an intermittent-like behavior, alternating a long period of blockage by the hydrophobic defect and relatively rapid flow after depinning. The second one is associated to a pitch- fork bifurcation and it is called the Rayleigh-Plateau instability. This instability corresponds to the development of front undulations. Both behaviors are reproduced by time integration and bifurcation analysis in the case of a stratified soil and presented in our publication [20]. At low water content, water is trapped in the hydrophilic layer. Above a critical water content, intermittent flow alternates between a long duration in the hydrophilic layer and rapid flow in the hydrophobic stratum. Prior this threshold, a Plateau-Rayleigh instability occurs, leading to the emergence of fingers, resulting from asymptotically stationary flow in the hydrophobic layer. The development of water distribution at the hydrophilic/hydrophobic interface is strongly reminiscent of the experiment description in Sililo and Tellam [307]. [SOURCE: Page_85]

2.4.1    Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_85]

This modeling based on a free energy is suitable for studying problems in both homogeneous and heterogeneous media. The model allows several equilibria to coexist, in contrast to the single state with the water transfer approaches presented in literature. Indeed some of these states of equilibrium cannot be reproduced by other models. It is difficult to follow and predict the dynamics exactly. Indeed, there is no usual analytical expression due to the non-linearity of the system. In this situation, it is important to approximate the time evolution of saturation using numerical simulation. More details about the numerical tools used to simulate the problem are presented in the chapter 3. [SOURCE: Page_85]

2.5      Publication of the model The work on the model and the simulation results presented in this chapter represent innovative work in the understanding of water transfer in a porous medium, especially in the presence of hydrophobic matter. These results were published in a letter of 7 pages (limit imposed by the journal) in the EPL-Europhysics Letters, in June 2022. Firstly, the publication strategy and the choice of this letter journal are discussed in section 2.5.1. A summary of the main results of this modeling in a homogeneous porous medium and in a stratified medium are presented in this article with some additional simulations in section 2.5.2. The preprint [SOURCE: Page_85]

2.5.1    Letter journal: EPL [SOURCE: Page_86]

The article has been accepted and published in the letter journal EPL, edited by IOPscience. This internationally peer-reviewed scientific journal publishes letters containing work of great interest and importance to one or more sections of the physics community, including Fluid and nonlinear dynamics. The main purpose of publishing in this journal is to introduce this water transfer model into the physics literature, particularly in the field of porous media with nonlinear dynamics. Once this publication is in the literature, it will be easier to develop future work in more specific and complex porous media such as soils. In particular, the variety of transfer dynamics reproduced by the model can be compared qualitatively with experiments in the literature. [SOURCE: Page_86]

2.5.2    Summary of the article [SOURCE: Page_86]

In the literature and in experiments in porous media, various water transfers occur, such as water trapping, fingering and uniform transfers when the medium is sufficiently saturated. Some of these phenomena are reproduced in mathematical models, as presented in section 2.2. However, current models are unable to reproduce all these phenomena in a same model. We return to the design of a wettability model based on the concept of free energy. This model is applied to a homogeneous medium and then to a stratified sandstone medium in the presence of a hydrophobic matter. Numerical simulations revealed the formation of partial wetting in the homogeneous medium, with a profile that varies according to the mean saturation. In addition, a potential hysteresis dynamic is highlighted by a bifurcation diagram (see section 3.5) whose parameter is the mean saturation of the water in the medium. These first results show the existence of multiple equilibrium states varying according to saturation between partial wetting and total wetting. In the stratified medium consisting of a hydrophilic stratum and a hydrophobic stratum repeated periodically, we revealed different transfer in 1D and 2D by varying only the mean saturation in the medium. In 1D, the numerical simulations captured: [SOURCE: Page_86]

• the dynamics of water trapping in the hydrophilic stratum at low saturation levels; [SOURCE: Page_86]

• the dynamics of intermittence in the hydrophobic stratum for saturation in a saturation hy- drophobic range (see section 2.3.1); [SOURCE: Page_86]

• the uniform transfer dynamics produced by the Darcy-Richard model with high saturation. [SOURCE: Page_86]

These results are confirmed in 2D where, in addition to the intermittence phenomenon, we have the formation of fingering in the hydrophobic stratum with a blocked wettability front in the hydrophilic stratum. This fingering is in agreement with the experiments of Nieber et al. [250] and Bauters et al. [15]. Figure 2.14 represents the different dynamics obtained by 2D simulations of the water transfer model in a porous medium. [SOURCE: Page_86]

This model is able to reproduce several water transfer phenomena by only changing the mean saturation of the water in the porous medium. These results are promising for a better understanding of water transfer in the presence of hydrophobic substances and for taking into account the hysteresis phenomena acting on the water in the medium. ## CHAPTER 2. WETTABILITY AND FREE ENERGY IN SOIL

Model of hydrophobic porous media applied to stratified media: Water trapping, intermittent flow and fingering instability [SOURCE: Page_88]

P. Beltrame^1 and F. Cajot^1,2 1 Avignon Université – UMR1114 EMMAH INRAE-AU - F84000 Avignon - FRANCE 2 INRAE - UMR1114 EMMAH INRAE-AU - F84000 Avignon - FRANCE [SOURCE: Page_88]

Abstract – A macroscopic model of water infiltration is developed and applied to a stratified porous medium containing hydrophilic and hydrophobic layers. The governing equation is a gra- dient dynamics as in the Richards model but in which a wettability term replaces the suction pressure, taking into account the effective action of the hydrophobic matter. The resulting gov- erning equation is a fourth order PDE similar to the lubrication model or Cahn-Hilliard equations known to reproduce complex dynamics such as intermittency and fingering instability. The model makes it possible to simulate the finite spreading of water in horizontal directions when the porous medium is hydrophobic. Applied to a stratified medium alternating hydrophilic and hydrophobic layers, we highlight, depending on the mean water content, phenomena of water trapping in hy- drophilic layers, intermittent flow and fingering instability. These regimes are studied using time integration bifurcation and numerical continuation tools. [SOURCE: Page_88]

## 2.6. CONCLUSION

2.6      Conclusion The water transfer model proposed in this chapter reproduces several water transfers in the presence of hydrophobic matter: water trapping, fingering and Darcy flow. Taking into account the free energy of the soil/water system improves our understanding of water transfer in porous media. In addition, taking repellent energy into account is fundamental for introducing the hysteresis phenomenon. During water transfer, there are multiple states of equilibrium varying according to the average saturation between partial wetting and total wetting. In other words, the dynamics change according to the average saturation of the soil, particularly during the wetting/drying cycle. This model offers a new way of representing water transfer in porous media in presence of hy- drophobic matter. Its general expression can be adapted to different porous media, such as soil in the presence of amphiphilic matter. This is a key point in the modeling, as in the soil physics. However, the promised results must be compared with experimental data, particularly in the presence of amphiphilic matter. It is important to define the model of the porous medium studied, i.e. to define the physical parameters of the model. In other words, we need to characterize the porous medium in the presence of an amphiphilic substance. This study is presented in the context of the rhizosphere in the part III. In the next chapter, we propose to present the numerical tool needed to simulate the water transfer present in this manuscript. [SOURCE: Page_95]

## CHAPTER 2. WETTABILITY AND FREE ENERGY IN SOIL

76/250 [SOURCE: Page_96]

Chapter 3 [SOURCE: Page_97]

Development and use of numerical tools to study water transfers [SOURCE: Page_97]

The aim of this chapter is to present the numerical models developed and used to study our fourth- order equation introduced in chapter 2. These modeling tools are used to define temporal solutions and to study dynamic behavior as a function of model parameters. These tools are fundamental in order to numerically analyzed the behavior of our fourth-order degenerate parabolic equation. Since analytical study is extremely difficult due to the strong non- linearity present in the equation, numerical methods are required to study water transfer in soil in the presence of amphiphilic matter. In this chapter, we introduce the basics of numerical modeling: to present the modeling difficulties for the same type of equation in the literature, and to justify the development of a model with the finite element method during this thesis (section 3.1). To facilitate the reader’s understanding of the developments, we present the principle of the finite element method in section 3.2 and the concept of spatial and temporal meshes in section 3.3. Then, we present and discuss algorithms developed to study the temporal dynamics of water transfer in a porous medium in section 3.4. Finally, we present bifurcation tools using continuation algorithm with the mean saturation as bifurcation parameter in section 3.5. 3.1      State of the art of the time integration 3.1.1   Numerical methods [SOURCE: Page_98]

Numerical methods are chosen and analyzed according to different mathematical properties: conver- gence, stability, consistency, etc. They enable the exact solution of continuous equations to be linked to the exact solution of discretized equations and to the numerical solution obtained. In addition to these mathematical criteria, there are computing criteria: CPU costs, accuracy, simulation time and memory used. In the field of applied mathematics, many mathematical tools are used to numerically approximate the solution of complex problems (wave dynamics, mechanics, thermodynamics, . . . ) modeled by par- tial differential equations (PDE). There are three main numerical methods for describing the solution of a partial differential equation from a continuous problem into an approximate discrete problem: Finite Difference [234], Finite Element [123, 225, 81, 46] and Finite Volume [99]. Finite difference methods are based on the development of Taylor series (derivative approximations). They are simple to construct, and form the basis of most industrial and research codes in computational fluid mechanics. Their limitation to simple geometries and the consideration of boundary conditions, in particular Neumann boundary conditions, are their main disadvantages. As a result, these methods are mainly used to study the time evolution of the numerical solution. As opposed to Finite Difference methods, Finite Volume and Finite Element methods are based on approximations of integrals. Finite Volume methods are based directly on the strong form of the equation to be solved. They evaluate the exact expressions of the mean value of the solution on a given volume. These data is used to construct approximations of the solution. Finite Element methods are based on a variational formulation of the equation which is considered as the weak form. These local approximations of the solution, using local data, form a global approx- imation by joining them together. Both methods can be easily applied to unstructured meshes, and are generally better suited to the numerical sapce treatment of the problems. Finite Volume methods have been developed for conservation laws and do not take into account the complexity of the mesh in discretizing conservation laws. However, the geometric characteristics of the mesh can influence the dynamics, for example when diffusive flows are considered. Conversely, Finite Element methods depend on the geometry of the domain and using mixed formulations based on mathematical theorems [47], they allow to take into account local and global physical dynamics [123]. [SOURCE: Page_98]

## 3.1. STATE OF THE ART OF THE TIME INTEGRATION

3.1.2    Numerical time schemes Numerical methods for solving problems are based on numerical schemes. There are two types of numerical scheme: [SOURCE: Page_99]

• Explicit schemes based on solutions at present and past times, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_99]

u̇ = f (un , un−p ),                                   (3.1) [SOURCE: Page_99]

with u the unknown, f a continuous function and un the solutions at each instant tn . Note that tn indicates the present time and tn−p , with n ≥ p > 0, are the past instants. [SOURCE: Page_99]

• Implicit schemes that are dependent on the future moment, i.e. [SOURCE: Page_99]

u̇ = f (un+1 , un−p ),                                  (3.2) [SOURCE: Page_99]

with un+1 the solution at each future instant and n ≥ p ≥ 0. [SOURCE: Page_99]

Implicit schemes are unconditionally stable in the linear case, i.e. they do not develop numerical instability except in the case of non-linearity. However, explicit schemes are numerically unstable if we do not impose a strong condition on the time step ∆t (time duration) similar to the Neumann’s stability criterion [203]. Examples of single-step and multi-step numerical schemes are given in Appendix A. [SOURCE: Page_99]

3.1.3    Numerical problem statement Eq. (2.40) defined in chapter 2, can be written, [SOURCE: Page_99]

ϕ∂t s = −∇ · [K(s)∇ (Πw (s) + Γ∆s + ρgz)] = F (s, x).                        (3.3) [SOURCE: Page_99]

This fourth-order partial differential equation is a degenerate parabolic equation [158] representing water transfer in a porous medium. It is similar to the lubrication [257, 236], Cahn-Hilliard [349, 152] or Kuramoto-Sivashinsky [193] equations. The strong non-linearity of these equations makes it impossible to define an analytical solution. Therefore, we need to develop numerical methods to solve this problem numerically. The Navier-Stokes equations, classically used in fluid mechanics, are solved numerically using nu- merical methods. However, these equations are of second order, whereas our equation is strongly non-linear and of fourth order, i.e it is more difficult to design numerical methods to simulate the physical behavior of the liquid. Tuckerman et al [339] has shown that numerical instabilities arise from higher-degree differential operators. For example, for Navier-Stokes equations, it’s the Laplacian, and for lubrication equations, it is the Bilaplacian [22]. Both are diffusion-related operators. Tuckerman et al [339] proposed to work with a semi-implicit scheme separating the linear opera- tor L from the nonlinear N part, i.e. F (s, x) = Ls + N(s, x). This scheme is stable if L is the Jacobian matrix J = Js$_0$ = Ds F (s^0 , x). In order to speed up linear system resolution in the semi-implicit method, it is convenient to take for linear part the higher degree differential operator instead of J. [SOURCE: Page_99]

## CHAPTER 3. DEVELOPMENT AND USE OF NUMERICAL TOOLS TO STUDY WATER TRANSFERS

This operator contains very large negative values as the the eigenvalues of J. Treating this operator implicitly will ensure the stability. Applied to the case of the Navier-Stokes equation, the linear oper- ator L is the Laplacian operator, and for the lubrication and for the Cahn-Hillard equations, L is the Bilaplacian operator. [SOURCE: Page_100]

Nevertheless, Beltrame and Thiele [22] have shown that this method does not succeed for the lubrication equation whenever wetting is partial, due to large variations in the disjoinning pressure in the neighbourhood of the wetting front. It is important to treat implicitly the whole linear part, i.e. J, and not only the Bilaplacian. However, solving linear system involving the JAcobina matrix raise new problems. Moreovre there is no general efficient method to perform the inversion. They propose to use an exponential propagation time integration scheme [137, 175, 331] combined with a Cayley-Krylov projection [359] and an incomplete LU decomposition. This method has proved stable and allows the application of an adaptive time step [36, 22]. However, this numerical development, implemented in finite difference, depends on an arbitrary parameter during the Cayley-Krylov projection, which influences the simulation time. If this parameter is not adapted to the problem, it can also lead to instability due to spurious modes. Finally, Finite Difference method is not suitable to adaptive mesh. At the wetting front, a variable mesh size is crucial to avoid numerical instabilities. A Finite Element approach would be better for the spatial discretization. In the literature, other Finite Difference numerical schemes have been proposed to integrate this type of equation: Chebychev pseudo-spectral method [85] or Positivity-Preserving Numerical Schemes [373]. However, the Chebychev pseudo-spectral method proposed by Cueto and Juanes [85] does not take into account a non-monotonic disjoinning function at the origin of numerical instability, and the Positivity- Preserving Numerical Schemes proposed by Zhornitskaya and Bertozzi [373] does not take into account a disjoinning term. As a result, these models do not suffer from the problems mentioned above for the development proposed by Beltrame and Thiele. The study of numerical schemes without the disjoining term allowed to develop a new numerical method for solving Eq. (3.3). At the same time as Zhornitskaya and Bertozzi, Grün and Rumpf [160] proposed a similar version of Positivity-Preserving Numerical Schemes by considering a Finite Element scheme. Their scheme is based on entropy estimation (in one space dimension [26, 29], in multiple dimensions [263]). They based their scheme on conservation of the positivity of the discrete solu- tion using an entropy formulation. When Πw ≡ 0 and gr = 0, Eq. (3.3) admits the existence and non-negativity of solution (one space dimension [28] and higher space dimensions [158, 120]). These models are considered as precursor notions for stronger regularity results as well as results on finite propagation velocity (in one space dimension [27], in several space dimensions [30]) or on a waiting time phenomenon [264]. Historically, Barrett et al are pioneers of a finite element scheme guaranteeing non-negativity of solutions (for the case Πw ≡ 0 [11] and in the context of the Cahn-Hilliard equation [12]). At each time step, they solve a second-order elliptic variational inequality by imposing non-negative solutions. However, this solution is not necessarily as regular as the limit of discrete solutions obtained in [160], as the discrete Laplacian operator of the discrete solution is not controlled. Later, Grün and Rumpf [161] adapted this numerical scheme to Eq. (3.3). The addition of this strong non-linear term introduces numerical instabilities. Grün and Rumpf propose to solve this [SOURCE: Page_100]

## 3.1. STATE OF THE ART OF THE TIME INTEGRATION

problem by decomposing the disjoinning function Πw such that [SOURCE: Page_101]

− Πw = Π+ w + Πw ,                                          (3.4) [SOURCE: Page_101]

where Π−w is the destabilizing function and Πw is the stabilizing function. They propose to explicitly + [SOURCE: Page_101]

treat only the destabilizing function Π− w . This strategy is similar to the method used for discretizing nonlinear source terms in semi-linear second-order parabolic equations [121]. Note that the primitive of the destabilizing function is concave, while the primitive of the stabilizing function is convex and non-negative. Naturally, this remark applies to the free-energy density presented in chapter 2. Thus, their numerical scheme achieves a strong convergence [159] in 1D of the discrete solution and simulation of dewetting in agreement with physical experiments [161]. Note that the method developed by Grün and Rumpf is efficient, but requires a good mesh to limit the degeneracy of the solution due to mobility. [SOURCE: Page_101]

3.1.4   My approach We opt for the development on a Finite Element method to numerically solve the equations (2.40a) and (2.40b). This choice is based on the fact that this method is supported by mathematical theories such as the concepts and properties of functional analysis, such as Hilbert and Sobolev spaces [47, 46] and the spatial adaptive meshing criteria. Moreover, the non-linearity of the problem makes it very difficult to find a strong solution directly, then that justifies to not use the method of Finite Volumes and Finite Differences. Instead, we will use a time scheme based on Finite Difference methods such as multi-step methods (Appendix A). Note that we need to develop this numerical model in a high-performance software program that allows to use finite element methods. We have therefore studied several software options: FreeFem++, Matlab, COMSOL or Vsoil. One of the main issues is the software’s handling of non-linearities. For this reason, we quickly excluded COMSOL and FreeFem++. The commercial software COMSOL does not handle the non- linear form of our equations, and it is very expensive. In the case of FreeFem++, the advantage is that it is based on finite elements and it is free, but the implementation of the non-linear parts was not intuitive. Then we considered an implementation using the Vsoil software developed at UMR EMMAH, but the development was limited to 1D simulations and did not allow the development of adaptive meshes. Therefore, it was decided to use Matlab. This payware was used to develop Beltrame and Thiele [22] Finite Difference method. Moreover, code for post-processing of data is easily implemented for analysis of results with this software. It should be noted that the FEM Operations Toolbox is available for a fee. However, the tools available in the toolbox are not always suited to our non-linear elementary matrices. Additional numerical methods had to be developed for the numerical resolution of Eq.(2.40a) and (2.40b). We will return later in the section 3.4 to the development of a more powerful code in particular developed by Hartmann and Thiele [323, 165] on similar equations. This development is implemented in C++ using the oomph-lib [169] library developed and maintained by Matthias Heil and Andrew Hazel (School of Mathematics, Oxford Road, University of Manchester). We propose to review some of the basics of finite element methods (section 3.2) and spatial and [SOURCE: Page_101]

3.2      Finite element method The finite element method is a numerical method for solving partial differential equations with a weak or variational formulation in a discrete domain Ωk ⊂ Ω, where Ω is a domain of RN representing the soil studied. An algorithm is implemented to define an approximate solution of this PDE on a compact domain with conditions on the edges and/or in the compact interior. [SOURCE: Page_102]

3.2.1   Weak formulation [SOURCE: Page_102]

A statement of a physical problem governed by PDE, generally expressed in strong form, is written in weak form, called weak formulation or variational formulation. Let define the mathematical space needed to express this formulation. Let Ω the soil studied without boundaries and Ω the whole soil studied during a period of time [0, T], with T ∈ R. (2.58) is related to the following weak formulation:                            δF   Find s,          ∈ V$_1$ × V$_2$ such that,              δs   Z          Z                   Z δF                 δF ∂t sv =      K(s)∂n     v−      K(s)∇      ∇v                              (3.5)    Ω           ∂Ω         δs       Ω         δs   Z           Z            Z             Z           Z      δF          w  =     ∂ f s w w −      Γ∂n sw +     Γ∇s∇w −     ρgzw, Ω δs         Ω            ∂Ω            Ω           Ω [SOURCE: Page_102]

where v and w are test function. This formulation, based on properties of functional analysis, is used to approximate the solutions of the problem in weak form. To simulate this model, we need to determine a discrete problem. [SOURCE: Page_102]

3.2.2   Galerkin method [SOURCE: Page_102]

In numerical analysis, Galerkin methods are a class of methods for transforming a continuous into a discrete problem. The continuous problem is given by the abstract formulation: ( Find u ∈ V such that (3.6) a(u, w) = f (w),   ∀w ∈ W, [SOURCE: Page_102]

where V and W are Sobolev spaces, a is a bilinear form define on V × W and f a linear form on W . Note that a and f are approximated to transform them into linear form if they are non-linear. [SOURCE: Page_102]

## 3.2. FINITE ELEMENT METHOD

Because the problem Eq. (3.5) is a non-linear system, its linear formulation is required in order to define the approximated solution uh : ( Find uh ∈ Vh such that (3.7) ah (uh , wh ) = fh (wh ),   ∀wh ∈ Wh , [SOURCE: Page_103]

where Vh ⊂ V and Wh ⊂ W are Sobolev subspaces, ah is a bilinear form defined on Vh × Wh and fh a linear form on Wh . [SOURCE: Page_103]

In our case, the abstract formulation is defined by linearising the non-linear functions K and ∂s fw . Once the system is linearised as in section 2.3.3, Galerkin’s method is used to approximate the solution u by a solution uh using a discrete linear system. Therefore, the subspaces of our studied Sobolev spaces must be defined. [SOURCE: Page_103]

Let N and M denote respectively the dimensions of the subspaces Vh and Wh , i.e. N = dim Vh and M = dim Wh . In other words, the domain is discretised into a mesh of N elements in space Vh (section 3.3.1). The families of vectors {φ1 , . . . , φN } and {Φ1 , . . . , ΦM } are the base elements of Vh and Wh respectively. The approximate solution uh is then decomposed onto each element from the basis Vh , XN uh =          Ui φi .                                        (3.8) i=1 [SOURCE: Page_103]

This decomposition associates to the discrete weak formulation a matrix system, derived from linear algebra, such that A(U − )U = b(U − ),                                  (3.9) [SOURCE: Page_103]

where U − is a vector known from our linearized problem. [SOURCE: Page_103]

The elements of the matrix A are given by Aij = ah (φi , Φj ) with i ∈ {1, . . . , N } and j ∈ {1, . . . , M }, the unknown is given by U ∈ RN and the RHS by b ∈ RM with bi = fh (Φi ). [SOURCE: Page_103]

However, the matrix form obtained depends on the time scheme used to approximate the continuous system (Appendix B). In addition, the elements used to define these matrices influence the formulation of the matrix system. Here we choose to use Lagrangian elements to represent functions of the form φi = Φi for all i ∈ 1, . . . , N with M = N . In other words, we use polynomial elements and resolve a matrix system with a square matrix A. [SOURCE: Page_103]

In our problem, Lagrange polynomials P$_1$ are at least used to have a correct approximation of the weak solution. These elements are linear function as displays in Figure 3.1. In our development, we propose to use Lagrange elements P$_2$ to obtain a higher accuracy of the weak solution without drastically increasing the computation time. The higher the degree of the Lagrange elements is chosen, the greater the construction time and complexity of the global matrices are. This criterion influences the time taken to solve the problem at each time iteration. [SOURCE: Page_103]

A weak solution is approximated numerically using the finite element method on each element of the discrete domain. A discrete solution needs to be defined using a method for discretizing the unknowns. [SOURCE: Page_103]

3.3      Meshing Meshing is an important element in the study of physical behavior, particularly when it is governed by a partial differential equation, as demonstrated in section 3.2 with the use of Galerkin method. It represents the decomposition of a continuous spatial or temporal domain into a discrete domain. In this section, we assume that spatial meshing 3.3.1 is associated with the finite element method and temporal meshing 3.3.2 with the finite difference method. [SOURCE: Page_104]

3.3.1    Spatial mesh A spatial mesh is the discretization of a continuous space by finite and similar elements. The same category of geometric shape is used, but the proportions may be different. Elements are generally found in the form of a segment in 1D, or in the form of a quadrilateral or triangle in 2D. This discretization must respect certain mathematical rules to be well defined. Let a set Ω and {Tk }, with k ∈ {0, 1, . . . , m − 1}, a family of geometric form of m elements such that the discrete domain Ωk is expressed in the form Ωk = ∪m−1 k=0 Tk .                                   (3.10) [SOURCE: Page_104]

This discrete domain is a conformal mesh if two elements Tk and Tj with k ̸= j and k, j ∈ {0, 1, . . . , m − 1} satisfy one of this criteria: [SOURCE: Page_104]

• Tk and Tj are disjoint, i.e Tk ∩ Tj = ∅; [SOURCE: Page_104]

• the intersection of these elements is a common edge or vertex. [SOURCE: Page_104]

Figure 3.2 shown some examples of meshes conformal and non-conformal for a rectangular set. [SOURCE: Page_104]

## 3.3. MESHING

Conformal mesh                     Non-conformal mesh [SOURCE: Page_105]

1D segment [SOURCE: Page_105]

2D triangle [SOURCE: Page_105]

2D quadrilateral [SOURCE: Page_105]

Figure 3.2: Meshing in a rectangular set [SOURCE: Page_105]

These conformal meshes correspond to a tiling of the studied domain. The vertices of the elements of our tiling correspond to the nodes of the mesh. They define the discretization points that allow the continuous problem to be simplified into a discrete problem, as mentioned in section 3.2.2. Note that the approximate solution of the initial continuous problem is calculated at each mesh node. This discrete space can be associated, in our case study, with the experimental measurements obtained locally (in 1D) or in a detection zone (in higher dimension) by sensors in the soil. We can distinguish two types of node configuration, i.e. two categories of mesh: [SOURCE: Page_105]

• uniform meshes, consisting of a single geometric shape, i.e. element of the mesh has the same geometric shape; [SOURCE: Page_105]

• non-uniform meshes, made up of a family of geometric shapes in which at least one shape in the mesh has different proportions to another element. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Uniform mesh [SOURCE: Page_105]

In the case of a uniform mesh, the link between the numerical mesh and the experiment is facilitated because we can place our sensors at constant intervals. However, in the study of the dynamics of water in a soil, this spacing can be a source of error in the numerical resolution. If the element size is too large, we risk to miss a precursor behaviour of the dynamics and to move the simulated dynamics far away from the experimentally observed dynamics. If the element size is too small, the computation time during increases significantly and due to the multiple approximations of the simulated dynamics errors are generated. Furthermore, from an experimental point of view, this represents a very high material cost, which is not always possible to set up in the field. Therefore, the uniform mesh is not the best choice for non-linear problem. [SOURCE: Page_105]

Non-uniform mesh [SOURCE: Page_106]

The aim of non-uniform meshing is to strategically place the nodes of the mesh to better capture the behaviour of water in the soil. To achieve this, it is necessary to adapt their location using mathematical tools such as error estimator. These tools make it possible to estimate a priori the error made on each mesh element. This estimation attempts to adapt the error in the mesh using a posteriori estimation error [347, 350, 123]. An algorithm is implemented to optimise the a posteriori error on each element and improve the mesh. This algorithm consists of 4 main steps: • step 1: construct an uniform mesh of N = 8 elements; [SOURCE: Page_106]

• step 2: estimate the a priori error on each element; [SOURCE: Page_106]

• step 3: adapt the mesh to optimise the error on each element; [SOURCE: Page_106]

• step 4: repeat steps 2 and 3 until to obtain a suitable mesh within the limit of niter = 8 remeshing. We propose to use the Z$_2$ error estimator in step 2 [378, 377]. This estimator evaluates the ϵ er- ror committed with respect to the approximated solution on each element. The error is considered acceptable if it satisfies the following criterion: [SOURCE: Page_106]

10−5 ≤ ϵ ≤ 10−3 .                                       (3.11) [SOURCE: Page_106]

In the case where the error on an element does not satisfy the criterion, the mesh elements must be adapted in order to satisfy the criterion. Figure 3.3 displays the mesh adaption. If the error is too small, the element is unrefined to reduce the resolution time and accuracy (Figure 3.3a). If the error is large, then the element is refined to reduce the error (Figure 3.3b). [SOURCE: Page_106]

(a)                                                        (b) [SOURCE: Page_106]

Figure 3.3: (a) Unrefined and (b) refined spatial mesh with triangle elements. [SOURCE: Page_106]

By analogy with an experimental device, the new node position is not necessarily at the same position as the sensor initially placed on a sample. But it does give a better estimate of the numerical solution. Therefore, a non-uniform mesh provides a better reproduction of the dynamics, but is more difficult to compare with experience at a given position and time. The mesh defined at a time tn is not necessarily adapted to the dynamics at the next time. It is important to be able to adapt the spatial mesh to the dynamics during the time evolution. However, the same approach as explained above should not be applied. Repeated adaptation of the domain discretization increases the calculation time and introduces numerical noise that can disturb the system. To limit these problems, we propose to generate an optimal mesh during the initialization of the problem by applying the algorithm describe before, then to limit the adaptation to the maximum per time iteration, niter = 1. [SOURCE: Page_106]
