## 3.3. MESHING

Spatial meshes synthesis [SOURCE: Page_107]

Two types of spatial mesh can be distinguished: uniform meshes and non-uniform meshes. Uniform meshes are easily developed and the comparison of numerical data with experimental data is natural. However, they do not evolve over time, i.e. they depend on the choice of initial discretization, which can affect simulated behavior when the phenomenon is complex. Non-uniform meshes, on the other hand, reproduce more accurately the numerical behavior of the phenomena simulated. However, their development and their comparison with experimental data is more difficult than uniform meshes. Therefore, the spatial mesh is an important element in the estimation of the numerical solution, but the type of mesh chosen depends on the constraints we require and the behavior studied. [SOURCE: Page_107]

3.3.2   Time mesh A time mesh is a discretization of time by discretization points tn , for all n ∈ N. Each discretization point is separated by a time interval which we call the discretization step dtn , where dtn is the space between the time at instant tn and the following instant tn+1 , i.e dtn = tn+1 − tn . As with spatial meshing, there are two categories of time meshing: [SOURCE: Page_107]

• uniform meshes where the discretization step is constant during the entire study, i.e. for all n ∈ N, dtn = dt; [SOURCE: Page_107]

• non-uniform meshes, where at least one discretization step varies during the study, i.e. there is at least one step between time tm+1 and tm , such that dtm ̸= dtn , for all n, m ∈ N and n ̸= m. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Uniform mesh [SOURCE: Page_107]

Uniform meshes are easy to set up numerically. Once the discretization step is defined, the time interval is constant. This time division ensures that exact time comparisons can be made between numerical output and experimental data measured at constant time intervals. However, the choice of discretization step must be selected strategically. In approximating the behaviour of the dynamics, the discretization step can be a source of loss of information about the transfer in the soil. If dt is very large, then there is a risk of changing the dynamics under study, as a fundamental behaviour of the dynamics would be missed. If dt is too small, the number of iterations increases, which increases the calculation time and the error of the approximate solution due to the time scheme. These increase can affect dramatically the solution. Since the dynamics of water in the soil contain regimes on different time scales, it is not appropriate to choose a uniform time mesh, but rather to adopt a mesh that adapts to the dynamics, such as a non-uniform mesh. [SOURCE: Page_107]

Non-uniform mesh [SOURCE: Page_107]

Non-uniform mesh is associated to time adaptation which is based on the estimation and correction of the time discretization step. Figure 3.4 displays the diagram associated to the time step resolution. We consider a profile of the dynamics at a time tn and try to determine the profile at a time tn+1 such that tn+1 = tn + dtn . We assume that we have determined a time step dtn at the previous instant tn−1 . We try to solve the problem on the discrete domain at time tn+1 , using an explicit numerical [SOURCE: Page_107]

The two methods have the same management when the resolution algorithm diverges. Since we have not succeeded in defining a profile at time tn+1 , i.e. the step dtn given initially does not allow us to converge, we need to correct it. We take the profile defined at time tn and repeat the resolution to determine the profile at time tn+1 by halving the time discretization step previously used, i.e. dtn = dt^2n . In the case where the resolution algorithm converges, we have succeeded in defining the profile of the dynamics at time tn+1 . We try to predict the future discretization step dtn+1 by means of an estimate. The constant prediction method used in the Vsoil software [202] and other software suggest considering that the future discretization step increase with respect to a constant. Generally, this constant is selected at 10% as shown in Figure 3.5. This method adapts the time mesh but requires multiple iterations to define a time instant if the constant is not correctly chosen. Figure 3.6 displays the diagram of the discretization time step estimation using the prediction- correction method. The prediction-correction method is a numerical method for estimating the dis- cretization step using mathematical criteria. Since the estimation of the time evolution of water in the [SOURCE: Page_108]

soil is governed by a numerical scheme, an error in the approximation of the solution is committed. This error is called the Local Truncation Error (LTE), denoted by dn . To maintain the stability of the numerical scheme through time, we have chosen to use an implicit scheme. These schemes, known for their unconditional stability, commit an LTE of order k. In other words, the LTE is written as a (k) function depending on the k th derivative of the variable u at a time tn , un . This error is expressed as a function of the difference between the approximate solution defined by the implicit scheme un+1 and an explicit scheme of the same order uPn+1 . For example, an implicit Euler scheme (BDF$_1$) with an explicit Euler scheme, a second order of the Backward differentiation formulas (BDF$_2$ scheme) with a Leapfrog scheme or a Crank-Nicolson scheme/Trapezoid rule with a second order of the Adams–Bashforth meth- ods (AB$_2$ scheme). More details are given in Appendix A. This trick makes it possible to express the LTE without depending on the derivatives of u and therefore without the need of a new numerical approximation. The Root-Mean-Square Error (RMSE) norm of the LTE on each element of the spatial mesh is used with the convergence tolerance ϵ of the resolution algorithm to define the predicted time step dtc [317] (Appendix A),               1 ϵ       k+1 dtc =                        . ||dn || As with the spatial mesh, we introduce a bounding criteria associated with our adaptation term estimate dtc : 0.8 ≤ dtc ≤ 4. dtn+1 = dtc dtn .                                       (3.13) [SOURCE: Page_109]

• if the estimate is below the criteria, the rate of increase in the discretization step is set at the lower limit of the bounding set, i.e. dtn+1 = 0.8dtn . This choice prevents the predicted time step from being reduced too much; [SOURCE: Page_109]

Time meshes synthesis [SOURCE: Page_110]

As for spatial meshes, there are two types of temporal mesh: uniform and non-uniform. Uniform meshes are easily implemented in numerical methods. Note that experimental measure- ments of a physical phenomenon are generally performed at constant time intervals. Thus, the com- parison between experimental and simulated data is well adapted to uniform meshes. However, this type of mesh is less suited to dynamics with time change. However, the dynamic is better fitted with non-uniform meshes. They allow to define a faster resolution method for a final dynamic time, which reduces simulation time and the number of calculus iterations required. As a result, the error of the solution after several iterations will be lower than for a uniform mesh. However, the development of this method requires more implementation work and the selection of the method used to best fit the dynamics is needed (e.g. Figure 3.5 and Figure 3.6).It should also be noted that this type of mesh is less suitable for the comparison with experimental data than the uniform mesh. Therefore, temporal meshes are extremely important to follow the dynamics of a numerical solution. In our situation, temporal meshes are fundamental to our numerical development to optimize the estimation of the simulated solution and better follow the time dynamics of the problem. [SOURCE: Page_110]

## 3.4. NUMERICAL TEMPORAL SCHEME

3.4      Numerical temporal scheme During this thesis, three methods of resolution are developed to determine the time evolution of the saturation in a porous medium. These methods are based on adaptive time meshes according to the following numerical scheme: [SOURCE: Page_111]

i. implicit Crank-Nicolson scheme; [SOURCE: Page_111]

ii. implicit Euler scheme (BDF$_1$) with an explicit Euler scheme; [SOURCE: Page_111]

iii. Backward differential formula (BDF$_2$) scheme with a generalized Leapfrog scheme. [SOURCE: Page_111]

These methods are all included in the same global algorithm structure presented in Figure 3.7. It displays the diagram of the time evolution depending on the time step dt. The global algorithm [SOURCE: Page_111]

consists in determining the equilibrium state of the dynamics by studying the time step variations dtn between two time points tn+1 and tn , dt = tn+1 − tn . A time loop is defined in order to determine the equilibrium state of water saturation in the soil. This loop is initialized for a time step dt = dt^0 defined at time t^0 and runs as long as the condition satisfies the following criterion: [SOURCE: Page_111]

10−12 ≤ dt ≤ 1012 .                                    (3.14) [SOURCE: Page_111]

We have adopted the criterion, Eq. (3.14), to control the numerically solved dynamics and determine the equilibrium state. When the condition defined by Eq. (3.14) is not satisfied, we exit the iterative loop in one of two ways: the algorithm has reached the equilibrium state, or it has failed. We consider [SOURCE: Page_111]

that the algorithm failed, if dt < 10−12 . The dynamics is too fast and that the numerical scheme or the physical formulation is not suitable. In addition, it controls the decimals that the computer is able to interpret as non-zero. Generally, the value 10−12 is defined as a numerical zero by the computer. However, the algorithm reaches the equilibrium state of saturation in the soil, if dt > 1012 . The dynamics of the water transfer is very slow, so the dynamics is considered stationary. Note that the algorithm may continue to run and never reach the equilibrium profile if it is in a periodic regime. In this case, an a posteriori study is required, with a bifurcation study (section 3.5) for example. When the condition defined by Eq. (3.14) is satisfied, the algorithm has not reached equilibrium and we continue the resolution described in the Time step resolution diagram shown in Figure 3.4 until the loop condition is no longer satisfied. The three versions of this algorithm are carried out according to the numerical schemes used (version i, ii and iii), which influences the resolution performance to integrate the dynamics between the initial time t^0 and the resulting equilibrium state. In the following, we present and discuss the performance of each version. [SOURCE: Page_112]

3.4.1    Implicit Crank-Nicolson scheme In a first step, we developed the global algorithm (Figure 3.7) in Matlab, based on the algorithm block from Figure 3.4. To simplify the development of this first version, we used a uniform spatial mesh composed of the Lagrangian finite element P$_1$ and an adaptive time mesh according to Constant prediction with adapt = 1.1 (Figure 3.5). We used the Crank-Nicolson scheme (scheme of order 2) with the method described by Grün and Rumpf [161] in the algorithm block associated to the Solver Step in the Time step resolution diagram (Figure 3.4). In this way, we have implicitly treated the stabilizing part and explicitly the destabilizing part. The matrix formulation of our equation is defined using the implicit scheme and it is solved using an iterative resolution method (Figure 3.8): the Newton/Picard method (described in the Appendix D). Therefore, without the Matlab Toolbox, we have constructed elementary matrices suitable for our matrix formulation. Note that the Matlab language is known for its vector calculations, which is an advantage for solving the weak formulation in matrix form. The iterative Picard/Newton method defines if the calculated solution converges or not after a maximum number of iteration kitermax for a given explicit solution uPn+1 . In this version, we assume that kitermax = 20 and the initialization of the iterative method is performed with the explicit Euler scheme. This version of the algorithm reproduces several water transfer dynamics, such as intermittence in a stratified medium in 1D with periodic conditions, or horizontal diffusion in 1D with homogeneous Neumann conditions. However, these time simulations are extremely long to reach the equilibrium dynamics. In the case of the 1D horizontal diffusion, the simulation was approximately one day, which is not sustainable for more complex domains such as 2D. [SOURCE: Page_112]

3.4.2    Implicit Euler scheme In this second version, we propose to adopt the structure of the first version, changing the adaptive method and the temporal scheme used. Thus, we have an implementation in Matlab, without using toolboxes and with a uniform spatial mesh using Lagrangian finite elements P$_1$ . [SOURCE: Page_112]

In this version, we opt for the temporal adaptive mesh based on the prediction correction method described in Figure 3.6. This mesh is more adapted to the simulated dynamics and limits the divergent loop turns resulting from the iterative resolution of the Picard/Newton algorithm in the Solver Step block (Figure 3.8). The aim of this approach is to drastically reduce simulation time compared with the first version. We used the implicit Euler scheme, also call 1-step Backward differential formula (BDF$_1$; scheme of order 1), with the method described by Grün and Rumpf [161] in the algorithm block associated to the Solver Step in the Time step resolution diagram (Figure 3.4). The explicit Euler scheme (scheme of order 1) is used to define the initialization of the iterative resolution method and to predict the future time step dt. The prediction correction method is used to compensate the loosing of accuracy due to the schemes of order 1 compare to the scheme selected in this version. This implementation obtained the same dynamics as the first version but with a shorter simulation time. The simulation was four times faster, dropping from one day to 6 h. However, this improvement still does not make the simulation time sustainable for more complex domains such as 2D. It is needed to retain the adaptive prediction correction method to define the temporal mesh and to improve the optimization of the functions developed, and to switch to an adaptive spatial mesh. In this third version, we aim to improve on the performance of the second version. This involves optimizing the implementation of numerical tools and the development of certain tools such as the creation of an adaptive spatial mesh. The Matlab language is sufficiently limited to allow all its improvements to be completed during this thesis. Moreover, the creation of matrices adapted to an adaptive spatial mesh becomes very quickly complex. Therefore, we considered using a different programming language and new software to acquire its improvements. Since one of the future objectives after this thesis work is to be able to integrate this numerical development into the Vsoil platform, in which models can be implemented in Fortran or C++, we decided to switch to development in C++. The third version has been implemented in C++, using the Oomph-lib library. This library, developed in Manchester by Matthias Heil and Andrew Hazel [169], contains a number of numerical tools that have been presented in the sections 3.2-3.3. In addition, these tools are suitable for gradient- type equations [236, 271, 321, 165]. We opted an adaptive spatial mesh using the Z$_2$ error estimator composed of the Lagrangian finite element P$_2$ . We also used an adaptive time mesh based on the prediction correction method described in Figure 3.6 with the 2-step Backward differential formula (BDF$_2$; implicit scheme of order 2). The solution is defined by the Newton algorithm with the initialized solution uPn+1 given by the generalised Leapfrog scheme (explicit scheme of order 2). Note that this scheme is used to predict optimally the time steps in the adaptive time mesh during the prediction-correction method. However, these schemes of orders 2 are two-step multi-step schemes. A preliminary initialization step must be added in order to run the algorithm. We have added this step according to the structure described in Figure 3.9. This preliminary step is essential for initializing the time loop in the case [SOURCE: Page_114]

In addition, we are able to handle different boundary conditions: Dirichlet, homogeneous Neumann, flux or periodic. This version has been used to produce the Figures 2.14. We have developed a numerical model to simulate the dynamics of water transfer in a soil in the presence of hydrophobic matter until an equilibrium state is reached. This result is limited to the initial parameter used for the simulation. It is important to be able to study these equilibrium states as a function of system parameters and to distinguish between different possible scenarios. A bifurcation study is a powerful tool to describe this behavior. [SOURCE: Page_115]

3.5      Numerical Bifurcation Analysis Time integration of the system of Partial Differential Equations (PDE) governing dynamics is depen- dent on initial conditions and the computation time required to capture phenomena over a sufficiently long period. As a result, it provides a very limited view of possible phenomena, without really being able to interpret them. A more precise description of the dynamics in phase space can be obtained by using the continuation method or path-following method for stationary or periodic states in parameter space [22, 102]. The principle is to consider different parameters, where stationary [periodic] solutions are known, then, to follow these solutions and their possible bifurcations by continuously varying the parameters. Thus, for each parameter variation, the phase portrait is known more precisely. In par- ticular, unstable directions of equilibria provide information on the behavior of the dynamics (stable, bounded, etc. . . ) for regardless the initial conditions. Knowledge of these bifurcation scenarios of equilibrium branches provides an explanation for the emergence of possibly complex dynamics. With the model we have developed, several asymptotic dynamics (steady-state or periodic dynam- ics) may coexist. Moreover, we pointed out the existence of thresholds, such as that fingering flow or water retained in a hydrophilic layer (see previous chapter). These asymptotic dynamics, called attractors, if they are stable, can be captured by the continuation method. Threshold determination corresponds mathematically to the existence of bifurcations. This theory is generally takes place in the mathematical theory of dynamical systems [374]. Although it is possible to track branches of periodic solutions [341] we focus only on stationary states. In the next sections, we present the continuation method applied to stationary state and the associate numerical scheme. [SOURCE: Page_115]

3.5.1    Principle of continuation method and bifurcation analysis Let us consider a stationary solution s^0 of the dimensionless PDE given by Eq. (3.3) [SOURCE: Page_115]

where p is a parameter as the c concentration of EPS or sm the mean water content. Since s^0 is stationary, then G(s^0 , x, z, p) = 0.                             (3.16) [SOURCE: Page_116]

According to the implicit function theorem, Eq. (3.16) defines an unique function s^0 (x, z, p) as long as the jacobian matrix DG Ds is invertible. The function s^0 (x, z, p) is continuous w.r.t the parameter p. If we associate to the function s^0 (x, z, p) a number corresponding to its norm ||s^0 (x, z, p)|| then by varying continuously the parameter p, we construct a continuous curve (p, ||s^0 ||): it is the continuation method. When the operator is non-invertible there is a bifurcation. Universal properties of these bifur- cations occur regardless of the physical context. Either a turning point appears: it is the saddle-node bifurcation or one (or many) branch(es) emerges(emerge)/vanishes(vanish). The main bifurcations are the transcritical, pitchfork and the Hopf bifurcations. The pitchfork and the Hopf bifurcations appear in the study of chapter 2. The different branches with the bifurcation points constitute the bifurcation diagram. The continuation method consists in tracking the stationary solution s^0 (x, z, p) when varying the pa- rameter p. The used method is the pseudo-arclength method based on a tangent predictor - secant corrector method. This method allows to follow steady-states regardless their stability and allow the turning points (saddle-node bifurcations) to be passed (Fig. 3.10). The tangent predictor - secant corrector method is detailed below. Let us assume the s^0 (x, p^0 ) stationary state known and it is not a bifurcation point. We seek the solution s(x, p^0 + δp). According to Eq. (3.16), [SOURCE: Page_116]

3.5.2    Implementation in PDE2PATH [SOURCE: Page_117]

Inverting the matrix raises the same numerical difficulties as those encountered with the time integra- tion scheme. The challenge lies in filtering out unwanted eigenvalues with a large negative real part due to the presence of differentiation operators. Using Finite Elements method, a continuation code has been implemented in Beltrame and Thiele [22]. The authors employed Krylov reduction using the Cayley transform and an incomplete LU factorization to perform the continuation step and the bifur- cation analysis, i.e. the computation of leading eigenvalues with the larger real part. This code was used at the beginning of the thesis and notably to carry out the bifurcation diagrams in the article [20] presented in chapter 2. [SOURCE: Page_117]

Therefore, we propose an alternative algorithm based on the same continuations methods, but using finite element methods to discretize the spatial domain as in the temporal case. This development is carried out in Matlab using the pseudo-arclength [197, 122] continuation method available in the PDE2PATH [342] package. Solution branches are tracked in parameter space via tangent predictor and Newton corrector while varying the control parameter. To illustrate the implementation steps, we consider the case of stratified media treated in chapter 2, where Ω domain is periodic (periodic boundary conditions) and the bifurcation parameter is the mean saturation sm . To have the uniqueness of the solution to Eq. (3.27) there are usually additional conditions. In our R case, we must impose the conservation of matter Ω w = 0. Moreover, in the presence of a continuous symmetry, i.e. in our case invariance along z, there is a continuum of solutions translated in the z-direction. To fix a solution w(x, z), we need to prohibit variations dw that induce translation wrt z, namely such that w(x, z) + dw = w(x, z + dz). Since the translational symmetry group wrt z is generated by the operator ∂z , the solution must be orthogonal to ∂z w. In a similar manner to the Lagrange multipliers, we add to Eq. 3.6      Conclusion We have presented the numerical tools developed and implemented to study the temporal dynamics of water transfer and the equilibrium dynamics of solutions. These developments are based on the finite element spatial discretization method. These tools, used on the Eq. (3.3), allowed to highlight different types of flow in a homogeneous then stratified medium in the chapter 2: intermittence, fingering, water trapping. The numerical tools developed for the temporal study are efficient for the case studies in this thesis. We have the tools to manage a wide range of problems in several spatial dimensions (1D and 2D classical or axisymmetric) with various boundary conditions (Dirichlet, homogeneous Neumann, periodic and flux). Note that this numerical development works for initial analytical profiles, but can also be applied to experimental data from a file. This offers greater flexibility of execution. Moreover, the numerical tools we have developed can be used to solve problems coupled with other dynamics, as we will see in chapter 5. Additionally, we can improve this temporal development by using the algorithm proposed by Grün. Conversely, the continuations tools developed are limited to periodic cases. An improvement would be to develop a version for different boundary conditions that could be applied to 1D and 2D do- mains [138, 176, 139]. This version would help us to study a wider range of situations and to better understand certain physical behaviors. The development of these tools is important for studying the dynamics of water transfer in soils in the presence of amphiphilic matter. In the following, we propose to focus on the application of these tools to the rhizosphere. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Part III [SOURCE: Page_121]

Application to the rhizosphere [SOURCE: Page_121]

101 [SOURCE: Page_121]

Chapter 4 [SOURCE: Page_123]

Characterization and parameterization of soil water properties in the presence of amphiphilic matter [SOURCE: Page_123]

The aim of this chapter is to characterize a soil properties in the presence of amphiphilic matter in order to parameterize the hydraulic properties required for water transfer modeling (part II). This characterization will be treated with reference to specific soil zone: the rhizosphere. Soil wettability data in relation with amphiphilic matter are rather scarce in the literature. To calibrate our water transfer model applied to rhizosphere, we carried out complementary laboratory experiments in order to get more data. In this experiment, we will define and create an analogue rhizosphere soil, as an artificial porous medium, to get wettability tendencies and hydraulic behaviour in the soil. In this chapter, we first present the design of the artificial rhizosphere medium with different mucilage concentrations: the selection of compounds for root mucilage and the porous substrate (sec- tion 4.1). The characterization of hydraulic properties is presented section 4.2, highlighting the wet- tability trends. Section 4.3 presents and discusses the parameters derived from the characterization of the model. CHAPTER 4. CHARACTERIZATION AND PARAMETERIZATION OF SOIL WATER PROPERTIES IN THE PRESENCE OF AMPHIPHILIC MATTER [SOURCE: Page_124]

4.2.1   Materials and methods . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 113 4.2.1.1   Analogue rhizosphere sampling . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 113 4.2.1.2   Saturated soil hydraulic conductivity measurement . . . . . . . . . . . 114 4.2.1.3   Soil water retention curve . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 115 4.2.1.4   Wettability and drop infiltration measurement . . . . . . . . . . . . . 116 4.2.2   Results . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 118 4.2.2.1   Saturated hydraulic conductivity . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 118 4.2.2.2   Retention curve following single samples . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 119 4.2.2.3   Retention curve with the "one pressure - one sample" process . . . . . 121 4.2.2.4   Wettability measurement and drop infiltration test . . . . . . . . . . . 122 4.3    Parametrization of soil hydraulic properties accounting for variable wet- tability . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 124 4.3.1   Wetting function: disjoining pressure . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 124 4.3.2   Hydraulic conductivity . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 127 4.4    Conclusion       . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . . 128 [SOURCE: Page_124]

4.1      An artificial rhizosphere medium The rhizosphere is the soil zone influenced by the root activity [171, 174, 259]. It is a biological hotspot which influences physico-chemical and hydraulic properties of this soil zone. In the rhizosphere, the roots release organic matter as rhizodeposits consisting of mucilage (ExoPolySaccharides - EPS, being a major component), border cells or simple molecules [185, 133], which highly stimulate microbial activity. Microorganisms of the rhizosphere degrade the exuded root mucilage but also release their own Extracellular Polymeric Substances, generally different from those degraded [75, 255, 292, 4]. As we are looking for a porous medium with a mucilage concentration that limits microbial activity in order to maintain a stable mucilage concentration during the experiment, the artificial medium must be relatively simple to construct and low in nutrients. This artificial medium must contain root mucilage that can be added with concentration to the rhizosphere. [SOURCE: Page_124]

4.1.1    Choosing of root mucilage for building rhizosphere soil As stated above, in the soil, organic substances are exuded by the plant’s roots, driving interactions with soil microflora and the rhizosphere effect [185]. A large quantity of these substances are produced in the form of hydrated mucilage. It is made up of between 15 and 90% of the plant’s photosynthesis [71, 351, 185]. Measured into a sand medium, this secretion of mucilage into the rhizosphere is estimated between 0.05 and 50 mg of dry mucilage for a 1 g mass of dry sand or 5 × 10−3 % to 5% expressed in percentage of sand dry mass [376]. Furthermore, according to Landl et al [204, 206], the high concentrations of 1% would be found only near the tip and close to the root surface, while the lower concentrations would be located further behind the tip and away from the root surface. [SOURCE: Page_124]

104/250 [SOURCE: Page_124]

## 4.1. AN ARTIFICIAL RHIZOSPHERE MEDIUM

Mucilage is a biohydrogel made up of EPS, proteins, minerals and lipids [247]. The proportion and chemical composition of these components vary according to the plant and its environment. However, it is estimated that the contribution of EPS is dominant. It amounts between 78% [246] and 94% [38] of the root mucilage. EPS plays an important role in the biological and physic-chemical properties of the rhizosphere. It is carbon substrate and energy source for rhizosphere’s microorganisms [195, 3, 243, 246] and influences water transfer in this zone [363, 89, 335, 65, 244]. Mucilage forms a gel by the cross-linkage of its polymer chains [283]. Once exuded, this gel interacts with the water and soil. The mucilage adsorbs to soil particles, creating a 3D mesh interconnecting the soil particles [38]. This mucilage link can facilitate cation exchange, water absorption and transfer in soil in the presence of mucilage [246]. When it comes to choose a representative molecule for mucilage analogue, embedding its various properties, synthetic molecules such as PolyGalacturonic Acid (PGA) were often used in 1990 and 2000 studies [148, 371, 13, 235, 266]. PGA was incorporated into synthetic porous media and calcium added for gel formation [376, 88, 13]. However, this substance has been debated in recent years, in particular because of its divergent physical effect compared with root mucilage [39]. In our experimental approach, we have therefore opted for the use of mucilage produced by plants. However, this needs to collect plant’s mucilage and we tested different methods for mucilage collection. [SOURCE: Page_125]

4.1.1.1   Mucilage sampling from young germinated seed root: Aeroponic collection [SOURCE: Page_125]

Mucilage can be collected from young roots after germination. This sampling can be carried out using an aeroponics system and adapted for different plant species (Appendix E). Aeroponics is a method of growing plants with roots hanging in air with a very high humidity level [376, 178]. We use the method described by Zickenrott [376] and Holz [376] (Figure 4.1a), with seedlings using a hermetically sealed box with two areas: [SOURCE: Page_125]

• in the bottom, a layer of demineralized water with aquarium bubblers; [SOURCE: Page_125]

• in the upper part, a metal grid tray where seeds are placed (about 100 − 500 seeds, depending on grain size). [SOURCE: Page_125]

After a few days in the system, the seeds germinate and grow seminal roots which start exuding mucilage near the tip, forming droplets with the high humidity level of the box. During 2-3 days of growth, before roots reach the water layer, the mucilage is collected using a metal stick and a suction device. This suction device is made of a vacuum pump connected to a baby fly, also known as a nasal aspirator (Figure 4.1b). The mucilage is accumulated at the extremity of the young root and collect each days. Collecting mucilage from the roots of seedlings on tray is rather quick, requiring less than half an hour per day. The collection is accumulated in the same vial during the 3 days of growth, for getting a sufficient quantity. With this low-cost method, the chemistry of mucilage collected from different plants can be studied (Appendix E). However, the collected amount (some mL) was not sufficient to produce our porous media impregnated with mucilage. [SOURCE: Page_125]

105/250 [SOURCE: Page_125]

4.1.1.2   Mucilage sampling from maize brace roots [SOURCE: Page_126]

Another possibility to get plant root mucilage is to use roots that emerge in air, not in in soil. Maize develops such roots, called brace roots, that emerge first in air and later go into soil. These brace roots at th base of maize stem exude mucilage at their tip [246]. This mucilage is similar to the one exuded by underground roots [71, 258]. Figure 4.2a shows maize brace roots secreting mucilage in the field. This photo was taken in a field at INRAE’s experimental center in Avignon. [SOURCE: Page_126]

(a)                                                        (b) [SOURCE: Page_126]

Figure 4.2: Collection of brace maize root mucilage (a) Brace maize roots in field with mucilage secretion (b) Brace roots maize mucilage collect in the lab. [SOURCE: Page_126]

We adapted the protocol proposed in the literature [25, 2] to collect mucilage of maize brace root. We sampled maize plants in the field by cutting them at ground level in the field. These sampled plants were selected based on the following two criteria: high numbers of brace roots, and these roots do not touch the soil. After sampling, the plants were transferred to the laboratory. The roots were then cleaned to remove soil using demineralized water and a toothbrush. Cleaning the plant is important to minimize soil contamination of the mucilage that will be collected. After washing, the maize stem [SOURCE: Page_126]

base with roots is placed in a hydroponic solution with calcium-rich mineral water. Collection of mucilage takes place after 24 hours in the hydroponic system with the suction device during 2-3 days (Figure 4.2b). Although this method gives access to root mucilage from field plants, (i) it necessitates a maize crop, at the good development stage, (ii) rather small amounts of mucilage are sampled: around 100 mL of fresh mucilage is collected over a period of several weeks using about a hundred maize plants (although this mucilage exhibits a high fresh/dry matter ratio: 0.93%). Moreover, the mucilage collected revealed traces of border cells and other soil deposits (Figure 4.3) and would necessitate further processing before use. [SOURCE: Page_127]

Figure 4.3: Brace root mucilage with soil and rhizodeposits. [SOURCE: Page_127]

4.1.1.3   Mucilage sampling from Chia seeds [SOURCE: Page_127]

Chia seeds are seeds of Salvia hispanica, a flowering plant (from Lamiaceae family). Chia seeds produce mucilage on contact with water. Its mucilage has a chemical composition (primarily xylose, glucose, and uronic acids; [216, 3]) and physical properties such as viscosity similar to those of mucilage exuded by the roots of lupines (Lupinus albus L. ) and maize (Zea mays L.) [282, 67, 3] and microbial Extracellular Polymeric Substances in soils [316, 59]. Its mucilage forms a gel that absorbs water when it is hydrated and becomes hydrophobic as it dries [3]. Because of the experimental problems of obtaining sufficient quantities of root mucilage [376, 198, 24, 39], chia seed mucilage has been chosen as a model mucilage since few years. It is easily and fastly available in large quantities. Collecting mucilage requires two steps: [SOURCE: Page_127]

1. mucilage production; [SOURCE: Page_127]

2. mucilage collection with a suction system (Figure 4.4). [SOURCE: Page_127]

For mucilage production, we placed 5 g of chia seed in a tube with 50 g of water and a magnetic stirrer shaked the mixture during 2 h [3]. During shaking, the seed’s mucilage is exuded, gradually transforming the water in the tube into a gel. At the end of shaking, the fresh mucilage was ready for collection. Collection is carried out using a suction system. The suction device is based on two soil filtration systems and a suction pump. Figure 4.4a is an illustration of the system developed (Figure 4.4b). Two [SOURCE: Page_127]

(b)                                  (c)                                 (d) [SOURCE: Page_128]

Figure 4.4: Chia seed mucilage collection system. (a) Sketch of the suction system. The red arrows represent the suction exercised by the pump located at the far right. (b) Photo of the collection system. (c) Chia seed mucilage collected after passing through the collection system. (d) Mixture of mucilage and chia seeds before (left) and after (right) passage through the collection system. [SOURCE: Page_128]

soil filtration systems (A and B) are connected by a pipe. System B is connected by another pipe to a suction pump. Suction is imposed by the pump in both soil filtration systems and is represented by the red arrows in Figure 4.4a. Collection is carried out in device A, comprising two compartments separated by a filter with a mesh size of 750 µm. This filter separates the mixture of mucilage and seed deposited in the upper compartment. The mucilage is filtered and recovered in the lower compartment under pressure in the system (Figure 4.4c). A spatula is required to facilitate the filtration process. Chia seeds clogg part of the mesh if not moved regularly. System B is used as a buffer to protect the pump from mucilage. A piece of fabric is placed in the lower compartment of this closed soil filtration device. This collection method is very efficient and guarantees a huge quantity of mucilage: 20 mL of fresh mucilage is collected in 3 h time (2 h of mixing and maximum 1 h of aspiration). We note that we could collect almost all the fresh mucilage initially (Figure 4.4d). Only the mucilage near the seeds could not be collected. Indeed, the capillary forces binding the remaining mucilage to the chia seeds were too strong for our suction pump to extract it. We also observed that the fresh/dry matter ratio was correct (ranging from 0.4 to 0.6% according to our measurements and the literature [199, 178, 24]) for processing into artificial porous medium. Therefore, chia seed mucilage is a candidate to be used as model mucilage for the creation of the rhizosphere analogue medium experiments. [SOURCE: Page_128]

## 4.1. AN ARTIFICIAL RHIZOSPHERE MEDIUM

4.1.1.4    Synthesis on root mucilage [SOURCE: Page_129]

There are several methods for collecting root mucilage from plants at different stages. After testing some different methods, the mucilage amount obtained from most of these methods is too small for a further use in a rhizosphere porous medium analogue [376, 245]. Galloway [141] explain that was difficult to collect enough pure mucilage, as we have shown. Thus, collecting enough mucilage is a limiting factor for carrying out experiments on the hydraulic properties of this porous medium impregnated with mucilage especially when a number of replicates is needed. Within the panel of methods tested, the only acceptable candidate for mucilae production is from chia seed. This mucilage is similar to that of lupins and maize, despite the fact that seed- and root-derived mucilage differ in some aspects. However, this mucilage has specific characteristics that differ according to the stage of the plant and its environmental context [101]. In the following of this manuscript, we will consider chia seed mucilage as our model mucilage. In the future, it would be relevant to carry out further studies, comparing the chemical and physical properties of this mucilage with other mucilages at different plant stages. An initial study of mucilage characteristics for different plant seedlings is presented in Appendix E. A comparison of these results with chia seed mucilage could be envisaged in order to estimate water behavior with another model mucilage. [SOURCE: Page_129]

4.1.2     The porous media design for rhizosphere analogue Soils are extremely diverse, depending on pedogenetic processes at work, climate and vegetation/- land use and will present large range of properties (chemical, physical, but also biological), texture, structure,. . . This diversity will also impact rhizosphere properties, which itself is submitted to in- tense biological, chemical and physical interactions, and make quite complex the study of this specific medium. To examine the hydraulic properties linked to mucilage effects, we will thus opt for a "simple" medium in order to get a stable medium over time and to limit microbial action. Sand is often used as "simple" porous medium and is a good candidate for hydraulic experiments on an artificial rhizosphere. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Figure 4.5: Particle size of Fontainebleau sand from [112]. [SOURCE: Page_129]

Its structure remains unchanged in the presence of water, and as oligotrophic substrate, should limit microbial development. We used the "Fontainebleau" sand in our experiments, often used in [SOURCE: Page_129]

109/250 [SOURCE: Page_129]

CHAPTER 4. CHARACTERIZATION AND PARAMETERIZATION OF SOIL WATER PROPERTIES IN THE PRESENCE OF AMPHIPHILIC MATTER [SOURCE: Page_130]

geophysics, geotechnical or porous media research. This sand is a pure silica sand (>99.5% SiO$_2$ ) made up of fine, round to sub-angular white grains [95, 34, 112, 6], with an uniform particle size distribution (mode and mean particle size of about 200 µm ± 100 µm [34, 109, 80] ; Figure 4.5). This substrate is classified as a very fine [215], which has an average dry density ranging from 1.39 to 1.77 g.cm−3 [34, 95]. This sand is available from suppliers (WRR). In order to control our medium, we selected a size range of particles between 200 and 250 µm by dry sieving using stainless steel sieves. Therefore, the homogeneous Fontainebleau sand we used has a particle size of between 200 and 250 µm. 4.1.3   Design and elaboration of the analogue rhizosphere soil [SOURCE: Page_130]

General elaboration of the analogue rhizosphere soil [SOURCE: Page_130]

We chose Fontainebleau sand (section 4.1.2) and chia seed mucilage (section 4.1.1) as the main con- stituents of the analogue rhizosphere soil. The elaboration of these analogue media is carried out in several steps: [SOURCE: Page_130]

### 5. Sampling and humectation desiccation cycles.

The different steps of this design method are described in detail in Appendix F. Step 1 is the collection of a sufficient fresh amount of mucilage needed to build the medium, using the system described in section 4.1.1. In step 2 the sand is washed limiting the presence of metals and bacterial colonies. After completing these steps, the mucilage is mixed with the substrate. In this step, the quantity of mucilage to add per gram of dry soil is needed. We designed our rhizosphere analogue medium with a range of concentrations that have been esti- mated in the rhizosphere. ## 4.1. AN ARTIFICIAL RHIZOSPHERE MEDIUM

of 0, 0.1, 0.5 and 1 g of dry mucilage for 100 g of dry sand and expressed as percentage in the fol- lowing (c = 1% correspond to 1 g of dry mucilage for 100 g of dry sand).Analogue media with higher concentrations than 1% were not produced because of high amount of mucilage needed. The wet/dry weight ratio of collected fresh mucilage (for each mucilage collection) was measured by oven drying (drying at 105°C for 24 h) and this ratio used in step 4 for mixing sand and fresh mucilage at the target concentration. In the step 5, samples are prepared for hydraulic experiments: saturated hydraulic conductivity, water retention and wettability measurement. During this step, humectation/desiccation cycles are performed to stabilize the structure of the medium. The humecta- tion/dessication method consists in humectating the medium by capillary rise from the bottom to limit the presence of air in the sample, then after saturation drying it at 30°C during 48 h [88, 199, 108]. [SOURCE: Page_131]

## 4.2. CHARACTERIZATION OF HYDRAULIC PROPERTIES OF THE RHIZOSPHERE ANALOGUE MEDIUM

4.2.1     Materials and methods [SOURCE: Page_133]

4.2.1.1    Analogue rhizosphere sampling [SOURCE: Page_133]

Several sample formats have been designed in order to perform the various experiments under optimum conditions for defining the hydraulic characteristics of the media. The design of each sample follows the steps described in section 4.1.3. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Hydraulic conductivity Samples from the disturbed medium design are used for Ksat measure- ments. In cylinders of 5 cm high and 5.1 cm in diameter, 100 g of dry sand with the appropriate concentration of dry mucilage is placed in the cylinder. The bottom of each cylinder is covered with muslin held in place with rubber bands, i.e the sample remains in place while the water drains away. We note that the dimensions of the cylinder require a large quantity of mucilage for this measurement. Then for these measurements, we selected two mucilage concentrations (0.1 and 0.5%) and a control medium without mucilage (0%). Three replicates were designed for the 0.1 and 0.5% concentrations and only two without mucilage. We have a total of 8 samples for Ksat measurements, whose height will be around 3.3 cm in the cylinders. Note that the cylinder must be filled at least to 3 cm of its height for the measurement to be carried out. Following the wetting/drying cycle, the dry samples are placed on a suction table to saturate them with water: they are now ready for measurement. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Water retention The disturbed medium design is used to create samples for measuring water re- tention. In small cylinders of 1 cm high and 2.6 cm in diameter, 7 g of sand with the desired mucilage concentration is placed in the cylinder. The bottom of the cylinder is closed with a cloth, allowing the sample to remain in place while the water drains away. We selected three mucilage concentrations (0.1%, 0.5% and 1%) in addition to control samples (0%). We performed two types of water retention measurements in these media. Initially, we made 10 replicates of each medium, and each replicate was used to measure water retention for the following suctions: 0.04, 0.11, 0.26, 0.51, 0.75, 0.99, 1, 2, 10 and 20 m. However, due to degradation issues of mucilage concentration over time, new samples were designed, i.e. 5 replicates of each medium for each suction to be measured: 0.25, 0.35, 0.5, 0.65, 1, 3 and 10 m. Thus, we have a first version with 40 samples designed while the second version requires 140 samples. The second version requires more samples and more mucilage, but limits mucilage degra- dation during measurements. Each sample will have a height of around 0.9 cm in the cylinders. Note that the cylinder must be filled to at least 5 mm of its height. Following the wetting/drying cycle, the dry samples are placed on a suction table to saturate them with water: they are then ready for measurement on the suction table and the pressure plate apparatus. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Drop infiltration Wettability measurements are performed by infiltrating a drop into two sample designs: disturbed and undisturbed medium. Small petri dishes of 1 cm high and 5.3 cm in diameter are used for disturbed media, while large petri dishes of 1 cm high and 8.7 cm in diameter are used for undisturbed media. Note that the bottom of each dish is closed by a rigid plastic base, allowing the sample to remain in place and retaining any water that infiltrates. We selected three mucilage concentrations (0.1%, 0.5% and 1%) in addition to control samples (0%) for each sample design. We have small petri dishes with 17 g of sand with mucilage concentration with the disturbed design and [SOURCE: Page_133]

113/250 [SOURCE: Page_133]

CHAPTER 4. CHARACTERIZATION AND PARAMETERIZATION OF SOIL WATER PROPERTIES IN THE PRESENCE OF AMPHIPHILIC MATTER [SOURCE: Page_134]

large petri dishes with 30 g of sand with mucilage concentration with the undisturbed design. Note that each sample is approximately 0.52 cm in height. Following the wetting/drying cycle, the dry samples are ready for infiltration measurements. [SOURCE: Page_134]

4.2.1.2   Saturated soil hydraulic conductivity measurement [SOURCE: Page_134]

The hydraulic conductivity depends on the intrinsic properties of the porous medium, the degree of saturation and the properties of the flowing fluid. The presence of organic matter such as EPS from mucilage can alter the properties of the porous medium (pore distribution, grain wettability) as well as those of the fluid with a change in solutes (variation in viscosity, surface tension). As a result, the hydraulic conductivity is also altered. It is relevant to study the hydraulic conductivity of analogue [SOURCE: Page_134]

Figure 4.10: A constant head permeameter system. [SOURCE: Page_134]

media for different mucilage concentrations. The saturated hydraulic conductivity Ksat is measure using a water system (Figure 4.10) This system is connected to the sample by pipes imposing a constant upward hydraulic head in the porous medium using a water height difference between two reservoirs. The water flowing out from the top of sample is collected on a precision balance placed under the evacuation tube to measure the accumulated mass of water and recorded each minute on a computer. Measurements at the same pressure continues until the flow rate is constant over several measurements. Several minutes can be necessary to reach equilibrium between 2 successive pressure steps. The water flow is measured at different pressures, and the Ksat is estimated from linear regression based on Darcy law: [SOURCE: Page_134]

H Q = Ksat S     ,                                        (4.1) L [SOURCE: Page_134]

where Q [g.min−1 ] is the flow rate, S (19.6 cm^2 ) is the cross-section of the column, Ksat [cm.min−1 ] is saturated hydraulic conductivity, H [cm] is the hydraulic head difference across the column, and L (3.3 cm) is the height of the column. [SOURCE: Page_134]

## 4.2. CHARACTERIZATION OF HYDRAULIC PROPERTIES OF THE RHIZOSPHERE ANALOGUE MEDIUM

4.2.1.3   Soil water retention curve [SOURCE: Page_135]

The water retention (i.e the soil moisture content for a given suction) of sand/mucilage media was measured for suctions varying in the range [0, 15] bars. Note that, the suction of 15 bars being the plant’s permanent wilting point where it is considered that the water held in the soil at or below this suction is no more available for plant transpiration. According to the range of suction imposed to samples, two types of system were used: suction tables and pressure plate apparatus. [SOURCE: Page_135]

(a)                                                      (b) [SOURCE: Page_135]

Figure 4.11: Water retention measurement system. (a) Suction table. (b) Pressure plate apparatus. [SOURCE: Page_135]

The suction table In the low suction range from 0 to 0.1 bars, suction tables are used (Figure 4.11a). Samples are placed on a bed of saturated fine sand (silica) connected to a suction system. This suction is imposed by a differential water height between the water level in an adjustable water reservoir and the saturated surface of the sand bed. The sample are first saturated on the table, by rising the water level at the surface of sand or slightly above. Then negative heads are imposed by lowering the water reservoir level at a given height. The imposed head is maintained until hydraulic equilibrium is reached. In order to confirm equilibrium, a replicate of the samples is weighed every day until constant mass between 2 consecutive days. After equilibrium, the gravimetric water content of the sample (g of water per g of dry soil) is measured by oven drying 24 h at 105°C. [SOURCE: Page_135]

The pressure plate apparatus In the high-suction range from 0.1 to 15 bars, pressure plate device was used (Figure 4.11b). Samples are placed in a sealed enclosure on a micro-porous ceramic plate. The plate has been saturated and connected to the outside of the enclosure to evacuate any water excess. It is important that the ceramic plate and the soil sample are well hydraulically connected, i.e there is no air between samples and ceramic plate. When the chamber is closed, gas nitrogen is injected to impose a constant positive pressure. The pressure in the enclosure pushes part of the water contained in the initially saturated sample out of the enclosure. The water flow stops and soil water reaches equilibrium when the pressure (in absolute value) in the soil equals the gas pressure. In order to check the equilibrium of the sample, the evacuated water volume is estimated each day by recording the height of water in a small tube placed at chamber outlet.In addition, the tube can be weighed to [SOURCE: Page_135]

115/250 [SOURCE: Page_135]

CHAPTER 4. CHARACTERIZATION AND PARAMETERIZATION OF SOIL WATER PROPERTIES IN THE PRESENCE OF AMPHIPHILIC MATTER [SOURCE: Page_136]

add a second control criterion. Equilibrium is considered to be reached when the mass of the tube and the level of water in the tube do not change for two consecutive days. When equilibrium is reach, samples are collected and their gravimetric water content measured. [SOURCE: Page_136]

4.2.1.4   Wettability and drop infiltration measurement [SOURCE: Page_136]

The determination of the retention curve presented here is associated, in relation to wettability, with the hydrophilic properties of the porous medium. However, it is difficult to assess the hydrophobicity of a medium using this method. There are other standardized methods for measuring soil hydrophobicity. These include the Molarity of Ethanol Droplet (MED) test [357, 194] and the Water Drop Penetration Time (WDPT) test [357]. These methods classify the degree of hydrophobicity of the medium using criteria based on drop infiltration times. This measurement give us a trend of the hydrophobicity in the medium. Based on these tests, we aim to classify the wettability of our analogue media in order to characterize the effects of EPS concentration on the hydrophobicity level of the medium. A water drop is deposited on our analogue media (disturbed and undisturbed) using the WDPT and MED tests to classify their degree of hydrophobicity (Figure 4.12). [SOURCE: Page_136]

(a)                                                        (b) [SOURCE: Page_136]

Figure 4.12: Photo of droplet deposited on rhizosphere analogue media using an adjustable laboratory micropipette. [SOURCE: Page_136]

WDPT test is a measure of hydrophobicity at the surface of a defined medium, based on the infil- tration time of a drop into the medium deposed on its surface. A degree of hydrophobicity is associated with the drop’s penetration time: instantaneous for a hydrophilic medium, very slow for hydrophobic media and staying at the surface until evaporation for media with very high hydrophobicity [338]. The definition of infiltration time associated with a hydrophobicity category is an arbitrary choice explaining the variety of classification tables present in the literature [32, 104, 103]. To estimate hydrophobicity in our analogue media (disturbed and undisturbed), we selected the classification defined by Bisdom et al (Table 4.2 for a 50 µL water drop). This classification is well described, and has equivalence with measurements of the contact angle of a drop on the medium. Contact angle is a common measure of surface wettability. This angle increases with hydrophobicity: an angle of 0° defines a hydrophilic medium, while an angle of 180° defines an extremely hydrophobic medium. However, it is difficult to [SOURCE: Page_136]

## 4.2. CHARACTERIZATION OF HYDRAULIC PROPERTIES OF THE RHIZOSPHERE ANALOGUE MEDIUM

Bisdom et al., 1993 [32]    Mandal et al, 2009 [224] Classification           Infiltration time [s])      Contact angle (°) Hydrophilic                     <5                          0 Slightly hydrophobic                5 à 60                      67 Strongly hydrophobic               60 à 600                     90 Severely hydrophobic              600 à 3600                    98 Extremely hydrophobic                > 3600                      122 [SOURCE: Page_137]

Table 4.2: Classification of the hydrophobicity of a medium: WDPT test. [SOURCE: Page_137]

measure because it requires an equilibrium state which is difficult to obtain because of the dynamics of infiltration into the medium and its spreading over the surface. [SOURCE: Page_137]

MED test also known as the "Percentage Alcohol Test" [357, 194], is an indirect measure of soil surface tension. It also indicates the repulsion of a drop of water solution containing some percentage of ethanol from the soil surface. The presence of ethanol modifies the surface tension of the drop. The surface tension of the drop decreases as the percentage of ethanol in the solution increases. This test measures the volume percentage of ethanol in a drop of water required to reduce the drop’s surface tension compared with the surface tension of the soil. The drop will rapidly penetrate the medium if its surface tension is lower than the surface tension of the medium. The spreading coefficient S is then positive, i.e. we have a hydrophilic tendency promoting infiltration. Otherwise, the drop will penetrate the medium slowly. The spreading coefficient S is then negative, i.e. we have a partial wetting tendency causing slower infiltration than with total wetting. The volume percentage of ethanol in a drop of water is associated with a drop penetration time. This time is compared with a reference infiltration time threshold tc to classify the degree of hydrophobicity at the surface of the medium. The reference time is an arbitrary criterion that varies in the literature: tc = 3 s [83], tc = 5 s [194] or tc = 10 s [164]. The Crockford criterion has been chosen for our study in order to have classification equivalences between the MED test and the WDPT test. The penetration of a drop of 50 µL of water and ethanol solution is measured to determine the minimum volume percentage of ethanol that satisfies infiltration in less than 3 seconds. Each minimum volume percentage of ethanol is associated with a degree of hydrophobicity at the surface of the medium (Table 4.3 [103]). This degree of hydrophobicity is categorized into 7 classes: from 1 (very hydrophilic) to 7 (extremely hydrophobic). [SOURCE: Page_137]

Class          Description             Ethanol (%) 1         Very hydrophilic                0 2            Hydrophilic                  3 3       Slightly hydrophobic              5 4      Moderatly hydrophobic             8.5 5       Strongly hydrophobic             13 6     Very strongly hydrophobic          24 7      Extremely hydrophobic             36 [SOURCE: Page_137]

Table 4.3: Classification of the hydrophobicity of a medium: MED test. [SOURCE: Page_137]

CHAPTER 4. CHARACTERIZATION AND PARAMETERIZATION OF SOIL WATER PROPERTIES IN THE PRESENCE OF AMPHIPHILIC MATTER [SOURCE: Page_138]

4.2.2     Results [SOURCE: Page_138]

4.2.2.1    Saturated hydraulic conductivity [SOURCE: Page_138]

Saturated hydraulic conductivity is measured from average of two samples for the control medium (c = 0%) and of three samples for the other two concentrations (c = 0.1% and c = 0.5%). Without mucilage (c = 0%), the measured Ksat is 1.35 × 10−4 m.s−1 , and quite close to mea- surements obtained from independent experiments in the literature (Table 4.1). For higher mucilage concentrations, we found that Ksat was of the same order of magnitude as the control medium. More- over, there is a slight increasing tendency of Ksat with increasing mucilage concentration: Ksat is 1.94 × 10−4 m.s−1 for c = 0.1% and Ksat is 2.26 × 10−4 m.s−1 for c = 0.5%. Our results suggest that the presence of molecules adsorbed on the particles and/or diffusing in the liquid did not affect hydraulic conductivity. Our results in the presence of mucilage are surprising compared with the trend obtained in the work of Kroener et al [199]. They showed that mucilage influences the hydraulic conductivity of the medium as a function of particle size. In their experiment, they produced 5 ranges of analogue rhizosphere medium from 5 different ranges of particle sizes, from coarse sand to silt (630 − 1000, 360 − 630, 200 − 500, 60 − 200, and < 20 µm) were prepared with varying concentrations of chia seed mucilage (0%, 0.1%, 0.4%, and 0.8%). Note that their medium design seems to be similar to our undisturbed design, except for the medium with particle sizes < 20 µm. This latter medium was sieved to break aggregates to enable homogeneous mixing with mucilage. This process is similar to disturbed design of our samples. It should also be noted that the substrates chosen in their experiment were not Fontainebleau sand. They made 3 replicas of each medium: two media using quartz sand from sand pit in southern Florida, USA (coarse sand with a particle grain size of 630 − 1000 µm and 360 − 630 µm), two other media using quartz sand from sand pit located near Duingen, Germany (fine sand with a particle grain size of 200 − 500 µm and 60 − 200 µm) and another using silt from Reinhausen, Lower Saxony, Germany (particle grain size < 20 µm). The Fontainebleau sand (fine sand with a particle grain size of 200 − 250 µm) and the sand near Duingen have similar particle grain sizes. We can therefore expect similar results for this range of media. However, they obtained a decreasing trend in hydraulic conductivity at saturation for all sand media with increasing mucilage concentration. Note that this trend is similar with the results in Landl et al [206]. For every experiment, their hydraulic conductivities for sandy media decreased by several orders of magnitude: [SOURCE: Page_138]

• Ksat was 3 × 10−4 m.s−1 for c = 0%, whereas it was 5 × 10−7 m.s−1 for c = 0.8% in the medium with a particle size 200 − 500 µm in the results of Kroener, [SOURCE: Page_138]

• Ksat was 10−4 m.s−1 for c = 0% , whereas it was around 10−6 m.s−1 in presence of EPS in the results of Landl. [SOURCE: Page_138]

4.2.2.2       Retention curve following single samples [SOURCE: Page_139]

In this experiment 1, we selected suctions between 0 and 20 m. At the end of these measurements, the dry density of our analogue media was similar for the different concentrations (γd = 1.31). The results show that porosity is similar in all designed samples. The porosity ϕ = 0.33 is more or less the same as that obtained in the literature (ϕ = 0.36). Figure 4.14 represents the average retention curves obtained for our analogue media with c = 0, 0.1, 0.5 and 1%. This figure shows water content results expressed as gravimetric water content (Figure 4.14a) or water saturation (Figure 4.14b). We observe trends in retention depending on the imposed suctions and EPS concentrations. For a given EPS concentration, moisture content in the medium follows the classical trend of a decrease with increasing suction. However, for a given suction, the water content increases with concentration. The attractive forces of the EPS in the medium enable more water to be absorbed in the sandy soil and a higher retention. This trend is visible all over the range of investigated suctions, except for the suction between 0.26 and 0.75 m, where this trend is reversed. As EPS concentration increases, the medium retains less water for these suctions. This could be interpreted as a greater repulsion of water by the EPS, with a greater proportion of repellent EPS parts compared to attractive parts, in this zone of sharp decrease in water content. So EPS could induce 2 kind of trends for retention: higher retention for low and high suctions (hydrophilic behaviour) and a lower retention for intermediate [SOURCE: Page_139]

4.2.2.3                                   Retention curve with the "one pressure - one sample" process [SOURCE: Page_141]

In order to limit degradation of mucilage in the medium, instead of following a single sample through multiple pressure steps necessitating long times, we used one sample for one pressure step. The time for measurement is considerably reduced, reducing degradation impacts. Globally, hydraulic equilibrium of samples was reached in less than 12 days. In parallel, measurements of microbial respiration and the amount of carbon present in the media were determined over time in the mucilage sand media. Results show that microbial activity was present in our media, causing the mucilage concentration to vary with time (Appendix G). A peak in respiration activity was detected at 12 days. This experiment shows that the biological activity complexifies the medium characterization, enhancing the need for a balance of time between time for sample equilibrium and degradation. 4.2.2.4   Wettability measurement and drop infiltration test [SOURCE: Page_142]

WDPT test [SOURCE: Page_142]

We observe that drop infiltration time increases with EPS concentration in both types of media. Media become more hydrophobic in the presence of EPS. Comparing the level of hydrophobicity results for the same concentration, we observe high differences between the two types of samples. Infiltration takes more time for the undisturbed medium. Figure 4.17 displays the average infiltration time of a water drop in our dry analogue media (disturbed and undisturbed samples for concentrations of c = 0, c = 0.5 and c = 1%). The undisturbed medium at c = 0.5% is severely hydrophobic (t ≈ 3500 s) and at c = 1% is on the boundary between severely and extremely hydrophobic (t ≈ 3600 s). In contrast, the disturbed medium at c = 0.5% is hydrophilic (t ≈ 2 s) and at c = 1% is strongly hydrophobic (t ≈ 380 s). Undisturbed media appear more hydrophobic than disturbed media with the same EPS concentration. This difference seems due to a difference in distribution of EPS in the undisturbed media. A crust is present on the surface of these undisturbed samples, in contrast to disturbed. The crust results from the accumulation of EPS on the surface of the media, making it more hydrophobic. [SOURCE: Page_142]

If the WDPT test is a suitable and reproducible method for our laboratory measurements it can be relatively long especially for highly hydrophobic media (e.g. for c = 0.5% and c = 1% in undisturbed media). In this case, evaporation can affect our infiltration time measurement. Despite this constraint, we have identified a trend of increasing hydrophobicity at the surface of our media with increasing EPS concentration. [SOURCE: Page_142]

## 4.2. CHARACTERIZATION OF HYDRAULIC PROPERTIES OF THE RHIZOSPHERE ANALOGUE MEDIUM

MED test [SOURCE: Page_143]

Drop infiltrations with different percentages of ethanol by volume were carried out in our undisturbed samples exhibiting the high hydrophobicity observed with the WDPT test (c = 0.5% and c = 1%). The c = 0.5% and c = 1% media were found to be consistent with the classification defined by the WDPT test: the c = 0.5% medium is classified as a very strongly hydrophobic medium (24% ethanol) and the c = 1% medium is classified as an extremely hydrophobic medium (36% ethanol). The MED test confirms the trend obtained with the WDPT test: hydrophobicity increases with EPS concentration. This MED test is more suitable for highly hydrophobic media than WDPT test. It gives a rapid result, limiting the effects of evaporation, especially in field measurements where it is difficult to control external phenomena impacting the infiltration of a drop [83, 352, 103]. However, in our situation, the WDPT test remains the most practical and reproducible because our measurements are carried out in a laboratory (controlled environment) [83, 103] and there is no addition of any other substance than water to the medium. This last point is particularly important for comparing the infiltration time between the successive infiltration of one and two drops, as addition of second drop occurs on an already moistened medium. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Hydrophobicity and water saturation in the presence of EPS from successive drop infil- tration test [SOURCE: Page_143]

Hydrophobicity increases with the presence of EPS at the surface of dry medium. We studied the infiltration behavior of a second drop placed at the same location as the first one in the disturbed medium. Figure 4.18 shows the infiltration time of the first and then second drop of the same volume in our disturbed media with concentration c = 0%, c = 0.1%, c = 0.5% and c = 1%. We observe that [SOURCE: Page_143]

Figure 4.18: Infiltration time of a first drop in a disturbed sample (3 replications) compared with the infiltration time of a second drop in the same sample. * Student’s t test (paired samples) shows a significant difference (p<0.0001) between the infiltration time of a second drop compared with the first drop, when the concentration of EPS is 1% in the sample tested. [SOURCE: Page_143]

123/250 [SOURCE: Page_143]

CHAPTER 4. CHARACTERIZATION AND PARAMETERIZATION OF SOIL WATER PROPERTIES IN THE PRESENCE OF AMPHIPHILIC MATTER [SOURCE: Page_144]

the second drop needs more time to infiltrate than the first. Apart the lowering of suction gradients in the second drop experiment this also means that the surface of the medium becomes more hydrophobic after the first drop. This is particularly illustrated with the 1% concentration where the first drop infiltrates the medium in around 380 s compared to 1130 s for the second. This increase in the time infiltration of the second drop is similar for each concentration compared with the infiltration time of the first drop on dry soil. Moreover, this infiltration time increases gradually with EPS concentration for both the first and second drops. Hydrophobic interactions between the EPS and the droplet are always present that increase time infiltration: lateral and vertical diffusion in the medium is subject to these hydrophobic interactions. At the surface, the second drop spreads more than the first one due to the effect of attractive interactions. But the majority of EPS in soil are still hydrophobic at low saturation [105], which increases infiltration time. Thus the degree of hydrophobicity changes with the water saturation at the surface of the medium in the presence of EPS. Wettability of the medium varies with saturation at the surface of the medium [105, 219]. Hydrophobicity increases after drying a very wet sample [230, 134]. The wettability of our media in the presence of EPS changes depending on their concentration and initial saturation in the medium. [SOURCE: Page_144]

4.3      Parametrization of soil hydraulic properties accounting for variable wettability Modeling of water transfer in porous media requires relationships which describe the hydraulic prop- erties of the medium. These relationships are defined by the kind of (semi-empirical) function chosen and its associated parameters (chapters 2-3). The aim of this section is to define the parameters of the hydraulic functions associated with our disturbed media. These parameters are fitted based on empirical data obtained during the characterization of hydraulic properties (section 4.2). [SOURCE: Page_144]

4.3.1    Wetting function: disjoining pressure Wettability in the analogue medium is modeled by the disjoining pressure Πw . This pressure is the linear combination of the attractive hc , repulsive hr and compressive hcomp pressures (Chapter 2), [SOURCE: Page_144]

Πw (s) = hc (s) + hr (s) + hcomp (s).                              (4.2) [SOURCE: Page_144]

The retention curve during drying is associated with attractive pressure (section 4.2.2.2). This curve is generally presented by the Brooks and Corey function [42] 1 hc (s) = Hcap s− λ ,                                      (4.3) [SOURCE: Page_144]

or the van Genuchten-Mualem function [240, 343] [SOURCE: Page_144]

with m = 1 − n^1 . [SOURCE: Page_144]

The repulsive pressure is based on a function of embedding 4 parameters, describing its influence in the intermediate saturation range. Based on the changes observed in the retention curve, we consider that this repulsive pressure acts for suctions ranging from 0.35 to 0.65 m (section 4.2.2.3) corresponding to saturation between s = 0.2 and s = 0.6. We represent the repulsive pressure with a Gaussian function with a standard deviation of σ = 0.2 and residual saturation given by sr = 0.05 then a maximum repulsion occurs around s = 0.2.               χ         (s − sr )2 hr (s) = −       √ exp −              ,                              (4.5) Hcap σ 2π        2σ 2 [SOURCE: Page_146]

where χ is the amplitude of repulsion. In the case of sand, research indicates hydrophobicity effects for saturation around s = 0.2 [219]. This result is consistent with our function. However, the parameters determined here are based on tendencies and need to be defined more accurately with appropriate experiments, in particular for the amplitude of repulsion. This parameter should vary according to the concentration of EPS in the medium. A capillary rise experiment in a stratified medium (one stratum without EPS and another with different EPS concentrations) [201] could enable us to define the χ parameter for our media. Finally, we define an air compression function hcomp taking into account air trapping when the medium is close to saturation.
