## 4.3. PARAMETRIZATION OF SOIL HYDRAULIC PROPERTIES ACCOUNTING FOR VARIABLE WETTABILITY

It depends on two parameters: a coefficient κ and the shape parameter λ derived from the retention curve. The κ coefficient is associate to the maximum saturation considered in our study, i.e. we define κ such that we have a maximum water saturation in our sample scomp close to 1 (scomp ≈ 1). This model allows us to take account of air trapping in the medium. We have determined most of the wettability parameters from our characterizations of disturbed analogue rhizosphere media. The remaining parameters χ and κ according to the EPS concentration need to be characterized and parameterized. Literature experiments will be used to complete these parameters required for numerical simulations (Chapter 5). [SOURCE: Page_147]

4.3.2   Hydraulic conductivity [SOURCE: Page_147]

Hydraulic conductivity in the disturbed medium is modeled using Eq.(1.27) with a relative conductivity function kr and the hydraulic conductivity at saturation Ksat . Hydraulic conductivity at saturation is obtained from measurements in section 4.2.2.1. The relative conductivity is represented by the van Genuchten-Mualem function [343, 240], [SOURCE: Page_147]

√ h        1  m i^2 kr (s) =    s 1 − 1 − sm        ,                               (4.7) [SOURCE: Page_147]

with m = 1 − n^1 . This function can be calibrated more accurately by measuring relative conductivity. However, we believe that this measurement is not the most important, because the effects of relative conductiv- ity are less important than the pressures associated with the wettability of the medium. Parameters of the relative conductivity function depend on the shape parameter n (m = 1 − n^1 ) of the drying water retention curve Eq. (4.4) that was determined using Van Genuchten parameter defined in Ta- ble 4.4. Moreover, the relative conductivities for the different concentrations are more or less the same (Figure 4.21). [SOURCE: Page_147]

(a)                                                      (b) [SOURCE: Page_147]

Figure 4.21: Relative conductivity of (a) undisturbed and (b) disturbed media for the concentration c = 0%, c = 0.1%, c = 0.5% and c = 1%. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Therefore, the hydraulic conductivity function is described according to the van Genuchten-Mualem function with the measured conductivity at saturation and the parameters of the retention curve. [SOURCE: Page_147]

127/250 [SOURCE: Page_147]

CHAPTER 4. CHARACTERIZATION AND PARAMETERIZATION OF SOIL WATER PROPERTIES IN THE PRESENCE OF AMPHIPHILIC MATTER [SOURCE: Page_148]

4.4      Conclusion We presented the creation of rhizosphere analogue media from Fontainebleau sand and Chia seed mu- cilage for EPS concentration ranges referenced in a rhizosphere soil (c = 0%, c = 0.1%, c = 0.5 % and c = 1%). On these media, we carried out measurements to characterize its hydraulic proper- ties: measuring hydraulic conductivity at saturation, defining the drying water retention curve and defining drop infiltration trends. During these measurements, we noted that temporal constraints due to the equilibrium of the water content in the medium, as well as the time degradation of EPS by microbial activity, had to be taken into account (Appendix G). Moreover, we highlight the fact that mucilage quantities are generally rather low, which limits the number of replicates in the statistical study. Despite this, we noted that hydrophobicity in the media increased with EPS concentration and influenced the drying water retention curve: variable trend observed as a function of saturation and EPS concentration in the medium. Nevertheless, we did not observe any significant variation in hydraulic conductivity at saturation, unlike Kroener et al. [199] experiments. We suspect that the design of the medium has a strong influence on its characterization, as we observed between disturbed and undisturbed soils. Finally, we have defined hydraulic parameters for the numerical simulation of analogue rhizosphere disturbed soil and different EPS concentrations using these experiments (Table 4.4). Some parameters remain to be defined more precisely, in particular those related to the hydropho- bicity of the medium. Additional experiments are required to parameterize them as capillary rise in a stratified medium to parameterize the amplitude of repulsion, or infiltration in a stratified medium to detect possible typical sizes of fingerings. The water retention curves parameters obtained in this chapter are similar to those defined in Kroener et al. [201, 199]. However, they defined an EPS concentration thresholds illustrating notions of greater or lesser hydrophobicity (see section 5.2). In the next chapter, we will use these results to calibrate our disturbed media for simulating transfer in rhizosphere analogue media (infiltration of a drop and water uptake by a single root). [SOURCE: Page_148]

128/250 [SOURCE: Page_148]

Chapter 5 [SOURCE: Page_149]

Water transfer model in the rhizosphere in the presence of EPS [SOURCE: Page_149]

The aim of this chapter is to model water transfer in a rhizosphere analogue soil containing EPS. We propose to study two situations using two different models. These models are based on the same free energy representation of the water dynamics in the soil. The first model represents the dynamic of a drop infiltrating in a soil with different EPS concentrations. It takes into account several processes: the spreading of the drop on the surfaceof the soil, the infiltration and diffusion of the drop into the soil. The second model simulates water transfer in soil in the presence of a single root. The simulations with this model highlight the delays rewetting of the rhizosphere [366] compared to the bulk soil. The first model coupling drop spreading and infiltration on the the soil surface and drop in the soil is presented in section 5.1. The repellent energy density used in this model is calibrated depending on different EPS concentrations in the section 5.2. The coexisting equilibrium states of the saturation and the height of the drop are presented in section 5.3. This results obtained by this first model are discussed in section 5.4. The second model simulating water transfer in the soil in the presence of a single root is presented in section 5.5. Simulation of water transfer from bulk soil to the root obtained by this model are presented in section 5.6. [SOURCE: Page_149]

5.1      Model of drop infiltration in rhizosphere analogue soil We consider a planar 2D drop on a porous medium with a concentration c of amphiphilic matter. The drop is defined by a water height ζ(x, t) and the porous medium contains a mean saturation profile s(x, t) over a depth D. We define an equivalent height of water ξ(x, t) corresponding to the mean saturation to simply the study. A sketch of the considered geometry is shown in Figure 5.1. [SOURCE: Page_150]

Figure 5.1: Sketch of the considered geometry for a liquid drop on a soil with amphiphilic matter. [SOURCE: Page_150]

This situation corresponds to the wettability infiltration test used in the chapter 4. We could also related this situation to the rain drop infiltration into the soil. [SOURCE: Page_150]

5.1.1   Free energy of the drop/soil system In this study, we neglect the evaporation in the different phases. We assume an Helmholtz free energy F in the closed system. The free energy is the sum of three terms: free energy in the film F ζ , in the soil F ξ and at the interface film/soil F int : [SOURCE: Page_150]

F = F ζ + F ξ + F int .                                                 (5.1) [SOURCE: Page_150]

Each energy term F □ has an associated f □ energy density and then Z              Z h                    i F=          f dΩ =         f ζ + f ξ + f int dΩ                                     (5.2) Ω            Ω [SOURCE: Page_150]

We called wetting energy of liquid film, resp. water in porous media, the energy resulting from the liquid/substrate interaction, resp. liquid/solid matrix interaction. The wetting energy is distinguished [SOURCE: Page_150]

## 5.1. MODEL OF DROP INFILTRATION IN RHIZOSPHERE ANALOGUE SOIL

from the free surface energy related to the surface tension. In fact, the latter is non-local since it depends on the derivative of ζ or ξ. Therefore, when we neglect gravity energy, the energy density of the system is given by f = fw + f∇ .                                        (5.3) [SOURCE: Page_151]

Note that fw and f∇ are also components of the three energy, then we note fw□ and f∇ □ where □ denotes the corresponding energy. [SOURCE: Page_151]

In the next subsections, we describe the model in each phase: drop on a substrate (Section 5.1.2) and water in porous medium (Section 5.1.3) separately. Then, we present the coupled system (Section 5.1.4) with respect to the free energy of the system. [SOURCE: Page_151]

5.1.2    Drop on an impervious substrate [SOURCE: Page_151]

In this section, we present the model of a thin film liquid on a flat impervious solid substrate in the case of partial wetting. A similar model is derived for the soil in the next section. The dynamics of the thin film is based on lubrication-type model, i.e. the flow is assumed be quasi-static approximation with long wavelength hypothesis. Thin film model is based on mass conservation of the water height ζ(x, t): [SOURCE: Page_151]

∂t ζ = −∇ · J ζ                                          (5.4) [SOURCE: Page_151]

where the flux J ζ is proportional to the pressure gradient ∇P : J = Q(−∇P ). Mobility Q, for no slip boundary condition at liquid/solid interface, depends on ζ as follows: [SOURCE: Page_151]

ζ3 Q(ζ) =      .                                           (5.5) 3µ [SOURCE: Page_151]

The pressure is the sum of three terms: the hydro-static pressure, the Laplace pressure, and the Derjaguin or disjoining pressure representing the effective molecular interaction between the substrate and the free surface [98, 93]. In the context of longwave approximation, the Laplace pressure is linearized and then −P = γ∆ζ + Π(ζ).                                        (5.6) [SOURCE: Page_151]

The parameters ρ, g, µ and γ are the liquid density, the gravity, the dynamic viscosity and the liquid-gas interface tension, respectively. For submillimetric droplets, hydro-static pressure is negligible compared to the Laplace pressure or disjoining pressure. Here, we have decided to neglect hydro-static pressure, as its effect does not significantly affect drop behavior. From Eqs. (5.4) and (5.6), the governing equation of the film height can be written [257, 236] [SOURCE: Page_151]

∂t ζ + ∇ · [Q(ζ)∇ (γ∆ζ + Π(ζ))] = 0.                                 (5.7) [SOURCE: Page_151]

This equation is known as the lubrication equation [22, 21] and it is proven to reproduce thin film dy- namics for partial wetting. Partial wetting results from a competition between attractive and repulsive interaction acting at different scales between the substrate and the free surface [328, 18]. Usually, the [SOURCE: Page_151]

This ultra-thin refers initially to a precursor film of a few nanometers which is very quickly developed before a drop completely spread. In this modeling the precursor height ζp represents a cut-off scale and not necessary a pseudo partial wetting. For instance, the static contact angle is related to Π by the augmented Young-Laplace equation: Z +∞ Π(ζ)dζ = γ (cos θ − 1) .                               (5.8) ζp [SOURCE: Page_152]

where a long range attractive interaction as ζ −3 and a short range exponential repulsive interaction. Note at very short range, the interaction is again attractive allowing the existence of stable precursor film. Three parameters characterize this disjoining pressure: Π0 the pressure amplitude, b dimension- less amplitude of the repellent interaction and ζp is the precursor film height. Such an interaction between a thin film and the soil is not described in the literature in the of soil framework. 5.1.4.2     Exchange of water [SOURCE: Page_157]

In this section, we model the water flux at the interface. We define the infiltration velocity v of the drop to the porous medium proportional to the difference between the thin film and the soil pressure:               δF   δF v∝          − δζ   δξ [SOURCE: Page_157]

As in the Washburn law in a porous medium [356, 142], we introduce a linear model to represent the infiltration flux:               δF   δF v=β          −                                           (5.29) δζ   δξ where β > 0 is called transfer rate. This expression (5.29) of the infiltration velocity v is consistent with dissipate free energy property (Appendix H.1). Figure 5.4 shows the difference between the thin film and the soil pressure for uniform (ζ, ξ) profiles. It represents the water flux sign when only the wetting energy is considered. The flux of water goes from the film to the soil, if the flux density is positive. In this condition, that corresponds to the yellow area in Figure 5.4. In other words, the water in the drop phase infiltrates in the porous medium. For high saturation or an amphiphilic concentration above a critical concentration, we see the formation of a hydrophobic barrier (in blue in Figure 5.4). This formation corresponds to a region where flat film of water cannot infiltrate the medium. For example, for c = 0.8 a barrier is present for any film height when saturation in the soil is between s = 0.155 and s = 0.32. [SOURCE: Page_157]

5.1.4.3     Governing equations of the coupled drop/soil system [SOURCE: Page_157]

(5.30e) δξ [SOURCE: Page_157]

To solve PDE system Eq.(5.30), we employ a finite element approach using the C++ FEM library OOMPH-LIB [169]. For time-stepping, a backward differentiation formula (BDF) scheme of second order is used, while both the time-steps and the spatial mesh are adaptive to the dynamics. The same method is used to solve Eq.(5.13). [SOURCE: Page_157]

Before to study the coupled system, we need first to calibrate the parameters in the soil model (section 5.2). [SOURCE: Page_158]

5.2      Calibration of soil model In this section, we focus on the amphiphilic porous media defined by Eq.(5.13). We aim to calibrate the function χ(c) define in Eq.(5.21), representing the influence of the repellent part of the amphiphilic matter in a porous medium. In this section, we will focus on exopolysaccharides (EPS) in the rhi- zosphere, in particular on percolation experiments in stratified media with different concentrations of EPS. In Kroener’s work [198, 201], capillary rise experiments were carried out on sand samples with EPS similar to the rhizosphere. The studies were conducted on three ranges of media with different sand grain sizes. Their sandy media are classified as fine (0.125 − 0.2 mm in diameter), medium (0.2 − 0.355 mm) and coarse (0.63 − 1.0 mm). The 5 cm high samples are made up of a layer of approximately 5 mm of a mixture of EPS and sand representing the rhizosphere surrounded by two layers of dry untreated sand representing the average soil, without EPS. The samples were placed on suction tables with a hydraulic charge of 2.5 cm in order to fix a constant pressure at their base. This experiment shows that after five minutes of capillary rise, depending on the concentration of EPS, two behaviors exist: [SOURCE: Page_158]

i) the water rises in the medium until it reaches its capillary height, [SOURCE: Page_158]

## 5.2. CALIBRATION OF SOIL MODEL

ii) the water does not rise through the layer containing EPS. The situation ii) occurs beyond a threshold concentration ccrit . This critical concentration depends on the size grain of sand and more precisely decreases with coarser grain. This experimental result can be explained by a smaller surface area of coarse grains than of fine grains. Wettability energy is therefore lower as grain size increases. These experimental results provide a basis for calibrating the function χ(c) defined in Eq.(5.21). In the numerical simulation, only two layers are considered: one representing the base made up of untreated sand with a height of 3.8 cm and the other the layer of mixture with a height of 1.7 cm. The domain length is fixed to Lx = 55 mm and we apply Dirichlet boundary condition at z = 0 and homogeneous Neumann boundary condition at z = Lx . The wettability of the stratified medium Π(s, c, z) is governed by the wettability in each stratum defined by Eq.(5.17) as defined in Beltrame and Cajot [20]. It can be generalised by the relation, [SOURCE: Page_159]

Πw (s, c, z) = hc (s) + fhet (z)χ(c)hr (s) + hcomp (s).                   (5.31) [SOURCE: Page_159]

where fhet is a heterogeneous function representing (in dashed line) in Figure 5.6 the region of influence of the repulsive part of the EPS, [SOURCE: Page_159]

1 + tanh (100(z − 38.5)) fhet (z) =                                    .                    (5.32) 2 Here, the repellent interaction acts when fhet (z) = 1, i.e between 39 and 55 mm. Using Eq.(5.13) with the heterogeneous wettability in Eq.(5.31) and the data for the different media given in Kroener works [201, 199], we model the different range of media studied. The function χ(c) is presented in Figure 5.5. The experimental data from Kroener et al [198] are compared with our model as a coefficient influencing the repulsive forces. These data (red dot in Figure 5.5) were fitted using Eq.(5.21) (in blue line in Figure 5.5). As a result, the best fit gives us parameters ϑ = 1.418 and υ = 1.402. 1.5 Fitted curve Experimental data [SOURCE: Page_159]

5.3                           Equilibria of flat film and uniform saturation In this section, we study the different equilibria of the system (5.30) on the particular case of flat film and uniform saturation. We aim to determine the stability of steady-states according to a water height H such that H =ξ+ζ                                               (5.33) [SOURCE: Page_160]

This description is similar than the sorption isotherm of a brush studied in Hartmann et al. [166]. As the closed thermodynamics system is dissipative, equilibria of the system are minima of the free energy with the constraint Eq.(5.33). 5.3.1   Equilibria for fixed H Because of mass conservation, H is constant. In this case, the wetting free energy is only expressed by the variable ξ. Let us define φ(ξ) such as: [SOURCE: Page_161]

φ(ξ) = fw (ζ(ξ), ξ), [SOURCE: Page_161]

where ζ(ξ) = H − ξ is the water film height. Stable steady-states correspond to minima of φ or in equivalent manner, the second derivative of φ verifies (H.3), d^2 φ (ξ) = ∂ζζ fw − 2∂ζξ fw + ∂ξξ fw > 0                     (5.37) dξ 2 In Figure 5.7, we represent q     the analysis of equilibria for a fixed total water height equal to the γ capillary length H = ℓc = ρg ≈ 2.65 mm and a fine sandy soil with different EPS concentration c. In the presence of amphiphilic matter, the free energy density curve (displayed in Figure 5.7a) is modified as a function of the concentration c. The solution U$_0$ of Eq. (5.35) changes with respect to c and the initial profile in the soil. There are three case of equilibrium: [SOURCE: Page_161]

(i) There is only one global minimum which is reached when the medium is totally saturated, i.e. the compressed saturation scomp = 0.96. [SOURCE: Page_161]

(ii) There is a global minimum corresponding to situation i) and an inflection point in the dry zone around a saturation sI . [SOURCE: Page_161]

(iii) There is a global minimum corresponding to situation i) and two local extrema: a minimum in the dry zone around a saturation sm and a maximum around a saturation sM . [SOURCE: Page_161]

These solutions are in agreement with the no-flux exchange condition in Figure 5.7b. [SOURCE: Page_161]

We find numerically, a critical concentration zone around ccrit = 0.71 separating situations i) and iii). We focus the study on the case i) and iii). For low concentrations c < ccrit , corresponding to situation i), the unique solution is a minimum of the free energy F. When the concentration is greater than the critical concentration c > ccrit , there are three possible equilibrium states as describe in situation iii). In this case, we have two additional equilibria corresponding to local extrema. These steady-states varying according to c. For example, the saturation of the minimum change from sm (0.8) = 0.151 to sm (1) = 0.101 whereas the maximum change from sM (0.8) = 0.317 to sM (1) = 0.385 (see Figure 5.7). Only the two minima steady-states are stable whereas the maximum is unstable. To conclude, there exist two kinds of infiltration: a complete infiltration associated to the global minimum and a partial infiltration due to the repellent barrier (local minimum). [SOURCE: Page_162]

5.3.2       Equilibrium with respect to H We study steady-states for different concentration and different total amount of water H in the system. If initially, the porous medium is moist, i.e. the initial saturation is large than sM (c), the equilibrium does not depend on c. In other words, equilibria U$_0$ after an infiltration into a wetted porous medium do not depend on the amphiphilic concentration c. These steady-states correspond to a minimum of the free energy density. Note that the profile h^0 increases linearly with H once the saturation s^0 has reached a minimum energy. In the case of dry initial porous medium, steady-states depend on the amphiphilic concentration c. Figure 5.8 displayed the simulate equilibrium with respect to H and c. For low height of water H < ϕD, there is not enough water to saturate the soil. As long as c < 0.7, the equilibrium saturation increases linearly with the height H (Figure 5.8b). Further increasing, the height of water H > ϕD, the soil is always saturated, for low concentration c ≤ 0.71. The energy density of these porous medium have only the global minimum as equilibrium. Since the soil is saturated, the height of the flat film increases linearly with the height H as shown in Figure 5.8a. Equilibrium saturation corresponds to the lower boundary of the hydrophobic barrier shown in blue in Figure 5.4d. This boundary is the local minimum defined by sm (c).Moreover, equilibrium height of [SOURCE: Page_162]

the flat film increases linearly with respect to the height H. As for the fixed height of water, the model reproduces two infiltration kinds of uniform saturation and a flat film of water depending of the amphiphilic concentration. Stable equilibria are used to re- produce the initial soil profile before the drop is deposited. In the following, we focus on the infiltration of a drop in a soil in presence of EPS. [SOURCE: Page_163]

5.4            Drop infiltration In this section, we numerically simulate the spreading and the infiltration of a water drop on a fine sandy soil (grain size about 200 µm) with an EPS concentration c. The volume per length of water drop is fixed 20.9 mm^2 and only the initial saturation of sandy soil and the EPS concentration c vary. [SOURCE: Page_163]

5.4.1          Wet porous medium [SOURCE: Page_163]

We consider a pre-wetted fine sandy soil with a mean saturation s = 0.8 with a EPS concentration c = 0.8. According to the previous study the equilibrium state corresponds to an uniform saturation. We then place a drop on the surface (20.9 mm^2 volume per length) and study its dynamics. Note that it can eventually infiltrate completely without saturating the soil. [SOURCE: Page_163]

The drop spreads rapidly over the substrate until it reaches the domain boundaries. This very fast dynamic takes about 0.1 s and the infiltration is negligible (see Fig. 5.9). The profile of the thin film of liquid is still drop-shaped: the drop height goes from 3 to 1 mm. Because of the boundary conditions, the water no longer spreads out, but the profile gradually flattens out over the course of a second. At the same time, the water gradually percolates down. Then the saturation reaches the capillary compressibility barrier at t about 3 s. The dynamics converges to the same equilibrium computed in the previous section for the same total water height H = 2.246 mm. A wet sandy soil without EPS (c = 0) and the same initial saturation behaves in a similar way, both qualitatively and quantitatively. As a result, soil moisture hides the effects of the amphiphilic matter, and the soil surface behaves like a substrate with complete wetting properties. [SOURCE: Page_163]

Equilibrium profiles of the liquid in each phase are displayed in Figure 5.10. These equilibria depends on concentration c. For c ≤ 0.7 the drop infiltrates almost completely in the porous medium (see Figure 5.10a). The remaining volume fraction increases with concentration but remains below 12.6%. At low saturation (c = 0.1), the water infiltrates uniformly, whereas at higher saturation (c ≥ 0.6), infiltration occurs essentially at the vertical of the drop. In the latter case, the equilibrium state is formed by a dry/wet structure corresponding to pseudo-partial diffusion (see Figure 5.10b). This type of structure was shown for hydrophobic soil in [20]. At c = 0.8 and larger concentration, the drop fails to infiltrate. It is stopped by the hydrophobic barrier formed in the soil. For instance, the saturation at equilibrium for a concentration of c = 0.8 is about s = 0.1 corresponding to the hydrophobic barrier boundary in Figure 5.4d. Figure 5.11 shows that the infiltration rate increases with the concentration during the early stages. The imbibition duration increases with the concentration c. Moreover, depending on c there are several stages associated to different infiltration rates. A break in slope occurs at t = 1 s for c = 0.6 resulting [SOURCE: Page_164]

We detail in Figure 5.12 the drop and saturation profiles for c = 0.7 at different times stages. Initially, the drop spreads rapidly, t = 10 ms, over the substrate almost without infiltrating. Then, during the second stage, the radius of the drop decreases as it infiltrates vertically into the soil with little lateral diffusion. Infiltration is almost stopped as shown by the plateau of the infiltration curve in Figure 5.11. This behavior is due to the hydrophobic barrier in the hydrophobic saturation range around s = 0.12 as shown in Figure 5.12b. Once the hydrophobic saturation range has passed, infiltration is faster during the third and last stages than the second. At the end of the last stage, the drop is imbibated and the equilibrium state is reached after 35 s. There is a factor 1000 between the characteristic time of infiltration and the spreading. In a sandy soil with c = 0.1, we have an IDA stage corresponding to a spreading behavior during 0.14 second. During this step, the contact angle goes from 60° to 11° and the drop radius from 4.19 to 11 mm. After the spreading stage, the drop starts a DDA stage where the infiltration drives the dynamic until the total imbibition in the substrate after 6.6 seconds. During this step, the contact angle decreases slightly until the equilibrium state corresponding to the complete imbibition. From this study, the spreading times of the drop are lower than a millisecond for c = 0.6 and c = 0.7. It is noteworthy that these infiltration times are shorter than for soils with a concentration of c = 0.1. We believe that the hydrophobicity of the soil is the cause of this phenomenon, since it reduces drop spreading.In contrast, the infiltration time into the substrate is shorter for low concentrations, going from 6.6 seconds for c = 0.1 to 35 seconds for c = 0.7. These trends are consistent with the experimentally observed dynamics. Our model is able to reproduce different experimental observation of infiltration dynamics in dry soil. We now examine infiltration on pre-wetted soil. [SOURCE: Page_166]

5.4.3                        Pre-wetted porous medium In this section, we simulate the experimental work that we have carried out and those of Charles- Williams [73]: deposit a second drop on a medium previously wetted by a first drop. Thus, we consider a soil previously wet by a first drop of 20.9 mm^2 . The initial saturation profile in soil corresponds to the equilibrium profile found in Section 5.4.2 after imbibition of the first drop on dry soil. The infiltration behavior of the second drop is similar to the first: if the drop has not infiltrated, the second drop does not infiltrate, otherwise it infiltrates. Thus, for c > 0.8, the drop infiltrates [SOURCE: Page_166]

5.4.4   Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_168]

The infiltration model developed in section 5.1 reproduces different types of infiltration corresponding to the literature on powdery media [82, 73, 228, 96, 177]. It would be interesting to compare this model with spreading dynamics on brush polymers [166], or with other empirical laws such as Tanner’s law [320]. Nevertheless, this model offers a new opportunity to better understand the influence of amphiphilic matter on infiltration phenomena in a porous medium. In addition, it illustrates that this transfer model in the presence of amphiphilic matter is relevant to the study of water transfer behaviors in the rhizosphere in the presence of a root. [SOURCE: Page_168]

5.5      Water transfer around a single root The aim of this section is to model water transfer between bulk soil and a root, passing through the rhizosphere. To achieve this, we propose to consider a horizontal soil fraction with a root where we distinguish: bulk and rhizosphere soil (white dashed line in Figure 5.16). The rhizosphere soil contain an EPS concentration from plant due to root activity unlike the bulk soil. However, the bulk soil can contain an Extracellullar Polymeric Substances concentration due to microorganism in stress [254]. [SOURCE: Page_168]

5.5.1   Model of soil around a single root [SOURCE: Page_168]

In this study, we assume that Extracellullar Polymeric Substances are similar to the EPS effect. Thus, the EPS concentration in the bulk soil is lower than a critical concentration Ccrit . This concentration represents a boundary between bulk and rhizosphere. In this study, we define the rhizosphere from root radius rroot to the rhizosphere radius rrhizo and a bulk soil from the rhizosphere radius rrhizo to the bulk radius rbulk . The rhizosphere contains a concentration of EPS that varies with the distance between the soil and the root. Then we consider an EPS concentration c depending on the space r. As the EPS concentration is decreasing from the root exuded concentration cmax to the microor- ganism in stress Ccrit in the rhizosphere, EPS concentration decreases with increasing distance from the root (grey area in Figure 5.16). Therefore, the concentration distribution c(r) in the rhizosphere [SOURCE: Page_168]

## 5.6. HUMECTATION OF THE SOIL

We use the same parameters and functions to represent energies and hydraulic conductivity as in section 5.1.3 but we add in the free energy an osmotic energy fosm , i.e [SOURCE: Page_171]

f (s, c) = fw (s, c) + f∇ (s) + fosm (s, c).                      (5.49) [SOURCE: Page_171]

The osmotic energy represents the swelling property of the EPS, i.e it represents the ability to store more water in the soil in presence of EPS. This property represents the hydrophilic effects of the amphiphilic matter. We propose to model this effect as acting only at high saturation, since at low saturation we find the hydrophobic property of EPS. In addition, hydrophilic effect increases with respect to the EPS concentration. Therefore, the osmotic energy is a function such that [SOURCE: Page_171]

• the osmotic pressure hosm is an increasing function with respect to s and c; [SOURCE: Page_171]

• the osmotic effect are neglected for low saturation; [SOURCE: Page_171]

• there is no osmotic effect for a soil without EPS (c = 0). [SOURCE: Page_171]

In this study, we assume a theoretical expression of the osmotic pressure hosm , [SOURCE: Page_171]

δfosm hosm (s, c) =         (s, c) = c (exp(s) − 1) .                     (5.50) δs [SOURCE: Page_171]

Note that this function require some experimental calibration model a specific situation. Moreover, since we neglect the degradation of the amphiphilic matter, the distribution of the EPS concentration remains unchanged over time and the repellent and osmotic property of EPS depend only the initial distribution of the EPS. In the case of soil humectation near a root, the soil is model using specific boundary condition far the root/rhizosphere interface. We propose to impose a saturation and pressure on the bulk soil (r = rbulk ) corresponding to an equilibrium far from the root. Moreover, when the soil is very dry, we can consider that the root imposes no suction, i.e. we impose non-flow conditions on the root (r = rroot ). To solve PDE system Eq.(5.48), we employ a finite element approach on the weak formulation (see appendix I) using the C++ FEM library OOMPH-LIB [169]. For time-stepping, a backward differen- tiation formula (BDF) scheme of second order is used, while both the time-steps and the spatial mesh are adaptive to the dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_171]

5.6      Humectation of the soil In this context of rhizophere humectation, we propose to present two simulations: a first one without taking into account the amphiphilic matter [290, 183, 111] and a second one considering the spatial distribution of the amphiphilic matter. In the first simulation, we start with a dry profile close to the root and with a high saturation of 80% at r = rbulk (Figure 5.18a). The imbibition front approaches the root over time: it arrives in the rhizosphere after 3.6 s (Figure 5.18b), then passes through an intermediate point (black dashed line in [SOURCE: Page_171]

5.7      Conclusion Two water transfer models were proposed in this chapter: first the coupling between a water droplet and the soil, taking into account surface interactions, and then water transfer around a single root. These models are based on the same construction of water transfer in the rhizosphere in the presence of EPS. Using the same free energy construct, we were able to reproduce different behaviors found in the literature, such as the dynamics of infiltration and spreading of a drop on the soil surface, slow diffusion in the presence of EPS, capillary rise in stratified media and delayed wetting of the rhizosphere. The free energy concept has allowed us not only to build a thermodynamically coherent model, but also to facilitate the formulation of process coupling. In particular, the coupled influence of wettability between a thin film of liquid on the surface and a porous medium could not have been taken into account by working only on the pressure terms. In this way, the notion of free energy is a key factor in the construction of transfer dynamics in soils in the presence of amphiphilic matter. These models offer new ways of representing water transfer and coupling processes. Therefore it is an encouraging view to better understanding water processes in presence of EPS. [SOURCE: Page_174]

154/250 [SOURCE: Page_174]

Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_175]

The final objective of this work was to propose a soil transfer model taking into account the presence of amphiphilic matter. This work was carried out in four stages: [SOURCE: Page_175]

1) The development of a water transfer model based on the free energy of water in the soil. This model provides new parameters to describe the macroscopic processes at work between water, porous matrix and organic matter. [SOURCE: Page_175]

2) The implementation of numerical tools to simulate the temporal evolution of the developed models. We also provide analysis tools to study water transfer behaviors and the detection of thresholds. [SOURCE: Page_175]

3) Characterization of hydraulic properties in the presence of amphiphilic matter in a rhizosphere analogue medium, to understand the role of this substance. We also parameterize the hydraulic functions related to this kind of medium. [SOURCE: Page_175]

4) The development of models able to represent water transfers in a rhizosphere analogue medium in the presence of amphiphilic matter, such as infiltration and humectation near the root. [SOURCE: Page_175]

We clarified first the ambiguity between capillary pressure and thermodynamic potential in soils: capillary pressure is the functional derivative of free energy density. Then we noted that capillary pressure is often associated with soil wettability by taking into account a contact angle Θ and the related capillary height. However, contact angle or capillary height do not allow to reproduce flows specific to the presence of organic matter such as preferential flows or water retention. In contrast, the free energy provides a general framework to derive a model of gradient dynamics as it is done in chapter 2. Moreover, to take into account all components acting on the water, the total free energy is the sum of the different components taken separately. But this reasoning cannot be applied to pressure. We define two new terms in the free energy: i) the wetting energy and ii) the free surface energy. i) The wetting energy represents the interaction energy between water and the soil matrix. Both attractive and repellent interactions are included. We derive a wetting pressure generalizing the capillary pressure. The integration of repellent term in free energy is not documented in the literature and it is a key term of the improvement of the model. ii) The surface energy was already introduced in the literature. In our context, it plays a regularizing role in modeling hydrophobic media. The constructed wettability introduces a non-monotonic curve, reproducing different dynamics during wetting/drying cycles. This allows us to reproduce hysteresis phenomenon during water transfer in soil. Thus, we have provided an alternative to the relaxation time models for modeling hysteresis and wetting phenomenon in the presence of organic matter [201]. [SOURCE: Page_175]

## CONCLUSION

Using this concept, we have developed a new model of water transfer in the presence of hydrophobic matter. This model is a highly non-linear fourth-order PDE that requires numerical methods to study the dynamics of water transfer. We began by developing our own tools in Matlab, using the proposed numerical schemes, such as taking into account explicitly the destabilizing term that occurs in the presence of hydrophobic terms. However, to reduce computation time and optimize the algorithm, we switched to a finite element library Oomph-lib. This library offers adaptive time-stepping and meshes for irregular dynamics as intermittent flow in 1D and 2D. In addition, a first version of continuation code has been developed to track water behavior for different situations and scenarios. These numerical developments associated with this free energy model highlighted several transfer dynamics: fingering, water trapping, intermittency. Then, we carried out hydraulic experiments on rhizosphere analogue media to characterize it with respect to the concentration of amphiphilic matter. This characterization enabled us to extract a first range of parameters to model a rhizospheric soil. Further experiments are required to extract all the parameters for modeling, in particular the influence of amphiphilic matter concentration on the hydrophobicity of the medium. Once we had calibrated our model on the basis of our experiments and literature data, we were able to obtain results applied to the rhizosphere, such as drop-soil exchanges, infiltration dynamics and humectation delay in the rhizosphere. These results are consistent with the literature, but require further experiments to be completely calibrated. We correlated the retention curve for different EPS concentration c with a reference medium (c = 0%). We adjusted the free energy of the hydrophobic part with our water retention experiments and experiment capillary rise in a stratified medium with EPS literature experiments [201]. However, we have not adjusted the hydrophilic part in relation to EPS swelling. Taking into account the calibration of this energy should better simulate the water storage capacity in the rhizosphere as defined in the literature [288, 280, 130, 302]. A first outlook in the continuity of this work is the calibration of the osmotic effect of EPS in contact with water by modifying the wetting energy density. An increasing of the hydrophilic term in the saturation s range near 1 could reproduce the osmotic effect near saturation. Moreover experiments could be realize to calibrate more accurately the law using to represent the hydrophobic effect of EPS concentration on the water transfer or define the EPS degradation (project MucilageMatter submit). It is also possible to define experiments to calibrate surface tension, such as measuring the typical size between two fingering. Moreover, the analogy between multiphase media and porous media can be discussed to define this tension. Indeed, a more complex expressions could be considered as proposes by Beljadid et al [17]. Another perspective of this work would be to provide more continuation tools to study behavior in several contexts than the current implementation (periodic boundary condition in 1D). An improvement to our continuation tools with the PDE2PATH library would be to develop a version with Dirichlet or Neumann boundary conditions in 1D and 2D. In this way, a new tool, not currently available in our laboratory, would be developed to study stationary saturation states in the bulk soil and rhizosphere around a root under different wetting conditions. Moreover, the library trullekrul developed under MATLAB would be used to adapt the mesh to our solution obtained with a finite element method. Finally, this free energy approach appears to be a potential candidate for modeling coupling pro- cesses in the soil. This type of construction would make it possible to associate an energy with each [SOURCE: Page_176]

156/250 [SOURCE: Page_176]

process to define a global energy for the system. For example, it could be possible to modeling the dynamics of EPS degradation with soil transfer as biofilm model proposed by Trinschek et al. [336, 337] and coupled it with the soil transfer model developed during this thesis. Finally, to couple all these dynamics with the root uptake. Integrated into a broader framework, at the scale of a root sys- tem [110, 299], this modelling will contribute to the analysis of rhizospheric traits likely to improve water use by plants in a context of climate change and practices. In another context, this model could be used to study the transport of substances to remediate soil pollution. For this, it would be interesting to introduce energies associated with diffusiophoresis, as proposed in the TRACE-it project: Controlling particle flow driven by local concentration gradients in geological porous media. Therefore, this work opens up a new way of coupling the processes associated with soil water transfer in the presence of amphiphilic matter. [SOURCE: Page_177]

Appendix A [SOURCE: Page_181]

Numerical time scheme [SOURCE: Page_181]

The aim of this chapter is to present several time scheme and prediction-correction methods used in this manuscript. Let’s define Ω a domain in RN with N the dimension of the domain and a time interval [0, T]. We consider a physical phenomenon represented by the function y(x, t) in the domain during the time interval, i.e the spatial coordinates are given by x ∈ Ω and the time duration by t ∈ [0, T]. This phenomenon is express by the general formulation of differential equation, [SOURCE: Page_181]

ẏ(x, t) = ∂t y(x, t) = f (y(x, t), t),                             (A.1) [SOURCE: Page_181]

where y(x, 0) = y^0 (x) is the initial condition, f is a smooth (linear or non-linear) function defined on Ω × [0, T] and satisfying the Lipschitz condition. To simplify the notations, the spatial part is neglected and we concentrate on solving the EDO from Eq. (A.1): ẏ(t) = f (y(t), t).                                (A.2) [SOURCE: Page_181]

A.1       Time scheme A numerical time scheme is used to numerically approximate the solution at each time t. These schemes are used to solve problems where the analytical solution is not known. The finite difference method is used to generate our numerical schemes. This method is based on Taylor series applied in a discrete domain. [SOURCE: Page_181]

Definition A.1.1. Let’s a function y is infinitely differentiable at a real or complex number t^1 . The Taylor series of this function is a power series: ∞ X y (k) (t^1 ) y(t) =                   (t − t^1 )k ,                            (A.3) k! k=0 [SOURCE: Page_181]

where k! denotes the factorial of k, y (k) (t^1 ) denotes the k th derivative of y evaluated at the point t^1 . [SOURCE: Page_181]

A subdivision of time is assumed 0 = t^0 < t^1 < t^2 < · · · < tm = T such that [0, T] = ∪m−1 i=0 [ti , ti+1 ]. This subdivision forms a temporal mesh in which each point constitutes a discretization node. The [SOURCE: Page_181]

## APPENDIX A. NUMERICAL TIME SCHEME

discretization step between the mesh nodes on the interval [ti , ti+1 ] is defined by dtn such that tn+1 = dtn + tn . A numerical scheme allows the calculation of an approximation of the solution at time tn+1 , denoted yn+1 , and an approximation error is made between the exact formulation of y(tn+1 ) and yn+1 . This error is called the local truncation error (LTE), i.e. [SOURCE: Page_182]

LT E = yn − y(tn ).                                         (A.4) [SOURCE: Page_182]

This error is considered of order q, if yn is of the form form is: for all n, l ∈ [0, m], n ̸= l, [SOURCE: Page_182]

q−1 (k) X  y yn =           l (tn − tl )k .                              (A.5) k! k=0 [SOURCE: Page_182]

The LTE is therefore of the form ∞ X   (k) y                                      LT E =         l (tn − tl )k ≈ O (tn − tl )q+1 .                        (A.6) k! k=q [SOURCE: Page_182]

In the rest of this chapter, we consider that the approximation is given for all n ∈ [0, m], [SOURCE: Page_182]

y(tn ) ≈ yn .                                          (A.7) [SOURCE: Page_182]

A.1.1      Time scheme at one step [SOURCE: Page_182]

A one-step scheme is a time scheme that depends only on the previous instant. By applying the Taylor series given by Eq.(A.5), the exact behaviour of the physical phenomenon at time tn+1 is naturally given as a function of time tn by the relation: ∞ X y (k) (tn ) y(tn+1 ) =                     dtkn ,                           (A.8) k! k=0 [SOURCE: Page_182]

or ∞ X y (k) (tn+1 ) y(tn+1 ) = y(tn ) −                        (−dtn )k .                   (A.9) k! k=1 [SOURCE: Page_182]

The one-step schemes are linear combinations of its two exact expressions. Because we are trying to solve Eq. A.1, the schemes that we need to use are limited to the function y and its first derivative. Let θ ∈ [0, 1], we define the family of θ-schemes: [SOURCE: Page_182]

yn+1 = yn + dtn [(θ − 1) ẏn + θẏn+1 ] .                            (A.10) [SOURCE: Page_182]

There are two main categories of scheme: [SOURCE: Page_182]

• explicit schemes that depend only on known instants, in this case tn ; [SOURCE: Page_182]

• implicit schemes depending on the future moment tn+1 . [SOURCE: Page_182]

## A.1. TIME SCHEME

In the family of θ schemes, this differentiation is made according to the value of θ. If θ = 0, then the scheme is explicit, otherwise the scheme is implicit. Among these schemes, we find the classically used Euler schemes: [SOURCE: Page_183]

• Forward Euler scheme (FE), which is an explicite scheme [SOURCE: Page_183]

yn+1 = yn + dtn ẏn .                               (A.11) [SOURCE: Page_183]

• Backward Euler scheme (BE), which is an implicite scheme [SOURCE: Page_183]

yn+1 = yn + dtn ẏn+1 .                                 (A.12) [SOURCE: Page_183]

The particularity of these schemes is their simplicity to develop, but their order of accuracy is low. They are first-order methods, with a LTE, [SOURCE: Page_183]

dt^2n LT E F E = −          ÿn + O(dt^3n )                           (A.13) 2 and dt^2n LT E BE =           ÿn + O(dt^3n ).                            (A.14) 2 Generally, θ schemes are simple to use but have a low order of accuracy, except for the Crank- Nicolson scheme, also known as the Trapezoid rule (TR) [180]. This scheme is of order 2, when the temporal mesh is uniform, so θ = 21 , [SOURCE: Page_183]

dtn yn+1 = yn +           [ẏn + ẏn+1 ] ,                          (A.15) 2 with a LTE, dt^3n ... LT E T R =          y + O(dt^4n )                               (A.16) 12 n In order to have wider range of schemes with better accuracy, it is a smart strategy to turn to multi-step schemes. [SOURCE: Page_183]

A.1.2     Multi-step time scheme [SOURCE: Page_183]

Multi-step schemes are schemes that depend on the previous time instants tn−p , with n ≥ p ≥ 0. Let’s introduce K$_1$ ≥ 1, K$_2$ ≥ 0 and K = max (K$_1$ , K$_2$ ). We consider two real sequences {αj }j∈I$_1$ and {βj }j∈I$_2$ where I$_1$ = {1, . . . , K$_1$ } and I$_2$ = {0, . . . , K$_2$ }. The linear multi-step method is a K-step method given for all n ≥ K [45, 51, 305, 154]: [SOURCE: Page_183]

K$_1$ X                    K$_2$ X yn =         αj yn−j + dt         βj ẏn−j .                     (A.17) j=1                  j=0 [SOURCE: Page_183]

There exists two main classes of methods: Adams methods and Backward differential formula (BDF). [SOURCE: Page_183]

## APPENDIX A. NUMERICAL TIME SCHEME

Adams methods [SOURCE: Page_184]

schemes based on the Adams method are divided into two categories: Adams-Bashforth schemes and Adams-Moulton schemes. Adams-Bashforth schemes are explicit schemes of order q written as q X yn = yn−j + dt           βj ẏn−j .                       (A.18) j=1 [SOURCE: Page_184]

The form of this class of scheme is in the form of a multi-step linear scheme with K$_1$ = 1 (and α1 = 1), K$_2$ = q and β0 = 0. The FE scheme is a first-order scheme from this class, denoted AB$_1$. schemes of higher order than 1 are present, such as AB$_2$ [305], which is a scheme of order 2, defined by:                 yn+1 − yn             dtn                    1 =       1+               ẏn −          ẏn−1 ,           (A.19) dtn               2dtn−1                2dtn−1 [SOURCE: Page_184]

with a LTE, dt^2n                  ... LT E AB$_2$ =        (2dtn + 3dtn−1 ) y n + O(dt^4n ).                  (A.20) 12 Adams-Moulton schemes are implicit schemes of order q written as [SOURCE: Page_184]

q−1 X yn = yn−j + dt           βj ẏn−j .                       (A.21) j=0 [SOURCE: Page_184]

This form corresponds to a multi-step linear scheme with K$_1$ = 1 (and α1 = 1), K$_2$ = q − 1 and β0 > 0. This class of scheme includes the BE and TR schemes, called AM$_1$ and AM$_2$ respectively. [SOURCE: Page_184]

BDF methods [SOURCE: Page_184]

The BDF schemes [146, 172, 173] are implicit schemes of order q written q X yn =         αj yn−j .                               (A.22) j=1 [SOURCE: Page_184]

This form corresponds to a multi-step linear scheme with K$_1$ = q, K$_2$ = 0 and β0 > 0. The BE scheme is a first-order scheme in this class, denoted BDF$_1$. Note that the BE scheme is a member not only of this class but also of the Adams-Moulton class. Higher-order schemes are present in this class, such as the BDF$_2$ scheme [70], which is a second-order scheme, [SOURCE: Page_184]

yn+1 − yn        dtn    yn − yn−1    dtn + dtn−1 =                       +              ẏn+1 .                  (A.23) dtn      2dtn + dtn−1 dtn−1      2dtn + dtn−1 [SOURCE: Page_184]

Other scheme [SOURCE: Page_184]

Linear multi-step schemes are not limited to these two main classes of schemes. In these two classes, only Adams-Bashforth schemes are explicit schemes. ## A.2. PREDICTION-CORRECTION METHODS

generalised Leapfrog scheme,            2 !                     2                               dtn                       dtn                              dtn yn+1 =    1−                      yn +                    yn−1 + dtn       1+             ẏn ,   (A.24) dtn−1                     dtn−1                            dtn−1 [SOURCE: Page_185]

with a LTE,                                                               dt^3             dtn−1         ... LT E    Leapf rog = n           1−               y n + O(dt^4n ).                     (A.25) 6               dtn This scheme is an explicit scheme of order 2. [SOURCE: Page_185]

A.2       Prediction-correction methods Prediction-correction methods are iterative methods used to define the future time step once yn+1 is obtained. In order to better follow the physical dynamics represented by Eq. (A.1), these methods are based on the prediction and correction of the time step dtn+1 . [SOURCE: Page_185]

A.2.1    General principle The principle of these methods consists in the prediction of a time step using the estimation of the expression of an implicit scheme’s LTE depending on the expression of an explicite scheme of the same order. The prediction is considered correct if the matrix system associated with Eq. (A.1) converges at the next iteration, otherwise the time step must be corrected by reducing it. To prevent multiple corrections, it is important to predict the future time step. We explain below how the prediction principle works. An explicit scheme is used to predict the solution of Eq. (A.1), this solution is denoted yn+1 P . This [SOURCE: Page_185]

solution is used to solve the matrix system associated with the problem based on an implicit scheme and define the solution yn+1 . However, an approximation error is generated using this implicit scheme. In order to reduce the error at the next iteration, the LTE of the implicit scheme is expressed as a function of the predicted solution yn+1 P   and the solution yn+1 , at time tn+1 . A constraint is defined in order to establish our estimate and remain within a finite and bounded error formulation, [SOURCE: Page_185]

||dn || ≤ ϵymax ,                                              (A.26) [SOURCE: Page_185]

where || · || is a norm, ϵ is a tolerance and ymax is the maximum of yn+1 , ∀n ≥ 0. In addition to this constraint, the ratio of the norms of the LTEs over two iterations is expressed as a function of the previous discretisation steps by a function h, [SOURCE: Page_185]

||dn || = h(dtn+1 , dtn , . . . , dt^1 ).                                  (A.27) ||dn−1 || [SOURCE: Page_185]

Using these two expressions, we determine the relationship [SOURCE: Page_185]

||dn || = h(dtn+1 , dtn , . . . , dt^1 )||dn−1 || ≤ ϵymax . [SOURCE: Page_185]

From this last relationship, we estimate the future discretisation step dtn+1 . [SOURCE: Page_185]

Now we will do the case disjunction on s: [SOURCE: Page_198]

Ω             Ω              Ω [SOURCE: Page_198]

Ω                 Ω              Ω        Γin [SOURCE: Page_198]

Appendix D [SOURCE: Page_199]

Numerical resolution in time [SOURCE: Page_199]

Solving the matrix problem, Eq. (3.9), is performed using numerical solution algorithms. [SOURCE: Page_199]

There are two main categories for solving the system numerically: direct methods and iterative methods. [SOURCE: Page_199]

The direct method to solve a linear system is an algorithm whichwould give the solution in a finite number of operations. There are also iterative methods which involve constructing a sequence of vectors ukn converging towards the solution un+1 , i.e. when the number of iterations k → +∞ then ukn → un+1 . [SOURCE: Page_199]

In our case, the problem is non-linear. Iterative methods are used to define the solution. Figure 3.8 represents the diagram associated with solving the matrix system using iterative methods. [SOURCE: Page_199]

An iterative algorithm is developed to define the solution at time tn+1 , this means using a resolution loop of k iterations. The initialisation of this algorithm is based on the solution at time tn . The solution obtained at the first iteration u^0n is defined using an explicit scheme to approximate the solution at time tn+1 . Therefore, it is denoted u^0n = uPn+1 . [SOURCE: Page_199]

The resolution loop continues to run until the profile has converged, i.e. until the residual r = b(u) − A(u)δu obtained from the matrix resolution has a norm less than a sufficiently small ϵ tolerance. [SOURCE: Page_199]

However, limiting the algorithm to this single criterion means that it may converge after a high number of iterations, i.e imposing a very long resolution time, or diverge because the choice of time step is incorrect (section 3.3.2). In order to prevent these two situations, a finite number of iterations kitermax is imposed. Thus, if the norm of the residual is less than ϵ and k < kitermax then the iterative solution algorithm has converged and un+1 = ukn . If k > kitermax and the norm of the residual is greater than ϵ then the system has not converged. [SOURCE: Page_199]

Now that we have presented the general principle of the iterative algorithm, it is necessary to choose a resolution algorithm to establish the value of the residual. In the remainder of this section, we propose to focus on the Picard and Newton methods. [SOURCE: Page_199]

179 [SOURCE: Page_199]

## APPENDIX D. NUMERICAL RESOLUTION IN TIME

D.1       Picard method Picard method is an iterative method for solving non-linear systems of the form [208]: [SOURCE: Page_200]

∂u F (u) =      − ∇ · (α(u)∇u) − f (u) = 0,     in Ω × (0, T).                   (D.1) ∂t [SOURCE: Page_200]

This system can be expressed in a matrix form as mentioned in section 3.2, [SOURCE: Page_200]

A(u)u = b(u)                                         (D.2) [SOURCE: Page_200]

Eq. (D.2) is solved in linearized form starting from an initial value of u. Let u− be this initial value and u the solution of the system Eq. (D.1) at time t > 0. In other words, this method solves a problem of the form: F̂ (u, u− ) = 0,                                        (D.3) [SOURCE: Page_200]

where F̂ is the linear function of F . It is defined by [SOURCE: Page_200]

∂u                F̂ (u, u− ) =      − ∇ · α(u− )∇u − f (u− ).                           (D.4) ∂t [SOURCE: Page_200]

This formulation is governed by a matrix form such that Eq. (D.3) is expressed in the form of Eq. (D.2), A(u− )u = b(u− ).                                   (D.5) [SOURCE: Page_200]

The matrix A(u− ) and the vector b(u− ) are the matrix forms defined in section 3.2 and described according to the time scheme chosen in Appendix B. Thus, the resolution required to use Picard method is to solve the given matrix form without modifying the system. During the iterations of the solution, the computed solution can be very different from the solution predicted by the explicit scheme. To prevent this, we use a relaxation method. A relaxation method adapts the solution u⋆ obtained at each iterationby making it depend on the solution obtained at the previous iteration. In this way, the evolution of the solution is not too violent. For this reason, the solution defined after each iteration is given by the relationship: [SOURCE: Page_200]

u = u⋆ ω + (1 − ω)u− .                                      (D.6) [SOURCE: Page_200]

In our study, we consider ω = 1. This means that we solve F̂ (u⋆ , u− ) = 0, i.e. A(u− )u⋆ = b(u− ) which defines the solution of the system, governed by Eq. (D.2), given by Eq. (D.6). A general algorithm associated with Picard method is given below. This algorithm is one of the versions developed and tested to solve our system governed by Eq. (3.5). [SOURCE: Page_200]

D.2       Newton method Newton method, like Picard method, can be used to solve a system in the form of Eq. (D.1). However, this method requires the use of the Jacobian. The solution is approximated by estimating the tangent [SOURCE: Page_200]

## D.2. NEWTON METHOD

Algorithm for relaxed Picard iterations [SOURCE: Page_201]

Initialization: u− = uPn+1 : initial guess r = A(u− )u− − b(u− ) ω relaxed value while ||r|| > ϵ do solve A(u− )u⋆ = b(u− ) u = u⋆ ω + (1 − ω)u− u− ← u r = A(u− )u− − b(u− ) end while [SOURCE: Page_201]

Output: un+1 = u− solution of equation (D.2) [SOURCE: Page_201]

of the function F at the solution u. In other words, we are trying to solve the following system: [SOURCE: Page_201]

Du F (u) = 0.                                          (D.7) [SOURCE: Page_201]

This weakly formulated system can be expressed in matrix form, [SOURCE: Page_201]

J(u)u = r(u),                                          (D.8) [SOURCE: Page_201]

where r(u) = A(u)u − b(u) is the residual of the system governed by Eq. (D.2) and J is the Jacobian matrix associated with the differentiable form of F at u, Du F . [SOURCE: Page_201]

Eq. (D.8) is solved in linearized form from a value of u. Using the same notations as those defined in Picard method, this amounts to solve [SOURCE: Page_201]

Du F̂ (u, u− ) = 0.                                      (D.9) [SOURCE: Page_201]

This formulation is governed by a matrix form such that Eq. (D.3) is expressed in the form of Eq. (D.8), J(u− )u = r(u− ).                                  (D.10) [SOURCE: Page_201]

The matrix J(u− ) depends on the time scheme chosen. [SOURCE: Page_201]

The relaxation method, given by Eq. (D.6), is also used for Newton method. Like Picard method, this method allows us to remain in the neighbourhood of the solution predicted by an explicit scheme and not to diverge or converge towards a solution that does not correspond to our dynamics. [SOURCE: Page_201]

This means that we solve Du F̂ (u⋆ , u− ) = 0, i.e. J(u− )u⋆ = r(u− ) in order to define the solution of the system, governed by Eq. (D.8), given by Eq. (D.6). [SOURCE: Page_201]

A general algorithm associated with Newton method is given below. It is one of the versions developed and tested to solve our system governed by Eq. (3.5). [SOURCE: Page_201]

## APPENDIX D. NUMERICAL RESOLUTION IN TIME

Algorithm for relaxed Newton iterations [SOURCE: Page_202]

Initialization: u− = uPn+1 : initial guess r = A(u− )u− − b(u− ) ω relaxed value while ||r|| > ϵ do solve J(u− )u⋆ = r u = u⋆ ω + (1 − ω)u− u− ← u r = A(u− )u− − b(u− ) end while [SOURCE: Page_202]

Output: un+1 = u− [SOURCE: Page_202]

D.3       Synthesis Our system governed by Eq. (3.5) is iteratively solved. The Picard and Newton algorithms have been presented to solve it. Both methods use a number of iterations to converge to the solution depending on the choice of the first element of the sequence at initialization time. Naturally, the solution must prealably approximated using an explicit scheme. In addition, both methods have advantages and disadvantages. Picard method is fast to implement and requires relatively small storage space. The matrix form defined by Eq. (3.9) is directly reused in the Picard method. However, its resolution time is relatively long because of the large number of iterations required for the system to converge. Newton method requires the Jacobian matrix of the system to be defined. The construction of this matrix requires more time and storage than Picard method. However, its convergence time is relatively faster due to its low number of iterations. Depending on the user’s needs for solving the system, both methods can be used. For fast tests that do not require a lot of construction time and low memory storage, Picard method is recommended, whereas for faster long-term simulation, Newton method is better. An algorithmic version giving the choice of the method has been implemented using the Picard/Newton algorithms. It is described below. [SOURCE: Page_202]

## D.3. SYNTHESIS

Algorithm for relaxed Picard/Newton iterations [SOURCE: Page_203]

Initialization: u− = uPn+1 : initial guess r = A(u− )u− − b(u− ) γ ω relaxed value while ||r|| > ϵ do if γ = 1 then b̃ = r elseif γ = 0 then b̃ = b end if Ã = A(u− ) + γ (J(u− ) − A(u− )) solve Ãu⋆ = b̃ u = u⋆ ω + (1 − ω)u− u− ← u r = A(u− )u− − b(u− ) end while [SOURCE: Page_203]

Output: un+1 = u− [SOURCE: Page_203]

This appendix refers to the shortnote of 15 pages, accepted with minor correction, in the journal Rhizosphere. Note that the aims of this journal is to advance the frontier of our understanding of plant-soil interactions. [SOURCE: Page_205]

185 [SOURCE: Page_205]

APPENDIX E. CHEMICAL DIVERSITY OF CROP PLANTS ROOT MUCILAGES. IMPLICATIONS FOR THEIR MAXIMAL WATER CONTENT AND MINERALIZATION IN SOILS [SOURCE: Page_206]

Manuscript (without Author Details)                                                              Click here to view linked References [SOURCE: Page_206]

1     Chemical diversity of crop plants root mucilages. Implications for their 2     maximal water content and mineralization in soils. 3 [SOURCE: Page_206]

4     Abstract 5     The biophysical functioning of the rhizosphere can act on plant water use efficiency through root 6     mucilage release and their stimulation of microorganisms’ activity. Mucilages characteristics and roles 7     are still poorly studied for the diversity of agronomic plant species. We compared mucilages collected 8     from roots of germinated seeds (aerohydroponics technique) of eight plant species (four 9     Eudicotyledons, four Monocotyledon) in terms of total sugars content, MIR spectra, mucilage maximal 10     water content and induced respiration in contrasting soils. Our results suggest that the "chemical 11     fingerprint" of these mucilages could be discriminated according to the phylogenetic proximity of the 12     plant species. In addition, the maximal water content that mucilages retain seems to be linked to their 13     chemical composition and seems more related to the presence of high molecular weight sugars than 14     their total sugar amounts. If mucilage-induced respiration by soil microbiota appeared to be 15     independent from phylogeny, some mucilages induced more respiration than others regardless of the 16     soil studied. Microbial communities and soil physico-chemical properties interact in mineralization 17     with variations in mucilages’ chemical composition. [SOURCE: Page_206]

18     Keywords: root, mucilage, agronomic plant species, MIR spectra, EPS, water content, 19     mineralization [SOURCE: Page_206]

20 [SOURCE: Page_206]

21     Improvements in the efficiency of water use and resistance to water deficits are required for the 22     agroecological transition (Ahmed et al., 2018) and need new production systems as well as new 23     cultivars. To this aim, we need to understand the functional traits of the plant that could improve its 24     resistance to water deficit. 25     In relation with the root system (Vetterlein and Doussan, 2016), the biophysical functioning of the 26     rhizosphere (e.g variation in soil water retention, wettability, porosity, microorganisms activity, due to 27     root activity) is a characteristic that could modify plant water use efficiency. Peculiar biophysical 28     characteristics of rhizospheric soil (water retention and aggregation) are thought to be linked in part 29     to exopolymers (EPS) produced by plant roots via their mucilages and also by rhizospheric 30     microorganisms, themselves stimulated by rhizodeposits (including mucilages, Brax et al., 2019). Root 31     mucilages are mainly composed of exopolysaccharides (EPSac), with minor amount of proteins, 32     minerals and lipids (Nazari, 2021). Their fate in the soil in relation to their mineralization and according [SOURCE: Page_206]

33   to their plant origin has been little studied to date (Naveed et al., 2017) and no comparison between 34   different soils (with different microbial communities) has been made to our knowledge. In relation 35   with the diversity of agronomic plant species, their variability in characteristics and roles are however 36   poorly studied (Galloway et al., 2020). 37   Our aim was to characterize the root mucilages of various crop species, through their chemistry (sugar 38   content, MIR spectra), physical parameter (maximal water content) and mineralization potential in 39   contrasting soils (MicroRespTM bioassays). 40   We used an aero-hydroponic method adapted from Zickenrott et al. (2016), Holz et al. (2018) and Brax 41   et al. (2019) to harvest the mucilage of eight plant species of agronomic interest (four Eudicotyledons, 42   four Monocotyledons, Tab. 1). After sterilization the seeds (Isopropanol 70%, NaOCl 1% then tween 43   20 0.1%, followed by a wash with ultrapure water), the seeds were germinated on a tray (3x^3 mm 44   stainless steel mesh, adding a very thin layer of glass wool for very small seeds such as tomato, 45   rapeseed and sorghum), incubated in the dark at 25°C +/- 1°C, in an hermetic box above a solution of 46   CaSO$_4$ (2 mM) aerated continuously with two pumps. After 2-3 days of incubation, mucilage is 47   collected from the tip of each root daily using a baby fly connected to a suction pump over a further 48   1-2 days, until the roots reach the CaSO$_4$ solution. For each species, five growth box systems were set 49   up and run in parallel. From four growth boxes, the mucilage was collected and pooled to obtain 50   enough material for analysis and experimentation. Globally, the mucilage pools were obtained from 51   1100 to 29800 germinated seeds, depending on the species tested (Tab.1). The fifth growth box was 52   used to get the amount of mucilage (in total sugar equivalent) produced per dry mass of roots. In this 53   box, after the first mucilage collection, the roots were cut along the grid surface, dried and weighed. 54   The collected mucilage was pooled into tubes, weighed and first stored at -20°C. An aliquot was freeze- 55   dried to measure mucilage maximal water content (MWC, calculated as mucilage wet weight minus 56   dry weight divided by dry weight, Nazari et al., 2020) and for analyses with Medium Infrared (MIR) 57   spectroscopy. Total sugars were analyzed using the Dubois method (Dubois et al., 1956). MIR 58   measurements were performed with a Tensor 27 FTIR spectrometer (Bruker Optics, Wissembourg, 59   France). The samples were scanned between 4000 and 600 cm-1 and each spectrum was obtained by 60   averaging 16 successive scans. For each sample, four replications on different aliquots were made 61   (Bérard et al., 2020). MIR data were processed with ChemFlow chemometrics software 62   (GalaxyProject). Principal component analysis (PCA) of the MIR spectra of the eight species mucilage 63   showed that the first two axes explained 82 % of variance. 64   We also performed a MicroRespTM assay (Bérard et al., 2011; De Vries et al., 2019) of respiration induced 65   by the different mucilages in three contrasting soils selected for their distinct microbial biomass, 66   texture, organic C, and water holding capacity. These include a sandy clay loam from an irrigated [SOURCE: Page_207]

APPENDIX E. CHEMICAL DIVERSITY OF CROP PLANTS ROOT MUCILAGES. IMPLICATIONS FOR THEIR MAXIMAL WATER CONTENT AND MINERALIZATION IN SOILS [SOURCE: Page_208]

67    permanent grassland ("Crau"), a silty clay loam soil from a conventional agricultural field ("Lysi") and a 68    sandy soil from a pine forest ("Tavel") (Bérard et al., 2020, SuppMat^1). We introduced 25 µl of fresh 69    mucilage per well of the microplate system (containing 0.48 to 0.57 g in dry mass equivalent per well 70    depending on the soil type), with 4 replications per mucilage and per soil tested (the moisture content 71    of the test soils was 40% of their water holding capacity). Respiration measurements were recorded 72    after six hours' incubation at 23°C and respiration induced by each mucilage was normalized by the total 73    sugars amount introduced per soil sample. Non-parametric tests (Kruskal Wallis, Dunn Bonferroni test, 74    p<0.05) were used for data analysis. 75    From the results obtained, the variations of the eight species root mucilage in its chemical, physical and 76    degradation characteristics enabled to address the three points below: 77    - The chemical composition of plant root mucilages seems related to their phylogenetic proximity 78    The PCA of MIR measurements shows that the species are relatively well separated from each other 79    (Fig.1). The dendrogram of relative distances between points effectively separates Monocotyledons 80    (negative part of PC$_1$) from Eudicotyledons (positive part of PC$_1$), Asterids (Tomato) from Rosids, 81    Malvids (Rapeseed) from Fabids, and Roinoids (Lupins) from Genisteae (Alfalfa) (SuppMat^2). In the 82    Monocotyledons, Panicoideae having C$_4$ photosynthesis strategy (Maize and Sorghum) are at the 83    bottom left of the PCA, and Pooideae with C$_3$ photosynthesis (Wheat and Barley) at the top left. This 84    suggests that these plant mucilage chemical compositions are related to phylogenetic proximity, at 85    least up to the family scale, corroborating earlier studies by (Vančura and Hovadík, 1965) who 86    observed similar molecular compositions of root exudates whose plant species were closely related. 87    More recently, similar observations have been made on the phylogeny-chemical composition link of 88    root exudates from different plant families using GC-MS (Herz et al., 2018; Williams et al., 2022) or 89    using ESI FT-ICR MS (Miao et al., 2020). 90    According to MIR spectra acquired, dicots are located on the positive part of PC$_1$ (Fig.1a), 91    characterized by a high absorption in a broad band of wave numbers between 1217 and 1681 cm-1 92    (Fig.1b). This zone is largely represented by nitrogenous molecules of the amine, amide (I, II, III types), 93    proteins, as well as phosphates and fats (phospholipids, triglycerides, lipids, fatty acids). Benzene 94    (1328; 1489 cm-1), phenyl (1500 -1510 cm-1) and quinoid (1588 cm-1) rings are also in this band. Wave 95    numbers between 1419 and 1444 cm-1 indicate pectin- and cellulose-type polysaccharides (Talari et 96    al., 2017). 97    The negative part of PC$_1$ is represented by Monocots-Poaceae with MIR absorption bands at 932-1064 98    cm-1 and 1140 and 1198 cm-1. These zones are mainly represented by simple carbohydrates (sucrose, 99    mannose, galactose, fructose), polysaccharides (pectin, cellulose, glycogen) and, to a lesser extent, 100   phosphate, phosphodiester, amino acid (thyrosine) and aromatic molecules (Talari et al., 2017). 101   Setting aside the redundancies between spectra regions, these results suggest that PC$_1$ is structured 102   primarily by a gradient of fat abundance (lipids, fatty acids, triglyceride; wave numbers above 1300 103   cm-1). Nitrogenous molecules also drive PC$_1$, with an abundance of tertiary amides to its positive part 104   (wave numbers above 1400 cm-1). This joins the observations of (Miao et al., 2020): the degree of 105   unsaturation and the proportion of nitrogen in the molecules studied were the two main factors 106   discriminating species mucilage chemical composition according to their phylogenetic families. So 107   although mucilage from different phylogenetic families possess a "largely common composition" (Herz 108   et al., 2018), particularly for their carbohydrate base (Vančura and Hovadík, 1965), the abundance of 109   fat and nitrogenous molecules (amide III), albeit minor in mucilage composition (Nazari, 2021; Miao 110   et al,. 2020) seem the primary factors in their variability. 111   - The chemical composition of mucilages seems linked to their MWC 112   A positive correlation arises (R²=0.568, p=0.051) between amount of sugar (per g of mucilage dry 113   matter) and maximal water content of mucilage, only if maize is excluded. Nazari et al. (2020) also 114   found no correlation for maize cultivars. Furthermore, the PC$_2$ axis structured by a wavelength band 115   between 1100 and 1250 cm-1, corresponding to polysaccharide (pectin and cellulose) and sugar type 116   molecules (Talari et al., 2017), appears to rank mucilages according to the quantity of high molecular 117   weight sugar (at the top is the tomato whose exudates are rich in pectin, Jeong et al., 2018) and 118   therefore also to their ability to retain water (Kazanskii and Dubrovskii, 1992). This is underlined by 119   the positive correlation between the coordinates of the PC$_2$ axis and MWC values (R 2=0.623, 120   p<0.0001) (Fig.2). 121   - Mucilage-induced respiration depends on the type of soil and probably also on its plant origin. 122   Irrespective of the mucilage tested, we observed a ranking of respirations according to the 3 soils, with 123   mucilage-induced respirations systematically higher in Crau and Lysi soils than in Tavel soil (p<0.0001). 124   This shows an effect of microbial communities and their edaphic environment (with Crau>Lysi>Tavel 125   for MO content, Microbial Biomass, CEC, WHC and Basicity, Bérard et al., 2020 ; Riffaldi et al., 1996) 126   greater than the effect of the mucilage’s chemical composition on its induced respiration. However, 127   some mucilages are classified (in terms of induced respiration intensity) in the same way whatever the 128   soil (wheat, lupin, maize mucilages being the least degraded, while tomato, rapeseed mucilages the 129   most - Fig. 2). In particular, respiration induced by tomato mucilage was always much higher than that 130   induced by maize mucilage (p<0.002). The water content of tomato mucilage is higher than that of 131   maize mucilage (Tab.1), probably making the first more available to microorganisms. In addition, these 132   two mucilages are very far apart on the PCA of the MIR spectra (Fig.1), suggesting different 133   compositions. To a lesser extent than Naveed et al. (2017), we observe the same trend when 134   comparing maize and barley mucilages. This suggests that the chemistry and/or physics of the [SOURCE: Page_209]
