APPENDIX E. CHEMICAL DIVERSITY OF CROP PLANTS ROOT MUCILAGES. IMPLICATIONS FOR THEIR MAXIMAL WATER CONTENT AND MINERALIZATION IN SOILS [SOURCE: Page_210]

135   mucilage have a dominant role on its availability/degradation by soil microorganisms. It would be 136   interesting to develop chemical and physical analyses to characterize, for example, the size and 137   branching of the polymers, and their water retention and viscosity (parameters likely to influence the 138   degradability of these mucilages) in order to confirm these hypotheses. On the other hand, plant 139   phylogeny does not seem to be related to mucilage-induced respiration levels. Finally, in some cases, 140   mucilages-induced respiration differently according to soil type. For example, rapeseed mucilage- 141   induced respiration is higher than wheat or lupin mucilage in Crau soil (p<0.001), whereas this 142   difference fades in Lysi and Tavel soils (p>0.05). The soil, through its microbial community and its 143   physicochemical properties, can therefore interact with the degradation of the different mucilages. 144 145   The sugar amount of mucilage produced per seed is extremely variable (Tab.1) and not associated 146   with phylogeny but strongly correlated with seed weight (R²= 0.92, p=0.0001). This suggests that 147   species involve more or less the same fraction of seed’s C reserve in sugars of mucilage production. 148   As a corollary, this also results in a large range of amounts of mucilage-derived sugars delivered to soil 149   among species (according to seed weights), which may impact considerably in soil the shaping and 150   interactions in this “early stage rhizosphere” at a critical stage in plant establishment. Analyzes of C 151   stored in the seeds, in relation with mucilage-derived sugars could strengthen these findings. We also 152   observed that the sugars amount produced per root weight was highly variable between different 153   species (6-fold, Tab.1), which in real plant conditions in soils could impact rhizospheric soil water 154   retention. This exploratory study, based on mucilage harvested in aero-hydroponics from various 155   plant species, needs to be extended to later phenological stages in real field conditions, with a variety 156   of soils and water availability conditions, as done for maize (Nazari et al., 2023) and tomato (Le Gall et 157   al., 2021). 158 159   Acknowledgements: This work was made possible thanks to the support of our institute, INRAE, for a 160   post-doctoral grant and the funding of apprenticeship training for engineers, and to the funding of a 161   doctoral grant by INRAE and the University of Avignon. Special thanks to the SQPOV-INRAE unit (S. 162   Bureau and C. Le Bourvellec) for making their FTIR spectrometer available to us and for their advice 163   on the MIR spectral measurements and data. 164 165   References 166      Ahmed, M.L.A., Passioura, J., Carminati, A., 2018. Hydraulic processes in roots and the rhizosphere 167         pertinent to increasing yield of water-limited grain crops: a critical review. J. Exp. Bot. 69, 3255– 168         3265. https://doi.org/10.1093/jxb/ery^183. [SOURCE: Page_210]

238   Figures and Table captions [SOURCE: Page_213]

239   Figure^1: Results of the Principal Component Analysis (PCA) performed on MIR spectra (four spectra 240   per species) of mucilages harvested from the roots of aero-hydroponically germinated seeds of eight 241   plant species. a- PCA representing the eight species. b- PCA eigenvector of the PC$_1$ (black curve) and 242   PC$_2$ (red curve) axes of Fig.1a were built from the wavenumbers of the MIR spectra. [SOURCE: Page_213]

243   Figure.2: Correlation between the chemical characteristics of the different mucilages (harvested from 244   the roots of aero-hydroponically germinated seeds of eight plant species) (x-axis: coordinates of the 245   PC$_2$ axis of the PCA performed with the MIR spectra, see Fig.1) and a physical characteristic (y-axis: 246   mucilage maximal water content, see Tab.1) of these mucilages. [SOURCE: Page_213]

247   Figure.3: Respiration measurements induced by different mucilages (harvested from the roots of aero- 248   hydroponically germinated seeds of eight plant species) in three contrasting soils (MicroRespTM 249   technique). a- Respiration in Tavel soil. b- Respiration in Lysi soil. c- Respiration in Crau soil. Different 250   letters indicate significant differences (four replications, Kruskal-Wallis, Dunn Bonferroni test). Tom: 251   Tomato, Sor: Sorghum, Rap: Rape, Alf: Alfalfa, Bar: Barley, Lup: Lupin, Wht: Wheat, Mze: Maize. [SOURCE: Page_213]

252   Table.1: Phylogeny of the eight vegetal species studied, experiment characteristics, mucilages 253   characteristics harvested from the roots of aero-hydroponically germinated seeds of these eight plant 254   species. MWC= maximal water content (calculated as mucilage wet weight minus dry weight divided 255   by dry weight). [SOURCE: Page_213]

256 [SOURCE: Page_213]

257   SuppMat.1: Main physical, chemical and microbial properties of the three soils used for MicroRespTM 258   bioassay with the eight different mucilages tested. SOC: Soil Organic Carbon, TN : Total Nitrogen, CEC: 259   Cation Exchange Capacity, WHC: Water Holding Capacity, MB: Microbial Biomass, WSA : Water Stable 260   Aggregates [SOURCE: Page_213]

261   SupMat^2.a: Results of the Dendogram (Agglomerative Nesting Hierarchical Clustering) performed on 262   MIR spectra (four spectra per species) of mucilages harvested from the roots of aero-hydroponically 263   germinated seeds of eight vegetal species. SupMat^2.b: Years of phylogenetic phyla from compilations [SOURCE: Page_213]

Material and methods : Designing a rhizosphere soil [SOURCE: Page_219]

This chapter presents in poster form the steps involved in designing a rhizosphere model medium (Figure F.1-F.2). Ce Eau                                   Agitation                                  mucilage se dilue distillée                               durant 2h                                  dans l’eau dans (100 ml)                                                                           laquelle baignent les graines. Il est équipé d’un filtre placé au-dessus d’un                                                                    Filtre bac à récolte. Ce dispositif est relié à la pompe pour permettre Bac à récolte l’aspiration        du mucilage à travers le filtre [SOURCE: Page_221]

Appendix G [SOURCE: Page_225]

Experiments on the degradation of samples of chia mucilage mixed with sand [SOURCE: Page_225]

The aim of this study was to assess the extent to which the mucilage samples mixed with sand had un- dergone mucilage degradation over the time scale of the water retention measurements (approximately 6 weeks). [SOURCE: Page_225]

G.1       Materials and methods: We tested 3 concentration levels of mucilage (dry matter) mixed with Fontainebleau sand (200-250 µm grain size sieved and acid washed): 0% (control), 0.1% and 1%. We incubated samples of mucilage mixed with sand in metal rings placed on a suction table (100% saturation, 3 replications) for 44 days in the dark at laboratory temperature (25°C ±2). Measurements were taken at 4 key dates on the water equilibrium time scale: 0 days, 12 days (saturation on suction table), 29 days (middle of retention measurement experiment) and 44 days (end of retention measurement experiment). A total of 36 samples were studied. Two types of measurement were carried out. [SOURCE: Page_225]

G.1.1    Microbial degradation [SOURCE: Page_225]

To estimate microbial degradation of the mucilage we carried out micro-respiration measurements based on the MicroRespTM tool [56]. To summarize: aliquots of sand + mucilage (of the order of 0.4 g precisely weighed) were distributed in the deep wells of a plate (3 to 4 analytical replications). An initial reading of the optical density at 570 nm of the detection plate (containing a gel with a coloured indicator whose colour change after incubation correlates with the release of CO$_2$ from the sample) is taken. The MicroRespTM system was set up (the detection plate facing the deep-well plate containing the samples, the 2 separated by a silicone seal individualising the wells, the whole being placed in a press to hermetically seal the system, Figure G.1) and incubated in the dark at 23°C for 6 hours. The optical density of the detection plate was then measured a second time and the respiration rate of the sample was calculated to obtain µgC − CO$_2$ g−1 sand-mucilage h−1 [52]. This respiration is indicative of the degradation activity of the organic matter (in this case the mucilage introduced into the sand) [SOURCE: Page_225]

## APPENDIX G. EXPERIMENTS ON THE DEGRADATION OF SAMPLES OF CHIA MUCILAGE MIXED WITH SAND

by the microorganisms present in the sample, and is also indicative of the active microbial biomass of the sample [9]. [SOURCE: Page_226]

Figure G.1: Principle of the MicroRespTM colorimetric measurement to assess soil microbial respiration. In our case, we studied the respiration of sand samples mixed with chia mucilage. [SOURCE: Page_226]

G.1.2    Presence of ExoPolySaccharides (EPS) adhering to the sand To estimate the amount of EPS adhering to the sand and therefore likely to influence the water retention of the sample, we carried out total sugar measurements of the same samples after water extraction. According to Yang et al (2012), water extraction of the sample combined with centrifugation separates degraded EPS (supernatant after centrifugation) from non-degraded EPS (pellet after centrifugation). To do this, we introduced 0.03 g of sample and 20 µL of MilliQ water into an Eppendorf tube. After vortexing for 3 minutes, the sample was centrifuged (6869 g) for 3 minutes. The supernatant was removed and we analysed the total sugars in the pellet using the method proposed by Marasco et al [226]. The pellet was mixed with phenol and sulphuric acid and the coloration was measured by spectrophotometry (2 analytical replications, [113, 209]). Results are obtained in µg (glucose equivalent) L−1 . Statistical analyses were performed using XlStat software. The data did not meet the ANOVA criteria, non-parametric tests (Kruskal Wallis, Dunn Bonferroni test) were then used for data analysis. [SOURCE: Page_226]

G.2       Results and discussion Microbial respiration was significantly higher in the sand-mucilage samples, with a systematic gradient according to the concentration of mucilage introduced into the sand (p < 0.001, Figure G.2), which suggests a stimulation of the microbial communities present in the samples by the introduced chia mucilage. We observed a peak in respiration from the first sampling date at 12 days of incubation, followed by a drop in respiration at 29 days of incubation, which remained stable at 44 days of incubation (Figure G.2). [SOURCE: Page_226]

## G.2. RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

Figure G.2: Microbial respiration (CO$_2$ released) of sand + chia seed mucilage samples incubated on a suction table for 44 days. 0% = control sand without mucilage, 0.1% = sand with 0.1% mucilage, 1% = sand with 1% mucilage. Letters indicate significant differences between measurements (Kruskal Wallis, Dunn Bonferroni test, p < 0.05). [SOURCE: Page_227]

As the suction table is not sterile, nor is the chia seed mucilage used, despite washing the sand with acid before handling, we observed significant microbial activity in the sand (including without mucilage, p = 0.0004) from 12 days of incubation on the suction table. These results are consistent with the study by Yang et al. [362] (carried out, however, with sand collected in situ and not washed, but with a lower moisture content), which showed microbial development from the first day of incubation, as well as those of Ahmed et al [4, 1], who observed rapid degradation (a few days) of maize mucilage in a soil and microbial development rates linked to the initial quantities of mucilage added, as well as the study by van Veelen et al. [344], who observed the degradation of a chia mucilage in an artificial soil within 2 weeks. With regard to the quantity of EPS adhering to the soil (total sugars in glucose equivalent), we observed the same pattern as for microbial activity measurements: a difference in EPS quantities depending on the amount of mucilage introduced into the sand and a peak at 12 days (Figure G.3). Microbial degradation of the mucilage in the sand did not, therefore, reduce these measured quan- tities of EPS adhering to the sand, on the contrary. According to the work of Ahmed et al [4, 1], the quantity of mucilage integrated into the microbial biomass after 15 days of incubation in soil at optimum moisture (which is the case on the suction table, > 66% saturation) is of the order of 14% and can reach 40% for certain microbial groups. Our hypothesis is that the microbial biomass, which was probably low at the start of incubation (washed sand), was strongly stimulated by the addition of mucilage (Figure G.1), in an environment that was initially very low in organic matter, and that the microorganisms not only degraded this mucilage, but also incorporated it into their biomass and [SOURCE: Page_227]

## APPENDIX G. EXPERIMENTS ON THE DEGRADATION OF SAMPLES OF CHIA MUCILAGE MIXED WITH SAND

Figure G.3: Quantity of EPS adhering to the sand in samples of sand + chia seed mucilage incubated on a suction table for 44 days. 0% = control sand without mucilage, 0.1% = sand with 0.1% mucilage, 1% = sand with 1% mucilage. The EPS were measured using colorimetric analysis of total sugars, directly on the samples (after pre-extraction of the dissolved EPS with water). Letters indicate significant differences between measurements (Kruskal Wallis, Dunn Bonferroni test, p < 0.05). [SOURCE: Page_228]

their own EPS production. Our EPS measurements were carried out directly on the sand-mucilage samples (after extraction with water), but without extraction of the EPS adhering to the soil and not incorporated into the microbial biomass (additional step with an ion exchange resin, [53]). The EPS peak observed at 12 days probably corresponds to EPS produced and partially integrated into the biomass and microbial EPS. This hypothesis would need to be confirmed with microbial biomass measurements combined with isotopic labelling methods, in order to identify the origin of the EPS incorporated into microbial biomasses [4, 1]. In addition, this reality of the rhizospheric environment evolving over time (degra- dation of mucilages brought by roots into the soil, microbial growth and production of other EPS molecules by microorganisms stimulated by these mucilages) should be taken into account in comple- mentary stuadies to address the evolution of the physical and hydrophysical properties of the rhizo- sphere, likely to evolve as a result [244, 344]. [SOURCE: Page_228]

H.1      Dissipative free energy [SOURCE: Page_229]

We consider the system : water drop + porous media. [SOURCE: Page_229]

This system is isolated, if we neglect evaporation and impose zero mass and energy flux through the boundaries of the system. [SOURCE: Page_229]

Therefore it is possible to define a free energy F that decreases with time. [SOURCE: Page_229]

H.3.2       Equilibrium stability We consider U$_0$ the equilibrium satisfying the system of Eq.(5.35). Under the criteria of water height conservation Eq.(5.33), the couple (ζ0 ,ξ0 ) is an extremum or an inflection point of the free energy for flat film and uniform height of saturation. [SOURCE: Page_232]

H.3.2.1      Nature of critical point [SOURCE: Page_232]

To define the stability of equilibrium couple U$_0$ , we study the nature of critical point using hessian matrix H,                                    "               # ∂ζζ fw ∂ζξ fw H=                     .                                (H.16) ∂ζξ fw ∂ξξ fw [SOURCE: Page_232]

As ∂ξ a(ξ, c) < 0 and ∂ζ fwζr (ζ) > 0 then ∂ζξ fw < 0. The equilibrium couple satisfy the stability condition Eq.(H.15), i.e U$_0$ is a stable equilibrium couple. [SOURCE: Page_232]

H.3.2.1.2 Maximum The couple U$_0$ is a maximum, i.e the determinant of the hessian matrix is positive and its trace is negative, ∂ζζ fw ∂ξξ fw > (∂ζξ fw )2 , (H.18) ∂ζζ fw + ∂ξξ fw < 0. [SOURCE: Page_232]

## H.3. STABILITY ANALYSIS

The stability of a critical couple corresponding to a maximum is not as simple as in the case of a minimum. First of all, using Eq. (H.18) and the properties of the Hessian matrix H, we deduce that the derivatives ∂ζζ fw and ∂ξξ fw are negative at the equilibrium point U$_0$ , As ∂ζξ fw < 0 and using the positive determinant of H, we have [SOURCE: Page_233]

p                  |∂ζζ fw | + |∂ξξ fw | 0 > ∂ζξ fw > − ∂ζζ fw ∂ξξ fw > −                                                         (H.19) 2 Remark H.3.1. We can express the derivative of fw , ∂ζζ fw and ∂ξξ fw , as [SOURCE: Page_233]

∂ζζ fw = −|∂ζζ fw | ∂ξξ fw = −|∂ξξ fw | [SOURCE: Page_233]

Then the equilibrium couple don’t satisfy the stability condition of Eq.(H.15), i.e U$_0$ is an unstable equilibrium couple. [SOURCE: Page_233]

H.3.2.1.3 Inflection point The couple U$_0$ is an inflection point, i.e the determinant of the hessian matrix is negative, ∂ζζ fw ∂ξξ fw < (∂ζξ fw )2 ,                                (H.20) [SOURCE: Page_233]

In this situation, we can conclude that the couple is [SOURCE: Page_233]

• stable if the hessian trace is positive [SOURCE: Page_233]

• unstable if the hessian trace is negative and ∂ζζ fw ∂ξξ fw ≤ 0 [SOURCE: Page_233]

• Else we can not conclude the stability without studying in more detail functions. [SOURCE: Page_233]

H.3.2.2    Free energy density variation [SOURCE: Page_233]

To define the stability of equilibrium couple U$_0$ , we study the variation of the free energy density under the conservation criteria. Let us consider a total water in the system H and introduce the function ζ(ξ) = H − ξ that represent the height of water in the flat film ζ depending on the height of water in the porous media ξ. We define a function φ(ξ) such that it represent the free energy density under the conservation criteria [SOURCE: Page_233]

φ(ξ) = f (H − ξ, ξ). [SOURCE: Page_233]

The second derivative of φ is exactly the function required to study the stability of the varicose mode, [SOURCE: Page_233]

d^2 φ       d^2 h ζ             ξ         int i (ξ) =      fw (ζ(ξ)) + fw  (ξ) + fw    (ζ(ξ), ξ) dξ 2       dξ 2 d^2 fwh           d^2 fwξ         ∂ 2 fwint              ∂ 2 fwint             ∂ 2 fwint =        (ζ(ξ)) +         (ξ) − 2           (ζ(ξ),  ξ) +           (ζ(ξ), ξ) +           (ζ(ξ), ξ) dζ 2             dξ 2          ∂ζ∂ξ                     ∂ζ 2                  ∂ξ 2 ∂ 2 fw             ∂ 2 fw                  ∂ 2 fw =        (ζ(ξ), ξ) +        (ζ(ξ), ξ)   −  2        (ζ(ξ), ξ). ∂ζ 2                ∂ξ 2                  ∂ζ∂ξ [SOURCE: Page_233]

The stability condition of Eq.(H.15) can be rewrite as [SOURCE: Page_234]

d^2 φ (ξ) > 0.                                  (H.21) dξ 2 [SOURCE: Page_234]

Note : if the couple corresponds to an extremum of the free energy function, than this criteria correspond to an extremum in a convex area. In other word, the stability condition is verified only for minima of the free energy density function. [SOURCE: Page_234]

H.4       Extrema of free energy density For uniform saturation and flat film, we express the free energy density [SOURCE: Page_234]

f (ζ, ξ, c) = fwζ (ζ) + fwξ (ξ, c) + fwint (ζ, ξ, c) + Λ (H − ξ − ζ) ,     (H.22) [SOURCE: Page_234]

where Λ is the Lagrange multiplier. Thus, the derivative of f with respect to ζ and ξ are [SOURCE: Page_234]

δF$_1$                                         δF = ∂ζ fwζ (ζ) + ∂ζ fwint (ζ, ξ, c) − Λ =    −Λ                   (H.23) δζ                                         δζ [SOURCE: Page_234]

and δF$_1$                                            δF = ∂ξ fwξ (ξ, c) + ∂ξ fwint (ζ, ξ, c) − Λ =    −Λ                 (H.24) δξ                                            δξ where δF δζ and δξ are the functional free energy density without respect the mass conservative criteria. δF [SOURCE: Page_234]

We consider a steady-states U$_0$ = (ζ0 , ξ0 ) of the system (5.30) is define by Eq.(5.35). In other words, U$_0$ is an extrema of the free energy density Eq.(5.34),   δF$_1$       (U$_0$ ) = 0,    δζ   δF$_1$ (U$_0$ ) = 0,                                  (H.25)    δξ     δF$_1$ (U$_0$ ) = 0,  δΛ [SOURCE: Page_234]

We consider only film height depending on ξ such that ζ(ξ) = H − ξ, i.e the free energy density can express without the conservative condition as a function φ depending only of the height of water in the soil, φ(ξ) = f (ζ(ξ), ξ, c) = fwζ (ζ(ξ)) + fwξ (ξ, c) + fwint (ζ(ξ), ξ, c).    (H.26) [SOURCE: Page_234]

Then equilibria of the system correspond to extrema of φ. [SOURCE: Page_234]

Appendix I [SOURCE: Page_235]

Water transfer with single root [SOURCE: Page_235]

I.1     Finite Element Method [SOURCE: Page_235]

In this study, we assume a symmetry with respect to z axis. In other words, the angular part is invariant. Moreover, when we fix a depth z the studying system can be express in dimensional or dimensionless formulation as in the following section. [SOURCE: Page_235]

I.1.1   Dimensional formulation [SOURCE: Page_235]

We consider the water transfer in the soil close to a single root, i.e the water transfer is modelled using                                 1             δF ϕ∂t s = ∂r rK(s)∂r        + S, r             δs (I.1)   δF = −ρgHcap Πw (s, c) − Γ∆s,  δs where S is a sink term representing the water uptake of the root.
